Chapter 11: Ensemble Retriever

As retrieval systems become more advanced, engineers often discover that no single retriever performs best for every query.

For example:

Similarity Search may be excellent at semantic understanding.
BM25 may excel at exact keyword matching.
MMR may provide diverse results.
Multi Query Retriever may improve recall.
Self Query Retriever may leverage metadata effectively.

Each retriever has strengths and weaknesses.

The question becomes: Why choose one retriever when we can combine many?

This idea led to the development of the Ensemble Retriever. Ensemble Retrieval combines multiple retrieval strategies and merges their results using ranking, voting, scoring, and blending mechanisms to produce a stronger final retrieval result.

This concept is very similar to ensemble learning in machine learning, where multiple models are combined to achieve better performance than any individual model.


a. Definition

* An Ensemble Retriever is a retrieval strategy that combines the outputs of multiple retrievers and produces a unified ranked result set.
* Instead of relying on a single retrieval method. An Ensemble Retriever performs.

                                          User Query
                                              ↓
                                          Retriever 1
                                          Retriever 2
                                          Retriever 3
                                              ↓
                                          Aggregation
                                              ↓
                                           Ranking
                                              ↓
                                         Final Results

* The primary goal is to leverage the strengths of different retrieval methods while minimizing their weaknesses.

b. Why It Is Important

1. Combines the Strengths of Multiple Retrievers
* Every retrieval technique has its own strengths and weaknesses. Similarity Search is good at finding semantically related content, BM25 excels at exact keyword matching, MMR improves result diversity, and Self Query Retriever leverages metadata filters. Since no single retriever performs best in every situation, Ensemble Retrieval combines multiple retrieval approaches to take advantage of their strengths while minimizing their individual limitations.

2. Handles Diverse Enterprise Data More Effectively
* Enterprise knowledge bases contain a mix of structured data, unstructured documents, metadata, business terminology, and keyword-heavy content. A single retrieval strategy often struggles to work equally well across all these data types. Ensemble Retrieval allows different retrievers to specialize in different aspects of the data, ensuring more effective retrieval across a wide variety of enterprise information sources.

3. Improves Retrieval Accuracy and Robustness
* When multiple retrievers independently identify the same document, confidence in that document increases. If one retriever fails to retrieve a relevant document, another retriever may still find it. This creates a more robust retrieval system that is less vulnerable to the weaknesses of any individual retrieval method and significantly reduces retrieval failures.

4. Enhances Overall RAG Performance
* By combining results from multiple retrieval strategies, Ensemble Retrieval improves recall, precision, ranking quality, and knowledge coverage. More relevant documents reach the LLM while irrelevant documents are filtered out through consensus mechanisms. This leads to better context quality, improved answer generation, fewer missed answers, and stronger performance in enterprise RAG applications.

c. Architecture / Components

					It Will Created by the AI

d. Components

i) Component: Multiple Retriever Combination
* Multiple Retriever Combination is the process of using two or more retrievers together within a single retrieval pipeline. Instead of relying on a single 
  retrieval strategy, the system combines retrievers such as BM25, Vector Search, MMR, or Self Query Retriever to leverage their individual strengths.
* Every retriever has limitations and performs best in specific scenarios. Some are better at semantic understanding, while others are better at exact 
  keyword matching or metadata filtering. Combining multiple retrievers helps overcome individual weaknesses and creates a more comprehensive retrieval 
  system.
* The user query is sent to multiple retrievers simultaneously. Each retriever independently searches the knowledge base and returns its own set of relevant 
  documents. These results are then combined and processed to create a unified retrieval result.
* Multiple Retriever Combination improves recall, precision, retrieval coverage, and overall reliability. By incorporating multiple retrieval signals, the 
  system is more likely to discover relevant documents across diverse search scenarios.

ii) Component: Score Aggregation
* Score Aggregation is the process of combining relevance scores generated by multiple retrievers into a single overall score for each document.
* Different retrievers evaluate relevance differently and produce separate confidence scores. Without aggregation, the system would struggle to compare and 
  prioritize results from multiple retrieval sources.
* After retrieval, scores from different retrievers are collected and combined using techniques such as averaging, weighted scoring, or normalization. For 
  example, if a document receives scores from multiple retrievers, those scores are merged into one final relevance score. 
* Score Aggregation improves document ranking, increases confidence in retrieved results, helps identify the strongest candidates, and creates a more 
  accurate final result set for downstream processing.

iii) Component: Voting Mechanisms
* Voting Mechanisms are ranking strategies where multiple retrievers "vote" for documents they consider relevant. Documents receiving more votes are treated 
  as having higher importance.
* A document identified by several independent retrievers is generally more likely to be relevant than one found by only a single retriever. Voting 
  mechanisms use this agreement to improve retrieval reliability.
* Each retriever contributes votes for its retrieved documents. The system counts the votes across all retrievers, and documents with the highest vote 
  counts receive higher rankings in the final result list.
* Voting Mechanisms provide consensus-based retrieval, improve ranking stability, reduce the impact of individual retriever errors, and increase confidence 
  in the selected documents.

iv) Component: Rank Fusion
* Rank Fusion is the process of combining ranked result lists from multiple retrievers into a single unified ranking.
* Different retrievers may rank the same documents differently. A document ranked highly by one retriever may appear much lower in another list. Rank Fusion 
  helps reconcile these differences and produce a more balanced ranking.
* The system collects ranked lists from all retrievers and applies fusion algorithms such as Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF), Weighted Rank Fusion, or Borda 
  Count. These methods combine rankings to generate a final ordered list of documents.
* Rank Fusion produces more stable rankings, improves retrieval quality, reduces bias from individual retrievers, and increases the likelihood that the most 
  relevant documents appear near the top of the final results.

v) Component: Hybrid Retrieval
* Hybrid Retrieval is a retrieval approach that combines keyword-based search and semantic vector search within the same retrieval pipeline.
* Keyword search excels at finding exact matches for specific words and phrases, while semantic search excels at understanding meaning and intent. Relying 
  on only one approach can cause relevant information to be missed.
* The query is processed by both keyword retrieval systems such as BM25 and semantic vector retrieval systems. Results from both approaches are combined and 
  ranked before being returned to the user.
* Hybrid Retrieval improves recall, precision, semantic understanding, exact-match retrieval, and overall search effectiveness. It is one of the most 
  popular ensemble strategies used in enterprise RAG systems.

vi) Component: Retrieval Blending
* Retrieval Blending is the final stage of ensemble retrieval where results from multiple retrievers are merged into one consolidated document list.
* After multiple retrievers have generated their own results, the system must produce a single set of documents for the LLM. Retrieval Blending ensures that 
  these results are combined consistently and effectively.
* The blending process performs deduplication, score aggregation, rank fusion, and result selection. Duplicate documents are removed, rankings are refined, 
  and the best candidates are selected for the final retrieval output.
* Retrieval Blending creates a unified retrieval experience, improves ranking quality, reduces redundancy, ensures efficient document selection, and 
  provides the LLM with the highest-quality context available.

vii) Component: Ensemble Retrieval Pipeline
* The Ensemble Retrieval Pipeline is the complete workflow that combines multiple retrievers, scoring methods, ranking mechanisms, and blending techniques 
  into a single retrieval system.
* No individual retriever can handle every retrieval scenario effectively. Enterprise systems require a retrieval approach that can work across structured 
  data, unstructured documents, metadata, keywords, and semantic content.
* Multiple retrievers perform searches independently, scores are aggregated, votes are counted, rankings are fused, and final results are blended into a 
  unified ranked document list before being passed to the LLM.
* The Ensemble Retrieval Pipeline delivers higher retrieval accuracy, improved recall, stronger precision, better ranking quality, enhanced robustness, 
  broader knowledge coverage, and superior RAG performance across enterprise-scale applications.

e) Practical Code Example
* A multinational organization stores: HR Policies, Finance Documents, Technical Documentation, Compliance Records.
* Users search using: Natural Language, Keywords, Error Codes, Business Terminology. The company decides to combine: BM25 Retrieval + Vector Retrieval using 
  an Ensemble Retriever.

from langchain_openai import OpenAIEmbeddings
from langchain_qdrant import QdrantVectorStore

from langchain_core.documents import Document

from langchain_community.retrievers import BM25Retriever
from langchain.retrievers import EnsembleRetriever

# =====================================
# Enterprise Knowledge Base
# =====================================

documents = [

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Employees are entitled to
        180 days of maternity leave.
        """
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Travel reimbursement requests
        must be submitted within 30 days.
        """
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Employee benefits include
        maternity coverage and healthcare.
        """
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Expense claims require
        finance approval.
        """
    )
]

# =====================================
# BM25 Retriever
# Covers Keyword Search
# =====================================

bm25_retriever = BM25Retriever.from_documents(
    documents
)

bm25_retriever.k = 4

# =====================================
# Vector Retriever
# Covers Semantic Search
# =====================================

embeddings = OpenAIEmbeddings(
    model="text-embedding-3-small"
)

vector_store = QdrantVectorStore.from_documents(
    documents=documents,
    embedding=embeddings,
    location=":memory:",
    collection_name="ensemble_demo"
)

vector_retriever = vector_store.as_retriever(
    search_kwargs={"k": 4}
)

# =====================================
# Ensemble Retriever
# =====================================

ensemble_retriever = EnsembleRetriever(
    retrievers=[
        bm25_retriever,
        vector_retriever,
    ],
    weights=[0.4, 0.6]
)

# =====================================
# User Query
# =====================================

query = """
Explain maternity leave benefits.
"""

# =====================================
# Retrieval
# =====================================

results = ensemble_retriever.invoke(
    query
)

# =====================================
# Results
# =====================================

for i, doc in enumerate(results, start=1):

    print(f"\nDocument {i}")
    print("=" * 50)

    print(doc.page_content)


Interview Discussion Points
Q1. What is an Ensemble Retriever?
* An Ensemble Retriever combines multiple retrieval methods and merges their outputs to produce higher-quality retrieval results than any single retriever.

Q2. Why use Ensemble Retrieval?
* Different retrievers have different strengths. Ensemble Retrieval combines these strengths, improving recall, precision, robustness, and retrieval   
  quality.

Q3. What is Score Aggregation?
* Score Aggregation combines confidence scores produced by multiple retrievers to determine document relevance.

Q4. What are Voting Mechanisms?
* Voting Mechanisms allow multiple retrievers to vote for documents. Documents receiving more votes generally rank higher.

Q5. What is Rank Fusion?
* Rank Fusion merges ranking lists from different retrievers into a single ranking. Popular techniques include Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) and Weighted 
  Rank Fusion.

Q6. What is Hybrid Retrieval?
* Hybrid Retrieval combines keyword-based and semantic retrieval systems to maximize retrieval effectiveness.

Q7. What is Retrieval Blending?
* Retrieval Blending combines the outputs of multiple retrievers through ranking, deduplication, weighting, and score aggregation.

Q8. What are the advantages of Ensemble Retrieval?
* Advantages include: Higher Recall, Better Precision, Improved Ranking, Reduced Retrieval Failure, Better Context Quality, Stronger RAG Performance.

Q9. When should Ensemble Retrievers be used?
* They are ideal for enterprise environments where documents contain both structured and unstructured information and no single retriever consistently 
  performs best.

Q10. What is the difference between Hybrid Retrieval and Ensemble Retrieval?
* Hybrid Retrieval specifically combines keyword and semantic retrieval.
* Ensemble Retrieval is broader and can combine any number of retrievers, including BM25, Similarity Search, MMR, Multi Query, Self Query, Parent Document, 
  and Hybrid Retrievers themselves.

Chapter 12: Hybrid Retrieval

a. Definition:- What is Hybrid Retrieval?
* Hybrid Retrieval is a retrieval technique that combines semantic search and keyword-based search into a unified retrieval system.
* Instead of relying exclusively on embeddings or exact keywords, a hybrid system performs searches using both: Dense Retrieval and Sparse Retrieval. The 
  results are then merged, ranked, and returned as a final result set.
* The goal is simple: Semantic Understanding + Exact Matching = Better Retrieval.
* Hybrid Retrieval increases both: Recall, Precision while reducing retrieval failures.

b. Why It Is Important

1. Combines the Strengths of Semantic and Keyword Search
No single retrieval method can handle every search scenario effectively. Semantic search is excellent at understanding meaning, intent, and context, while keyword search excels at finding exact matches such as error codes, product IDs, ticket numbers, serial numbers, and version numbers. Hybrid Retrieval combines both approaches, allowing the system to benefit from semantic understanding and exact keyword matching simultaneously.

2. Overcomes the Limitations of Individual Retrieval Methods
Semantic search may struggle with technical identifiers that have little semantic meaning, while keyword search often fails when users use synonyms, paraphrases, or different business terminology. Hybrid Retrieval solves these weaknesses by ensuring that if one retrieval method misses relevant information, the other can still retrieve it. This creates a more balanced and reliable retrieval system.

3. Improves Retrieval Accuracy and Coverage
By running both semantic and keyword searches, Hybrid Retrieval explores more retrieval paths than either approach alone. Documents can be discovered through exact term matches, semantic similarity, or both. This improves retrieval recall, increases precision, reduces missed documents, and ensures broader coverage of enterprise knowledge bases.

4. Enhances Enterprise Search and RAG Performance
Enterprise repositories contain diverse content such as policies, contracts, reports, error logs, support tickets, and product documentation. Hybrid Retrieval can effectively handle all these content types because it supports both natural language understanding and exact term retrieval. As a result, more relevant context reaches the LLM, leading to better answer quality, improved user experience, and stronger overall RAG performance.

c. Architecture / Components

				It Will be created by the AI.

d. Core Components

i) Component: Semantic Search
* Semantic Search retrieves documents based on meaning and intent rather than exact keyword matches. It uses embeddings to understand the relationship 
  between concepts, allowing the system to find relevant information even when the query and document use different words.
* Users rarely know the exact wording used inside documents. They typically ask questions in natural language, while documents may use different 
  terminology. Semantic Search bridges this gap by focusing on meaning instead of exact text matching.
* Queries and documents are converted into vector embeddings. The retriever compares these vectors using similarity calculations and retrieves documents 
  whose meanings are closest to the user's query, even when there is little or no keyword overlap.
* Semantic Search understands intent, handles synonyms, supports conversational search, and works exceptionally well in RAG systems. However, it can 
  struggle with exact identifiers such as error codes, serial numbers, product IDs, and other values that have limited semantic meaning.

ii) Component: Keyword Search
* Keyword Search retrieves documents by matching the exact words present in the user's query with words stored in documents.
* Many enterprise searches depend on exact identifiers such as product IDs, contract numbers, policy IDs, ticket numbers, and error codes, where precise 
  term matching is critical.
* The search engine scans indexed documents for occurrences of the query terms and returns documents containing those exact keywords. Documents with 
  stronger keyword matches typically receive higher rankings.
* Keyword Search offers precise matching, fast retrieval, and excellent performance for identifiers and structured terms. However, it cannot understand 
  meaning, struggles with paraphrases and synonyms, and is highly dependent on exact wording.

iii) Component: BM25
* BM25 (Best Matching 25) is a keyword-based ranking algorithm that evaluates how relevant a document is to a search query using term frequency, inverse 
  document frequency, and document length normalization.
* Not all keywords carry equal importance. BM25 helps distinguish highly relevant documents from less relevant ones by assigning weighted relevance scores 
  based on keyword significance.
* BM25 calculates scores by analyzing how frequently query terms appear in a document, how rare those terms are across the collection, and the document's 
  length. Documents with stronger keyword relevance receive higher rankings.
* BM25 provides strong keyword matching, efficient retrieval, reliable ranking, and industry-standard performance. It is widely used in search engines, 
  enterprise search platforms, and hybrid RAG systems.

iv) Component: Dense Retrieval
* Dense Retrieval is a semantic retrieval approach where queries and documents are converted into dense neural embeddings and compared using vector 
  similarity.
* Traditional keyword retrieval cannot understand meaning, context, or user intent. Dense Retrieval enables systems to retrieve information based on 
  semantic similarity rather than exact word matches.
* Embedding models convert both queries and documents into high-dimensional dense vectors containing values in most dimensions. Vector similarity algorithms 
  then identify documents whose meanings closely match the query.
* Dense Retrieval provides strong semantic understanding, context awareness, and natural language search capabilities. However, it is computationally 
  expensive and may struggle with exact keyword matching and identifiers.

v) Component: Sparse Retrieval
* Sparse Retrieval is a retrieval technique that represents documents using sparse vectors, where only a small number of dimensions contain values while 
  most dimensions remain zero.
* Many enterprise searches require exact term matching for codes, identifiers, names, and specific business terminology. Sparse Retrieval preserves these 
  exact textual relationships.
* Documents and queries are represented using sparse vector structures based on keywords and term importance. Retrieval is performed by matching active 
  dimensions between query and document vectors.
* Sparse Retrieval delivers excellent precision, strong identifier matching, and fast search performance. However, it lacks semantic understanding and 
  depends heavily on the vocabulary used in both queries and documents.

vi) Component: Hybrid Ranking
* Hybrid Ranking is the process of combining relevance scores from dense retrieval and sparse retrieval systems to produce a final ranked list of documents.
* Semantic retrieval and keyword retrieval often rank documents differently. A document important to one retrieval method may not receive the same 
  importance from another. Hybrid Ranking balances these perspectives.
* The system collects scores from dense and sparse retrievers and combines them using weighted formulas or fusion techniques. The resulting score determines 
  the document's final position in the ranking.
* Hybrid Ranking improves ranking stability, retrieval accuracy, and overall search quality by utilizing the strengths of both semantic and keyword-based 
  retrieval approaches.

vii) Component: Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF)
* Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) is a ranking fusion algorithm that combines retrieval results based on document ranks rather than raw similarity scores.
* Different retrievers generate scores using different scales and scoring methods, making direct score comparison difficult. RRF solves this problem by 
  focusing only on document positions within ranked lists. 
* RRF assigns a score using the formula: RRF Score = Σ 1 / (k + rank)
  where k is typically 60 and rank is the position of a document in each retriever's result list. Documents consistently ranked highly across multiple 
  retrievers receive better final scores.
* RRF is simple, effective, model-independent, and highly robust. It combines rankings from dense and sparse retrieval systems efficiently, improves ranking 
  quality, and is one of the most widely used techniques in enterprise hybrid retrieval and RAG applications.

e. Practical Code Example
* A company maintains: HR Policies, Error Resolution Guides, Technical Manuals, Finance Documents.
* Users ask: What is the maternity leave policy?, ORA-12541 troubleshooting steps?, Travel reimbursement process?
* The retriever must support both: Semantic Understanding and Exact Keyword Matching.

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_openai import OpenAIEmbeddings
from langchain_qdrant import QdrantVectorStore

from langchain_community.retrievers import BM25Retriever
from langchain.retrievers import EnsembleRetriever

# ==========================================
# Enterprise Documents
# ==========================================

documents = [

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Employees are entitled to
        180 days of maternity leave.
        """
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Business travel reimbursement
        process for employees.
        """
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        ORA-12541 occurs when the listener
        is unavailable.
        """
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Employee benefits include
        maternity coverage and healthcare.
        """
    )
]

# ==========================================
# Sparse Retrieval (BM25)
# Keyword Search
# ==========================================

bm25_retriever = BM25Retriever.from_documents(
    documents
)

bm25_retriever.k = 5

# ==========================================
# Dense Retrieval (Semantic Search)
# ==========================================

embeddings = OpenAIEmbeddings(
    model="text-embedding-3-small"
)

vector_store = QdrantVectorStore.from_documents(
    documents=documents,
    embedding=embeddings,
    location=":memory:",
    collection_name="hybrid_demo"
)

dense_retriever = vector_store.as_retriever(
    search_kwargs={"k": 5}
)

# ==========================================
# Hybrid Retrieval
# Score Aggregation
# Hybrid Ranking
# Retrieval Blending
# ==========================================

hybrid_retriever = EnsembleRetriever(
    retrievers=[
        bm25_retriever,
        dense_retriever
    ],
    weights=[0.5, 0.5]
)

# ==========================================
# Query
# ==========================================

query = """
Explain maternity leave benefits.
"""

results = hybrid_retriever.invoke(query)

# ==========================================
# Results
# ==========================================

for i, doc in enumerate(results, start=1):

    print(f"\nDocument {i}")
    print("=" * 50)

    print(doc.page_content)


Interview Discussion Points

Q1. What is Hybrid Retrieval?
* Hybrid Retrieval combines semantic search and keyword search to improve retrieval quality.

Q2. Why is Hybrid Retrieval better than pure vector search?
* Vector search understands meaning but struggles with identifiers and exact matches. Hybrid Retrieval combines semantic understanding with exact keyword 
  matching.

Q3. What is BM25?
* BM25 is a keyword-ranking algorithm that scores documents using term frequency, inverse document frequency, and document length normalization.

Q4. What is Dense Retrieval?
* Dense Retrieval uses embeddings and vector similarity to find semantically similar documents.

Q5. What is Sparse Retrieval?
* Sparse Retrieval uses keyword-based representations where only a small number of dimensions are active.

Q6. What is Hybrid Ranking?
* Hybrid Ranking combines scores or rankings from dense and sparse retrieval systems.

Q7. What is Reciprocal Rank Fusion?
* RRF is a ranking fusion technique that combines rankings from multiple retrievers to create a stronger final ranking.

Q8. Why do enterprise RAG systems use Hybrid Retrieval?
* Because enterprise data contains both natural language content and exact identifiers such as error codes, policy IDs, and product numbers.

Q9. What are the benefits of Hybrid Retrieval?
* Better Recall, Better Precision, Improved Ranking, Reduced Retrieval Failure, Stronger Context Quality

Q10. When should Hybrid Retrieval be used?
* Whenever both semantic understanding and exact keyword matching are important for finding relevant information.

Chapter 13: Metadata Filtering

As enterprise Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems grow, organizations often discover that semantic search alone is not sufficient for high-quality retrieval.

A typical enterprise document contains not only content but also valuable metadata such as: Source, Department, Category, Region, Author, Tags, Creation Date, Document Type. 
Consider a query: Show HR policies related to maternity leave.

Even if semantic retrieval finds relevant documents, the results may include: HR Policies, Finance Policies, Legal Policies, Old Archived Policies.
because semantic similarity alone cannot fully understand business constraints.

Metadata Filtering solves this challenge by restricting the search space using document metadata before or during retrieval.

It is one of the most important techniques used in production RAG systems because it improves: Retrieval Precision, Response Accuracy, Security, Governance
Search Efficiency.

a. Definition
* Metadata Filtering is a retrieval technique that restricts search results based on document metadata attributes before returning documents to the user or 
  LLM.
* Instead of searching across every document in a vector database, metadata filters narrow the retrieval scope using structured business attributes.

b. Why It Is Important

i) Narrows Search to Business-Relevant Documents
Enterprise repositories contain thousands or even millions of documents across departments such as HR, Finance, Legal, Compliance, and Engineering. Many of these documents may discuss similar topics, making it difficult for semantic search alone to determine which documents are truly relevant. Metadata Filtering solves this by restricting retrieval to documents that satisfy specific business conditions, ensuring that only relevant documents are considered during search.

ii) Reduces Noise and Improves Retrieval Precision
Without metadata filtering, retrieval systems may return semantically similar but business-irrelevant documents. For example, a query about a leave policy could retrieve documents from HR, Legal, and Payroll departments. Metadata Filtering eliminates irrelevant results early in the retrieval process, reducing noise and improving the precision of retrieved documents.   

iii) Enables Enterprise Governance and Access Control
Organizations often enforce strict policies based on department, region, confidentiality level, document classification, or user roles. Metadata Filtering allows these governance rules to be applied directly during retrieval, ensuring users only access documents they are authorized to view. This makes Metadata Filtering a critical component for security, compliance, and enterprise AI governance.

iv) Improves Search Efficiency and RAG Performance
By searching within a smaller and more relevant document subset, retrieval becomes faster and more efficient. Higher-quality documents are passed to the LLM, resulting in better context quality, more accurate answers, improved user experience, reduced retrieval latency, and stronger overall performance of enterprise RAG systems.

c. Architecture / Components

		It Will Be created by the AI.

d. Components

i) Component: Metadata-Based Search
* Metadata-Based Search is a retrieval technique that uses document metadata along with document content to find relevant information. Instead of searching 
  across all documents, the retriever applies business attributes such as department, source, category, or date to narrow the search scope.
* Not every document that is semantically related to a query is actually relevant from a business perspective. Metadata provides additional business context 
  that helps limit retrieval to the most appropriate set of documents.
* The retriever evaluates both the user's query and metadata conditions. For example, when searching for "Maternity Leave" with Department = HR, the system 
  searches only within HR-related documents instead of the entire repository.
* Metadata-Based Search improves retrieval precision, increases business relevance, reduces unnecessary document scanning, and enables faster and more 
  targeted search results.

ii) Component: Source Filtering
* Source Filtering is a metadata filtering technique that restricts retrieval to specific document repositories or data sources such as SharePoint, 
  Confluence, HR Portals, Knowledge Bases, or Finance Repositories.
* Organizations often store information across multiple systems. Searching all repositories may return irrelevant results from unrelated sources. Source 
  Filtering ensures retrieval occurs only within the desired repository.
* The retriever applies a source-based filter before executing the search. For example, if Source = "HR Portal", only documents stored in the HR Portal 
  participate in retrieval.
* Source Filtering provides repository control, improves relevance, supports access management, reduces search space, and helps users retrieve information 
  from trusted sources.

iii) Component: Tag Filtering
* Tag Filtering is a retrieval technique that uses document tags or labels to restrict search results to documents associated with specific business topics.
* Enterprise documents are often categorized using tags such as Benefits, Payroll, Compliance, Training, or Healthcare. These tags help organize content and 
  make retrieval more focused.
* The retriever applies tag-based constraints before searching. For example, a query about "Health Benefits" may include a Benefits tag filter, ensuring 
  that only documents tagged with Benefits are searched.
* Tag Filtering enables business-driven retrieval, supports fine-grained search, improves content categorization, increases retrieval precision, and helps 
  users access topic-specific information more efficiently.

iv) Component: Date Filtering
* Date Filtering is a metadata filtering technique that restricts retrieval based on document dates such as creation date, publication date, or last 
  modified date.
* Enterprise information changes frequently, and older documents may contain outdated policies, procedures, or business rules. Users often need the most 
  recent and relevant information.
* The retriever applies date-based constraints during retrieval. Examples include searching for documents created after 2025, modified within the last 30 
  days, or published during a specific time period.
* Date Filtering improves content freshness, ensures users access up-to-date information, reduces retrieval of obsolete documents, and increases the 
  reliability of generated answers.

v) Component: Department Filtering
* Department Filtering is a retrieval mechanism that limits searches to documents associated with a specific business department such as HR, Finance, Legal, 
  Engineering, or Sales.
* Different departments often maintain documents with similar terminology but entirely different purposes. Searching across all departments can introduce 
  irrelevant results.
* The retriever applies a department-specific metadata filter before executing the search. For example, Department = HR ensures that retrieval occurs only 
  within HR-related content.
* Department Filtering improves business relevance, supports access control, aligns retrieval with organizational structures, and helps users locate 
  department-specific information more accurately.

vi) Component: Category Filtering
* Category Filtering is a metadata filtering technique that restricts retrieval to documents belonging to specific categories such as Policies, Contracts, 
  Compliance, Training, or Reports.
* Organizations store many different types of documents, and users often need information from a particular document category rather than every available 
  document type.
* The retriever applies category-based constraints during retrieval. For example, Category = Policy ensures that only policy documents are included in the 
  search process.
* Category Filtering improves document organization, creates more focused searches, increases retrieval precision, and helps users quickly access the type 
  of information they need.

vii) Component: Metadata Filtering
* Metadata Filtering is the process of restricting retrieval using business attributes such as source, tags, dates, departments, categories, regions, or 
  access-control information.
* Semantic similarity alone cannot always determine business relevance. Enterprise systems require additional constraints to ensure documents satisfy 
  organizational, security, compliance, and contextual requirements.
* The retriever combines the user's query with metadata conditions and searches only within the subset of documents that satisfy those filters. This 
  significantly reduces the search space and improves relevance.
* Metadata Filtering improves retrieval precision, reduces noise, enhances governance, supports security and compliance requirements, accelerates search 
  performance, and delivers higher-quality context to enterprise RAG systems.

e. Practical Code Example
A company maintains documents across multiple departments: HR, Finance, Legal, Compliance. Users should retrieve documents only from: Required department
Required category, Required source, Recent publication dates, Desired business tags. The organization implements Metadata Filtering.

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_openai import OpenAIEmbeddings
from langchain_qdrant import QdrantVectorStore

# =====================================
# Enterprise Documents
# =====================================

documents = [

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Employees receive 180 days
        of maternity leave.
        """,
        metadata={
            "source": "HR Portal",
            "department": "HR",
            "category": "Policy",
            "tag": "Benefits",
            "year": 2025
        }
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Travel reimbursement policy.
        """,
        metadata={
            "source": "Finance Portal",
            "department": "Finance",
            "category": "Policy",
            "tag": "Expenses",
            "year": 2024
        }
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Legal compliance guidelines.
        """,
        metadata={
            "source": "Compliance Portal",
            "department": "Legal",
            "category": "Compliance",
            "tag": "Regulation",
            "year": 2025
        }
    )
]

# =====================================
# Embeddings
# =====================================

embeddings = OpenAIEmbeddings(
    model="text-embedding-3-small"
)

# =====================================
# Vector Store
# =====================================

vectorstore = QdrantVectorStore.from_documents(
    documents=documents,
    embedding=embeddings,
    location=":memory:",
    collection_name="metadata_demo"
)

# =====================================
# Metadata Filtering
# Covers:
# Source Filtering
# Department Filtering
# Category Filtering
# Tag Filtering
# Date Filtering
# =====================================

results = vectorstore.similarity_search(
    query="maternity leave policy",

    k=5,

    filter={
        "must": [

            {
                "key": "department",
                "match": {
                    "value": "HR"
                }
            },

            {
                "key": "category",
                "match": {
                    "value": "Policy"
                }
            },

            {
                "key": "source",
                "match": {
                    "value": "HR Portal"
                }
            },

            {
                "key": "tag",
                "match": {
                    "value": "Benefits"
                }
            },

            {
                "key": "year",
                "range": {
                    "gte": 2025
                }
            }
        ]
    }
)

# =====================================
# Results
# =====================================

for i, doc in enumerate(results, start=1):

    print(f"\nDocument {i}")
    print("=" * 50)

    print(doc.page_content)

    print("\nMetadata:")
    print(doc.metadata)


Interview Discussion Points

Q1. What is Metadata Filtering?
Metadata Filtering is the process of restricting retrieval results using document metadata such as department, category, source, tags, and dates.

Q2. Why is Metadata Filtering important?
It improves retrieval precision, reduces irrelevant results, and supports enterprise governance.

Q3. What is Metadata-Based Search?
Metadata-Based Search combines semantic retrieval with structured metadata constraints.

Q4. What is Source Filtering?
Source Filtering restricts searches to specific repositories or content sources.

Q5. What is Tag Filtering?
Tag Filtering uses document tags to narrow retrieval results.

Q6. What is Date Filtering?
Date Filtering limits retrieval to documents created, modified, or published within a specified time range.

Q7. What is Department Filtering?
Department Filtering restricts retrieval to department-specific content.

Q8. What is Category Filtering?
Category Filtering limits retrieval to specific document classifications such as policies, reports, or compliance documents.

Q9. When should Metadata Filtering be used?
Whenever documents contain structured metadata and retrieval quality depends on business-specific constraints.

Q10. What are the major benefits of Metadata Filtering?
Better Precision, Faster Retrieval, Improved Governance, Better Search Relevance, Lower Retrieval Noise, Stronger RAG Performance.


Chapter 14: Retrieval Ranking

a. Definition
* Retrieval Ranking is the process of ordering retrieved documents according to their relevance to a user's query. The goal is to ensure that the most 
  useful and relevant documents appear at the top of the result list.
* The process usually occurs after initial retrieval.

                         User Query
                             ↓
                          Retriever
                             ↓
                      Top 50 Documents
                             ↓
                       Ranking Engine
                             ↓
                       Top 5 Documents
                             ↓
                            LLM

* A ranking system evaluates: Semantic relevance, Keyword relevance, Context relevance, Query-document relationship, User intent alignment and produces an   
  ordered ranking of documents. The highest-ranked documents have the greatest probability of helping the LLM generate a correct answer.


b. Why It Is Important

1. Filters Out Less Relevant Documents and Improves Context Quality

Most retrievers return a mixture of highly relevant, partially relevant, and weakly relevant documents. Although the information may be related to the query, not every retrieved document deserves to be included in the final context. Retrieval Ranking identifies and prioritizes the most valuable documents so that the LLM receives the highest-quality evidence first, resulting in more focused and relevant context.

2. Reduces Noise and Prevents Token Waste

LLMs have limited context windows, and sending large numbers of retrieved documents can waste tokens on low-value information. Irrelevant or weakly relevant documents may occupy space that could be used by stronger evidence. Retrieval Ranking solves this problem by pushing less useful documents lower in the result list and selecting only the top-ranked documents for generation.

3. Improves Retrieval Effectiveness and Information Accessibility

Even when retrieval successfully finds relevant information, the best document may not always appear at the top of the results. Retrieval Ranking reorganizes retrieved documents based on relevance, ensuring that the most useful information appears first. This improves recall at top positions and makes critical information easier for both the retrieval system and the LLM to utilize.

4. Enhances RAG Accuracy and User Experience

The quality of generated answers depends heavily on the quality of retrieved context. By supplying the LLM with stronger evidence, Retrieval Ranking improves grounding, answer accuracy, response reliability, and overall user satisfaction. In many enterprise RAG systems, improving ranking quality often delivers greater performance gains than simply upgrading to a larger language model.

c. Architecture / Components


					It Will done by the AI

d. Components

i) Component: Ranking Fundamentals

* Ranking Fundamentals are the core principles used to determine the order in which retrieved documents are presented. After retrieval identifies candidate 
  documents, ranking evaluates how relevant each document is to the user's query and assigns scores that determine their final position in the results list.
* Retrieval only finds potentially relevant documents, but it does not guarantee that the best documents appear first. Ranking ensures that the most useful 
  and relevant documents are prioritized, allowing the LLM to receive higher-quality context for answer generation.
* Ranking models analyze multiple relevance signals such as semantic similarity, keyword relevance, metadata relevance, query intent alignment, and 
  historical user feedback. These signals are combined to estimate how useful each document is for answering the query.
* Ranking Fundamentals improve result ordering, increase retrieval precision, reduce noisy context, enhance answer quality, and form the foundation for all 
  advanced ranking and re-ranking techniques used in enterprise RAG systems.

ii) Component: Re-ranking

* Re-ranking is a second-stage ranking process that reevaluates documents returned by an initial retriever and rearranges them based on a more accurate 
  relevance assessment.
* Initial retrieval systems often use approximate similarity search techniques, which may not perfectly rank documents. Highly relevant documents can 
  sometimes appear lower in the result list, reducing retrieval effectiveness.
* The retriever first returns a candidate set, such as the top 50 documents. A re-ranking model then performs deeper relevance analysis on those candidates 
  and produces a new ranking, selecting the most relevant documents for the final output.
* Re-ranking improves ranking quality, increases retrieval accuracy, provides more relevant context to the LLM, reduces hallucinations, and significantly 
  improves the quality of generated responses.

iii) Component: Cross Encoders

* Cross Encoders are transformer-based ranking models that evaluate a query and document together within the same neural network rather than encoding them 
  separately.
* Vector similarity methods compare independently generated embeddings, which may miss subtle relationships between query words and document content. Cross 
  Encoders analyze both simultaneously, allowing for much deeper relevance understanding.
* The query and document are fed into the model together. The transformer evaluates interactions between every query token and every document token and 
  produces a relevance score indicating how well the document answers the query.
* Cross Encoders provide extremely high ranking accuracy, superior semantic understanding, and excellent relevance estimation. However, they are 
  computationally expensive, slower than vector search, and best suited for re-ranking a small set of retrieved documents rather than an entire corpus.

iv) Component: ColBERT

* ColBERT (Contextualized Late Interaction over BERT) is an advanced retrieval and ranking architecture designed to combine the speed of dense retrieval 
  with the accuracy of interaction-based ranking.
* Traditional Bi-Encoders are fast but may lose detailed token-level information, while Cross Encoders are highly accurate but computationally expensive. 
  ColBERT was developed to achieve a balance between efficiency and ranking quality.
* Instead of representing an entire document with a single embedding, ColBERT creates embeddings for individual document tokens. During search, query tokens 
  interact with document token embeddings, enabling more detailed relevance matching without the full computational cost of a Cross Encoder.
* ColBERT provides stronger semantic matching, better retrieval accuracy, scalable ranking performance, and efficient large-scale search. It is widely used 
  in enterprise knowledge systems, legal search platforms, research retrieval, and large-scale RAG applications.

v) Component: Rank Fusion

* Rank Fusion is the process of combining rankings from multiple retrieval and ranking systems into one final unified ranking.
* Different ranking systems evaluate relevance differently. A document highly ranked by BM25 may not be highly ranked by a vector retriever or Cross 
  Encoder. Rank Fusion combines these perspectives into a more reliable final ranking.
* The system collects ranked lists from multiple rankers and applies fusion algorithms such as Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF), Weighted Rank Fusion, or Borda 
  Count. These methods merge rankings and generate a final ordered document list.
* Rank Fusion improves ranking stability, retrieval robustness, accuracy, and search quality by leveraging the strengths of multiple ranking techniques 
  simultaneously.

vi) Component: Search Quality Improvement

* Search Quality Improvement refers to the collection of techniques used to increase the relevance and usefulness of retrieval results returned to users.
* The effectiveness of a RAG system depends heavily on retrieval quality. Even powerful LLMs can produce poor answers if retrieval returns weak or 
  irrelevant documents. Improving search quality directly improves answer quality.
* Techniques such as Re-ranking, Cross Encoders, ColBERT, Rank Fusion, and Hybrid Retrieval are applied to improve document selection and ranking. The goal 
  is to ensure that the most relevant documents appear at the top of the results.
* Search Quality Improvement leads to better context, more accurate answers, stronger grounding, and higher user satisfaction. Its effectiveness is measured 
  using metrics such as:

	Precision → How relevant the retrieved documents are.
	Recall → How many relevant documents were successfully retrieved.
	MRR (Mean Reciprocal Rank) → Measures how highly the first relevant document is ranked.
	NDCG (Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain) → Evaluates ranking quality by considering both relevance and result ordering.

These metrics help organizations continuously optimize retrieval performance and maximize the effectiveness of enterprise RAG systems.

e. Practical Code Example

A global company maintains: HR Policies, Legal Documents, Technical Manuals, Compliance Reports. The company wants: High Recall Retrieval + Accurate Re-ranking + Better Search Quality. The solution combines: Vector Search, Cross Encoder Re-ranking, Rank Fusion Logic.

from langchain_openai import OpenAIEmbeddings
from langchain_qdrant import QdrantVectorStore

from langchain_core.documents import Document

from sentence_transformers import CrossEncoder

# =====================================
# Enterprise Documents
# =====================================

documents = [

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Employees are entitled to
        180 days of maternity leave.
        """
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Employee benefits overview.
        """
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Maternity leave eligibility requires
        one year of employment.
        """
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="""
        Travel reimbursement policy.
        """
    )
]

# =====================================
# Dense Retrieval
# =====================================

embeddings = OpenAIEmbeddings(
    model="text-embedding-3-small"
)

vector_store = QdrantVectorStore.from_documents(
    documents=documents,
    embedding=embeddings,
    location=":memory:",
    collection_name="ranking_demo"
)

retriever = vector_store.as_retriever(
    search_kwargs={"k": 10}
)

# =====================================
# Initial Retrieval
# =====================================

query = "Explain maternity leave policy"

candidate_docs = retriever.invoke(query)

# =====================================
# Cross Encoder Re-ranking
# =====================================

cross_encoder = CrossEncoder(
    "cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2"
)

pairs = [
    (query, doc.page_content)
    for doc in candidate_docs
]

scores = cross_encoder.predict(pairs)

ranked_docs = sorted(
    zip(candidate_docs, scores),
    key=lambda x: x[1],
    reverse=True
)

# =====================================
# Top Re-ranked Results
# =====================================

for rank, (doc, score) in enumerate(
    ranked_docs,
    start=1
):

    print(f"\nRank {rank}")
    print(f"Score: {score}")

    print(doc.page_content)

Interview Discussion Points

Q1. What is Retrieval Ranking?
--> Retrieval Ranking is the process of ordering retrieved documents according to relevance before passing them to an LLM.

Q2. What is Re-ranking?
--> Re-ranking is a second-stage ranking process that reorders retrieved documents using a more advanced ranking model.

Q3. What is a Cross Encoder?
--> A Cross Encoder evaluates a query and document together inside a transformer and produces a relevance score.

Q4. Why are Cross Encoders more accurate than embeddings?
--> Cross Encoders evaluate token-level interactions between query and document, resulting in more accurate relevance estimation.

Q5. What is ColBERT?
--> ColBERT is a late-interaction retrieval model that balances retrieval speed and ranking accuracy.

Q6. What is Rank Fusion?
--> Rank Fusion combines rankings from multiple ranking systems into a final ranking.

Q7. What is Reciprocal Rank Fusion?
--> RRF combines ranking lists by considering document positions instead of raw scores.

Q8. Why is Re-ranking important in RAG?
--> Because initial retrieval may not place the most relevant documents at the top. Re-ranking improves context quality before generation.

Q9. What metrics evaluate ranking quality?
--> Common metrics include: Precision, Recall, MRR, NDCG.

Q10. What are the benefits of Retrieval Ranking?
--> Better Precision, Better Recall, Higher Search Quality, Better Context Quality, Reduced Hallucinations, Improved RAG Performance, Stronger Enterprise 
    Search Systems.

Chapter 15: Retrieval Evaluation

a. Defintion
* Retrieval Evaluation is the process of measuring how effectively a retrieval system identifies, ranks, and returns relevant documents for a given user 
  query.
* Retrieval evaluation uses multiple metrics because no single metric can completely describe retrieval quality.
* Common metrics include: Precision, Recall, F1 Score, Hit Rate, MRR, NDCG, Context Relevance, Retrieval Accuracy. Together these metrics provide a complete 
  picture of retrieval performance.

b. Why Retrieval Evaluation Is Important

i) Identifies Retrieval Failures Before They Impact Answers
Even if an LLM is highly capable, it cannot generate accurate answers when the retriever fails to provide the correct documents. For example, if a legal assistant retrieves contract summaries but misses the actual termination clause document, the final answer will likely be incorrect. Retrieval Evaluation helps detect such failures by measuring whether the right documents are being retrieved.

ii) Provides an Objective Way to Measure Retrieval Quality
Without evaluation, teams cannot determine whether retrieval performance is improving or deteriorating. Retrieval Evaluation introduces measurable metrics that allow organizations to objectively assess retrieval quality rather than relying on assumptions or guesswork.

iii) Helps Compare and Optimize Retrieval Components
Retrieval Evaluation enables teams to compare different retrievers, chunking strategies, embedding models, and ranking techniques. By measuring performance quantitatively, organizations can identify which approach produces the best retrieval results and continuously improve their RAG systems.

iv) Directly Improves Overall RAG Accuracy
The quality of generated answers depends heavily on the quality of retrieved context. Better retrieval leads to better grounding, fewer missing facts, stronger answer accuracy, and more reliable AI responses. Retrieval Evaluation ensures retrieval continuously improves, which ultimately improves the performance of the entire RAG pipeline.

v) Reduces Hallucinations and Improves Answer Quality
Poor retrieval often provides incomplete or irrelevant context to the LLM, increasing the likelihood of hallucinations. Strong Retrieval Evaluation helps identify weak retrieval behavior, ensuring that more relevant information reaches the model and improving overall answer quality.

vi) Enhances User Experience and Trust
When users consistently receive accurate and relevant answers, their confidence in the AI system increases. Conversely, poor retrieval can lead to incorrect responses and frustration. Continuous retrieval evaluation helps maintain a reliable and trustworthy user experience.

vi) Lowers Operational and Support Costs
Incorrect answers frequently result in additional support requests, manual interventions, and increased workload for employees. By improving retrieval quality, organizations reduce the need for human assistance and lower the operational costs associated with supporting AI systems.

vii) Supports Enterprise Success and AI Adoption
Effective Retrieval Evaluation improves knowledge discovery, reduces compliance risks, increases system reliability, and encourages wider AI adoption across the organization. As a result, mature enterprise GenAI systems continuously monitor and optimize retrieval performance to maximize business value.

c. Architecture/Components

		It will be created by AI

d. Components

i) Component 1: Precision
* Precision measures how many of the retrieved documents are actually relevant to the user's query. It focuses on the quality of retrieval results and 
  determines whether the retriever is returning useful information or unnecessary documents.
* Retrieving a large number of documents does not necessarily mean retrieval is effective. A retriever may return many documents, but if most are 
  irrelevant, the LLM receives noisy context. Precision exists to measure the cleanliness and relevance of retrieval results.
* Precision is calculated by dividing the number of relevant retrieved documents by the total number of retrieved documents. For example, if 4 documents are 
  retrieved and only 2 are relevant, the precision is 50%.
* Precision helps identify noisy retrievers, improves context quality, and reduces irrelevant information reaching the LLM. However, a retriever can achieve 
  high precision while still missing many relevant documents, so precision must be combined with recall for a complete evaluation.

   Precision = 
                     Relevant Retrieved Documents
                    --------------------------------
                      Total Retrieved Documents


ii) Component 2: Recall
* Recall measures how many of the total relevant documents available in the knowledge base were successfully retrieved by the system. It focuses on 
  retrieval completeness rather than retrieval purity.
* A retriever may return only one relevant document and achieve excellent precision while missing many other important documents. Recall exists to measure 
  whether the retrieval system is discovering all relevant information required to answer a query completely.
* Recall is calculated by dividing the number of relevant retrieved documents by the total number of relevant documents in the knowledge base. If four 
  relevant documents exist and only two are retrieved, recall is 50%.
* Recall helps detect missing information, measures retrieval completeness, and is especially critical in legal, compliance, healthcare, and financial 
  systems where missing a document can lead to incorrect conclusions. However, maximizing recall may introduce large amounts of irrelevant content.


   Recall =
				Relevant Retrieved Documents
			       --------------------------------
				  Total Relevant Documents

iii) Component 3: F1 Score
* F1 Score is a single evaluation metric that combines Precision and Recall into one balanced measurement. It helps evaluate retrieval systems that need 
  both high-quality and comprehensive retrieval.
* Precision and Recall often conflict with each other. A retriever optimized for precision may miss important documents, while a retriever optimized for 
  recall may return excessive noise. F1 Score exists to balance these competing objectives.
* F1 Score uses the harmonic mean of Precision and Recall. Instead of favoring one metric over the other, it rewards systems that perform well on both. A 
  retriever receives a high F1 Score only when both precision and recall are reasonably strong.
* F1 Score provides a balanced evaluation metric, simplifies retriever comparisons, and is widely used as a benchmark during RAG optimization. However, it 
  evaluates retrieval quality only and does not consider document ranking positions.

 F1 Score =
					  2 × (Precision × Recall)
					----------------------------
					     Precision + Recall

iv) Component 4: Hit Rate
* Hit Rate measures whether a retriever was able to retrieve at least one relevant document for a query. It answers a simple question: did retrieval succeed 
  or fail?
* Many applications require only one correct document to generate a useful response. Hit Rate provides a straightforward way to evaluate whether retrieval 
  can consistently find at least one useful source of information.
* For each query, the system checks if at least one relevant document was retrieved. If yes, it counts as a success. Hit Rate is the percentage of 
  successful retrievals across all evaluated queries.
* Hit Rate is simple, easy to understand, and widely used in chatbots, enterprise assistants, customer support systems, and search engines. It provides a 
  quick indication of overall retrieval effectiveness.

				Hit Rate =
						Successful Queries
					      ----------------------
					          Total Queries

v) Component 5: MRR (Mean Reciprocal Rank)
* Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR) measures how quickly the first relevant document appears in the ranked retrieval results. It evaluates whether important 
  documents are positioned near the top of the results list.
* Users rarely examine all retrieved documents. They typically focus on the first few results. If relevant documents appear lower in the ranking, users and 
  LLMs may never utilize them. MRR measures ranking effectiveness from this perspective.
* MRR uses the reciprocal of the rank position of the first relevant document. A relevant document at Rank 1 receives a score of 1.0, Rank 2 receives 0.5,
  0.2. Higher scores indicate better ranking performance.
* MRR rewards retrieval systems that surface important documents early, improves user experience, and directly impacts RAG answer quality because highly 
  ranked documents are more likely to be included in the final context provided to the LLM.

Formula For Single queries:
 
				Reciprocal Rank = 1 / Rank

Formula For multiple queries:

						MRR =
							Σ (1 / Rank of First Relevant Document)
							-------------------------------------------
								Number of Queries

vi) Component 6: NDCG
* NDCG (Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain) is an advanced ranking metric that evaluates both the position of documents and their degree of relevance 
  within the ranked result list.
* Not all relevant documents are equally valuable. Some may contain the exact answer, while others only provide supporting information. NDCG exists to 
  reward retrieval systems that place the most useful documents at the highest positions.
* NDCG assigns greater value to highly relevant documents appearing near the top of the ranking. Documents placed lower in the ranking contribute less to 
  the final score. This allows NDCG to evaluate ranking quality more comprehensively than simple relevance metrics.
* NDCG is one of the most widely used metrics for ranking evaluation, accurately reflects user experience, and is particularly valuable for enterprise 
  search systems, retrieval ranking pipelines, and advanced RAG architectures.

			Formula: 
					DCG (Discounted Cumulative Gain) = Σ ((2^Relevance - 1) / log2(Position + 1))
                                       
                                        NDCG = DCG / IDCG, where IDCG is Ideal DCG

vii) Component 7: Context Relevance
* Context Relevance measures whether the retrieved documents actually contain the information required to answer the user's question. It evaluates the 
  usefulness of retrieved context rather than just its topical similarity.
* A document may be related to the query topic but still fail to contain the specific answer the user needs. Traditional retrieval metrics may consider such 
  documents relevant, even though they provide little value for answer generation.
* The evaluation examines whether the retrieved content contains sufficient evidence to answer the query accurately. Documents are assessed based on answer 
  usefulness rather than simple semantic similarity.
* Context Relevance improves grounding quality, increases answer accuracy, reduces hallucinations, and is often one of the most important evaluation metrics 
  for RAG systems because it directly measures the usefulness of retrieved context.

						Context Relevance =
									Relevant Context Chunks
								     ------------------------------
									 Total Retrieved Chunks

viii) Component 8: Retrieval Accuracy
* Retrieval Accuracy measures how often the retriever returns the correct documents for user queries. It evaluates the overall correctness of the retrieval 
  process.
* Organizations need a simple way to determine whether their retrieval system is consistently returning the expected documents. Retrieval Accuracy provides 
  a high-level measure of retrieval reliability.
* The metric compares retrieved documents against ground-truth documents and calculates the percentage of retrievals that are correct. For example, if 90 
  out of 100 retrievals return the correct documents, the retrieval accuracy is 90%.
* Retrieval Accuracy is easy to understand, useful for benchmarking retrievers, helps track performance improvements over time, and provides a 
  straightforward indicator of retrieval effectiveness in enterprise RAG and Agentic RAG systems.


 				Retrieval Accuracy =
							Correct Retrievals
						     -----------------------
							 Total Retrievals


e) Practical Code Example
* A company has built a RAG system for HR policies. Management wants answers to: How good is retrieval?, How much relevant information is retrieved?
  Are relevant documents ranked properly?, Can retrieved context answer employee questions?

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from sklearn.metrics import (
    precision_score,
    recall_score,
    f1_score,
    ndcg_score
)
import numpy as np

# ======================================
# Enterprise Knowledge Base
# ======================================

documents = [

    Document(
        page_content="Employees receive 180 days maternity leave.",
        metadata={"doc_id": "DOC_1"}
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="Travel reimbursement policy.",
        metadata={"doc_id": "DOC_2"}
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="Maternity leave requires one year employment.",
        metadata={"doc_id": "DOC_3"}
    ),

    Document(
        page_content="Health insurance policy.",
        metadata={"doc_id": "DOC_4"}
    )
]

# ======================================
# Query
# ======================================

query = "What is the maternity leave policy?"

# ======================================
# Ground Truth
# Documents that SHOULD be retrieved
# ======================================

ground_truth = [
    "DOC_1",
    "DOC_3"
]

# ======================================
# Simulated Retrieval Output
# ======================================

retrieved_docs = [
    "DOC_1",
    "DOC_2",
    "DOC_3"
]

# ======================================
# Binary Relevance Labels
# ======================================

all_docs = ["DOC_1", "DOC_2", "DOC_3", "DOC_4"]

y_true = []
y_pred = []

for doc in all_docs:

    y_true.append(
        1 if doc in ground_truth else 0
    )

    y_pred.append(
        1 if doc in retrieved_docs else 0
    )

# ======================================
# 1. Precision
# ======================================

precision = precision_score(
    y_true,
    y_pred
)

# ======================================
# 2. Recall
# ======================================

recall = recall_score(
    y_true,
    y_pred
)

# ======================================
# 3. F1 Score
# ======================================

f1 = f1_score(
    y_true,
    y_pred
)

# ======================================
# 4. Hit Rate
# ======================================

hit_rate = int(
    len(
        set(retrieved_docs)
        .intersection(
            set(ground_truth)
        )
    ) > 0
)

# ======================================
# 5. MRR
# ======================================

mrr = 0

for rank, doc in enumerate(
    retrieved_docs,
    start=1
):

    if doc in ground_truth:

        mrr = 1 / rank
        break

# ======================================
# 6. NDCG
# ======================================

relevance_scores = []

for doc in retrieved_docs:

    if doc in ground_truth:
        relevance_scores.append(1)
    else:
        relevance_scores.append(0)

ideal_scores = sorted(
    relevance_scores,
    reverse=True
)

ndcg = ndcg_score(
    [ideal_scores],
    [relevance_scores]
)

# ======================================
# 7. Context Relevance
# ======================================

relevant_count = len(
    set(retrieved_docs)
    .intersection(
        set(ground_truth)
    )
)

context_relevance = (
    relevant_count /
    len(retrieved_docs)
)

# ======================================
# 8. Retrieval Accuracy
# ======================================

retrieval_accuracy = (
    relevant_count /
    len(ground_truth)
)

# ======================================
# Evaluation Report
# ======================================

print("\nRETRIEVAL EVALUATION REPORT")
print("="*50)

print(
    f"Precision: {precision:.2f}"
)

print(
    f"Recall: {recall:.2f}"
)

print(
    f"F1 Score: {f1:.2f}"
)

print(
    f"Hit Rate: {hit_rate:.2f}"
)

print(
    f"MRR: {mrr:.2f}"
)

print(
    f"NDCG: {ndcg:.2f}"
)

print(
    f"Context Relevance: "
    f"{context_relevance:.2f}"
)

print(
    f"Retrieval Accuracy: "
    f"{retrieval_accuracy:.2f}"
)




RAG Syllabus


Chapter 1: RAG Fundamentals


a) Definition: What is RAG?

* RAG stands for Retrieval-Augmented Generation. It is a GenAI architecture in which relevant information is first retrieved from an external knowledge source and then supplied to a Large Language Model before the model generates its response. Instead of answering only from information stored in its parameters, the LLM receives additional context at query time. This enables it to answer questions using private, recent, or domain-specific information that may not have been available during its original training.

* RAG combines two important capabilities: information retrieval and natural-language generation. The retrieval system searches documents, databases, websites, APIs, or other knowledge sources and selects the content most relevant to the user’s question. This retrieved content is added to the prompt as supporting context, after which the LLM interprets the question and evidence to generate a clear, context-aware response.

* The term RAG can be understood through its three stages. Retrieval means finding relevant information from an external source; augmentation means adding that information to the original prompt; and generation means using an LLM to produce the final response from the augmented prompt. These stages work together, but each stage performs a different responsibility and can be evaluated separately.

* RAG combines the LLM’s parametric knowledge with external non-parametric knowledge. Parametric knowledge refers to patterns and information encoded inside the model’s weights during training, while non-parametric knowledge refers to explicitly stored information in documents, vector databases, relational databases, or knowledge graphs. The original RAG research described this combination using a pretrained sequence-to-sequence model as parametric memory and a dense vector index as non-parametric memory. 	


b) Purpose

* The primary purpose of RAG is to give an LLM access to information that is not reliably available in its internal knowledge. A general-purpose model may understand common concepts, but it cannot automatically know an organization’s private policies, project documentation, customer information, technical procedures, or proprietary research. RAG connects the model to authorized external sources so it can answer questions using information specific to the organization or application.

* RAG helps address the problem of static or outdated model knowledge. An LLM’s internal knowledge is based on the data available during its training, and the model does not automatically update when a company modifies a policy or when new information becomes available. In a RAG system, the organization can update its documents and search index independently, making the latest indexed information available without retraining the entire LLM.

* RAG is also intended to improve grounding and reduce unsupported responses. When a model answers without sufficient information, it may generate a fluent but incorrect response, commonly called a hallucination. By supplying relevant evidence and instructing the model to answer from that evidence, RAG can reduce hallucination and improve factual reliability, although it cannot eliminate errors completely.

* Another purpose of RAG is to improve traceability and verification. Retrieved chunks can include metadata such as the document name, page number, section title, URL, author, and creation date. The application can display this information as citations with the generated answer, allowing the user to verify whether the response is supported by an authoritative source.

c) Architecture Diagram


                    RAG ARCHITECTURE
                    ================

                A. INDEXING PIPELINE

 External Knowledge Sources
 PDFs | Word Files | Websites | Databases | SharePoint
                         |
                         v
              Document Loading and Parsing
                         |
                         v
                Cleaning and Chunking
                         |
                         v
                   Embedding Model
                         |
                         v
            Vector Database / Search Index
          [Embeddings + Text + Metadata]


                  B. QUERY PIPELINE

                    User Question
                         |
                         v
              Query Processing/Embedding
                         |
                         v
        Search Vector Database or Knowledge Base
                         |
                         v
             Retrieve Relevant Information
                         |
                         v
              Filter and Rerank Results
                         |
                         v
                   Prompt Builder
              +----------+----------+
              |                     |
         User Question       Retrieved Context
              |                     |
              +----------+----------+
                         |
                         v
                  Augmented Prompt
                         |
                         v
                  Large Language Model
                         |
                         v
              Grounded Answer + Citations

* The indexing pipeline prepares external information before users begin asking questions. Documents are collected from different sources, loaded, parsed, cleaned, and divided into smaller sections called chunks. An embedding model converts these chunks into numerical vectors, which are stored in a search index together with the original text and useful metadata.

* When the user submits a question, the query pipeline processes that question and searches the indexed knowledge. The query may be corrected, rewritten, expanded, or converted into an embedding before retrieval. The retriever then searches for chunks that are semantically or lexically related to the question and returns a limited collection of results, commonly called the Top-K results.

* The initially retrieved chunks can be passed through filtering and reranking. Filtering removes unauthorized, outdated, duplicate, or irrelevant information, while reranking places the most useful chunks at the top. This stage is important because semantic similarity does not always mean that a document actually contains the answer.

* Finally, the prompt builder combines the user’s question, retrieved context, and system instructions into an augmented prompt. The LLM reads this prompt and generates the answer, after which the application may attach citations, validate the output, mask sensitive information, or return a fallback response if sufficient evidence was not found.

d) Core Components

1. Knowledge source and document-processing components
* The knowledge source contains the external information that the RAG application can use, such as PDFs, Word documents, websites, databases, SharePoint content, product manuals, or internal policies. Document loaders and parsers read these sources, extract usable content, clean unnecessary information, and divide larger documents into smaller chunks. Metadata such as the file name, page number, author, department, document date, and access permissions is also attached so that the system can support filtering and citations.

2. Embedding model and vector database 
* The embedding model converts document chunks into numerical vectors that represent their semantic meaning. Texts that discuss similar concepts should generally have embeddings located close together, even when they use different words.
