A vector database stores these embeddings along with the original text and metadata, allowing the application to search large document collections efficiently using the semantic meaning of the user’s question.

3. Retriever and reranker
* The retriever accepts the user’s query and identifies a limited number of relevant document chunks from the knowledge source. It may use vector similarity, keyword matching, metadata filtering, hybrid search, graph traversal, or a combination of multiple strategies. An optional reranker then examines the retrieved candidates more carefully and reorders them so that the passages most likely to answer the question receive higher priority.

4. Prompt builder and Large Language Model
* The prompt builder combines the user’s question, retrieved chunks, system instructions, conversation history, and output rules into a structured prompt. It can instruct the model to answer only from the context, include citations, and admit when the answer is unavailable. The LLM then interprets the supplied evidence and generates a readable response by summarizing, explaining, or combining the relevant information.

e) Code Example:

from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
import numpy as np


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 1. Create a small external knowledge base
# ---------------------------------------------------------

documents = [
    {
        "id": "DOC-001",
        "source": "travel_policy.txt",
        "text": (
            "Employees travelling for approved business purposes "
            "can claim hotel accommodation for a maximum of seven "
            "consecutive days. Any extension requires written "
            "approval from the project manager."
        ),
    },
    {
        "id": "DOC-002",
        "source": "leave_policy.txt",
        "text": (
            "Permanent employees receive twelve paid sick-leave "
            "days during each calendar year. Unused sick leave "
            "cannot be carried forward."
        ),
    },
    {
        "id": "DOC-003",
        "source": "learning_policy.txt",
        "text": (
            "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees per financial "
            "year for approved technical certifications and "
            "professional training."
        ),
    },
]


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 2. Generate embeddings for the documents
# ---------------------------------------------------------

embedding_model = SentenceTransformer("all-MiniLM-L6-v2")

document_texts = [document["text"] for document in documents]

document_embeddings = embedding_model.encode(
    document_texts,
    normalize_embeddings=True
)


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 3. Accept and embed the user's question
# ---------------------------------------------------------

user_query = (
    "For how many days can an employee claim hotel expenses "
    "during a business trip?"
)

query_embedding = embedding_model.encode(
    [user_query],
    normalize_embeddings=True
)[0]


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 4. Retrieve the most relevant document
# ---------------------------------------------------------

similarity_scores = np.dot(
    document_embeddings,
    query_embedding
)

best_document_index = int(np.argmax(similarity_scores))
retrieved_document = documents[best_document_index]
retrieval_score = float(similarity_scores[best_document_index])


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 5. Construct the augmented prompt
# ---------------------------------------------------------

augmented_prompt = f"""
You are an enterprise policy assistant.

Instructions:
1. Answer the question using only the supplied context.
2. Do not add information that is not present in the context.
3. If the context does not contain the answer, say:
   "The required information was not found."
4. Mention the source document in the answer.

Context:
{retrieved_document["text"]}

Question:
{user_query}

Source:
{retrieved_document["source"]}
"""


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 6. Display the retrieval result and final prompt
# ---------------------------------------------------------

print("Retrieved source:", retrieved_document["source"])
print("Similarity score:", round(retrieval_score, 4))
print("\nAugmented prompt:")
print(augmented_prompt)

# The augmented_prompt can now be passed to an LLM.


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is RAG, and how does it work?
--> Retrieval-Augmented Generation is an architecture that combines external information retrieval with LLM-based response generation. When a user submits a question, the system searches an external knowledge source and retrieves the passages most relevant to that question. Those passages are added to the prompt as context, and the augmented prompt is passed to an LLM.

The LLM uses the retrieved evidence together with its general language capabilities to generate the final response. This approach enables the model to work with current, private, or domain-specific information without storing all knowledge permanently inside its parameters. RAG is commonly used in enterprise knowledge assistants, policy chatbots, document question-answering applications, and technical-support systems.

Q2. What is the difference between parametric and non-parametric knowledge in RAG?
--> Parametric knowledge refers to information and language patterns learned by the LLM during training and encoded inside its model parameters. This knowledge enables the model to understand language and answer general questions, but individual facts are difficult to update or trace back to an exact training source.

Non-parametric knowledge is stored outside the LLM in an explicit and searchable form, such as documents, databases, vector indexes, or knowledge graphs. RAG retrieves this external knowledge at query time and places it in the model’s prompt. It therefore combines the language and reasoning capabilities of parametric memory with the updateability and traceability of non-parametric memory

Q3. Does RAG completely eliminate LLM hallucinations?
--> RAG does not eliminate hallucinations completely, but it can reduce them by providing the model with relevant evidence. Hallucinations can still occur if the retriever selects an incorrect document, the knowledge source contains outdated information, the correct answer is split across poorly formed chunks, or the LLM misunderstands the provided context.

A production RAG system should therefore use good-quality data, suitable chunking, hybrid retrieval, reranking, grounding instructions, citations, confidence thresholds, and fallback responses. Retrieval quality and generation quality should also be evaluated separately because the final answer can fail at either stage.

Q4. Is RAG a model, framework, or architecture?
--> RAG is primarily an architectural pattern, not a particular model or framework. It describes how retrieval and generation components work together to answer questions using external knowledge. A RAG system can be built using different document loaders, embedding models, vector databases, retrieval strategies, reranking models, orchestration frameworks, and LLMs.

Frameworks such as LangChain can simplify the construction of a RAG pipeline, but they are not required to implement RAG. Similarly, a vector database is an important storage and retrieval component, but the vector database alone is not the complete RAG system. A complete implementation includes data preparation, indexing, query processing, retrieval, prompt augmentation, generation, security, and evaluation.


Chapter 2: RAG Architecture

a) Definition
* RAG architecture refers to the collection of interconnected components that ingest organizational data, retrieve relevant information for a user’s query, construct an augmented prompt, and generate a context-grounded response. A complete RAG application is not simply an LLM connected to a vector database. It is an end-to-end system containing data ingestion, document processing, embedding generation, knowledge storage, query processing, retrieval, prompt construction, response generation, and output validation components.

* RAG architecture is commonly separated into two major pipelines: the indexing pipeline and the query pipeline. The indexing pipeline prepares external knowledge by loading, cleaning, chunking, embedding, and storing documents. The query pipeline begins when a user asks a question and includes query processing, retrieval, reranking, prompt construction, LLM generation, citation creation, and answer validation.

* Every component has a specific responsibility and produces an output required by the next component. For example, a document loader produces standardized documents, a text splitter produces smaller chunks, an embedding model produces vectors, a vector store makes those vectors searchable, and a retriever returns relevant chunks. The prompt builder combines those chunks with the question, and the generator produces the final answer. LangChain similarly groups the major RAG layers into input processing, embedding and storage, retrieval, generation, and orchestration.

* The components of a RAG system should be designed as modular and replaceable units. An organization should be able to replace an embedding model, vector database, retriever, reranker, prompt template, or LLM without rebuilding the entire application. This modular design makes the system easier to test, maintain, scale, and improve because each component can be evaluated independently.

b) Purpose
* The main purpose of dividing RAG into separate components is to establish a clear separation of responsibilities. Document loaders focus on collecting information, text splitters focus on creating retrievable units, embedding models focus on semantic representation, retrievers focus on finding evidence, and generators focus on producing readable answers. This separation makes it easier to identify whether an incorrect answer was caused by poor source data, chunking, retrieval, prompt construction, or LLM generation.

* A component-based architecture allows the system to process a large knowledge collection without passing every document to the LLM. Documents are prepared and indexed in advance, and only the most relevant chunks are retrieved for each question. This reduces unnecessary token consumption, controls the size of the prompt, improves response latency, and helps the LLM focus on supporting evidence instead of unrelated information.

* Modular components also support independent optimization and evaluation. Developers can compare chunking strategies without changing the generator, test multiple embedding models against the same knowledge base, add a reranker without replacing the vector database, or change the LLM without rebuilding the entire retrieval pipeline. This makes experimentation more controlled because the effect of each architectural change can be measured separately.

* Enterprise RAG applications require additional capabilities beyond basic retrieval and generation. Components for authorization, metadata filtering, citation generation, prompt-injection protection, monitoring, caching, logging, evaluation, and fallback handling are needed to make the architecture reliable and secure. These components help ensure that retrieved information is permitted, trustworthy, traceable, and suitable for use in the final answer.

c) Architecture Diagram
                 COMPONENTS OF A RAG ARCHITECTURE
                 ================================

                     INDEXING PIPELINE
                     -----------------

  External Sources
  PDFs | Websites | Databases | SharePoint | APIs
                         |
                         v
              Document Loader and Parser
                         |
                         v
                Cleaning and Chunking
                         |
                         v
            Metadata Extraction and Enrichment
                         |
                         v
                  Embedding Model
                         |
                         v
              Vector Store / Search Index
              [Vector + Text + Metadata]


                       QUERY PIPELINE
                       --------------

                    User Question
                         |
                         v
             Query Processing or Rewriting
                         |
                         v
              Query Embedding Generation
                         |
                         v
                      Retriever
                         |
                         v
             Top-K Candidate Document Chunks
                         |
                         v
               Filtering and Reranking
                         |
                         v
                    Prompt Builder
       +-----------------+-----------------+
       |                 |                 |
 System Instructions  User Query    Retrieved Context
       |                 |                 |
       +-----------------+-----------------+
                         |
                         v
                    Generator / LLM
                         |
                         v
             Validation and Post-processing
                         |
                         v
               Answer with Source Citations

d) Core Components

1. Data ingestion and knowledge-preparation components

* The ingestion layer contains data connectors, document loaders, parsers, text cleaners, chunking mechanisms, and metadata processors. Connectors obtain information from sources such as file systems, cloud storage, SharePoint, databases, websites, and APIs. Parsers transform source-specific content into a standardized text representation, while chunking divides the extracted information into smaller units that can be independently indexed and retrieved.

* Metadata processing enriches every chunk with information about its origin and business context. Typical metadata includes the document ID, file name, section heading, page number, creation date, document category, department, language, confidentiality classification, and access-control information. This metadata supports filtered retrieval, citation generation, result freshness, document traceability, and permission enforcement.

2. Embedding and knowledge-storage components

* The embedding model converts each text chunk into a numerical vector that represents its semantic characteristics. Content with similar meanings should generally produce vectors that are close together, even if it does not contain exactly the same keywords. The same or a compatible embedding model is also used to represent the user’s query so the system can compare the meaning of the query with the indexed document chunks.

* The knowledge-storage layer stores the document chunks, embeddings, and associated metadata. A vector database supports semantic similarity search, while a broader search platform may additionally provide keyword search, hybrid retrieval, metadata filtering, and access-control integration. The knowledge base may also include structured databases, APIs, document stores, and knowledge graphs rather than depending entirely on vector storage.

3. Retrieval and context-selection components

* The retriever searches the knowledge base and returns a set of candidate chunks relevant to the user’s question. It may use dense vector search, sparse keyword retrieval, hybrid search, SQL queries, graph traversal, metadata filtering, or multiple retrievers working together. Its output normally contains the retrieved text, relevance score, source metadata, and other information needed for prompt construction and citations.

* The context-selection layer improves the results before generation. A reranker can apply a more accurate relevance model to reorder the retrieved candidates, while filters remove duplicates, low-scoring passages, expired content, and unauthorized documents. Context compression may shorten the retrieved chunks so that only information directly relevant to the query is included in the LLM’s context window.

4. Prompt construction and generation components

* The prompt builder creates the final input sent to the LLM. It brings together system instructions, the original or rewritten query, retrieved evidence, conversation history, citation metadata, and formatting requirements. A carefully designed prompt should clearly distinguish trusted instructions from retrieved content and tell the model what to do when the context does not contain sufficient evidence.

* The generator is the LLM responsible for converting the augmented prompt into a natural-language answer. It can summarize evidence, combine information from several chunks, explain technical material, and format the answer according to the application’s requirements. The generated response may then pass through citation generation, grounding validation, sensitive-data filtering, safety checks, and output formatting before being returned to the user.


e) Code Example
The following Python example creates a small component-based RAG pipeline. It uses separate functions for embedding, retrieval, prompt construction, generation, and output validation so that the responsibility of each component remains clear.


from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
import numpy as np


# =========================================================
# COMPONENT 1: KNOWLEDGE BASE
# =========================================================

knowledge_base = [
    {
        "id": "POLICY-001",
        "source": "travel_policy.txt",
        "department": "Finance",
        "text": (
            "Employees travelling for approved business purposes "
            "can claim hotel accommodation for a maximum of seven "
            "consecutive days. Extensions require written approval "
            "from the project manager."
        ),
    },
    {
        "id": "POLICY-002",
        "source": "leave_policy.txt",
        "department": "Human Resources",
        "text": (
            "Permanent employees receive twelve paid sick-leave "
            "days during each calendar year. Unused sick leave "
            "cannot be carried forward."
        ),
    },
    {
        "id": "POLICY-003",
        "source": "certification_policy.txt",
        "department": "Learning and Development",
        "text": (
            "Employees can claim reimbursement of up to 25,000 "
            "rupees per financial year for approved professional "
            "certifications."
        ),
    },
]


# =========================================================
# COMPONENT 2: EMBEDDING MODEL
# =========================================================

embedding_model = SentenceTransformer("all-MiniLM-L6-v2")


def create_embeddings(documents):
    """Convert knowledge-base documents into normalized vectors."""

    texts = [document["text"] for document in documents]

    return embedding_model.encode(
        texts,
        normalize_embeddings=True
    )


document_embeddings = create_embeddings(knowledge_base)


# =========================================================
# COMPONENT 3: RETRIEVER
# =========================================================

def retrieve_documents(query, top_k=2):
    """Return the Top-K documents most relevant to the query."""

    query_embedding = embedding_model.encode(
        [query],
        normalize_embeddings=True
    )[0]

    similarity_scores = np.dot(
        document_embeddings,
        query_embedding
    )

    ranked_indices = np.argsort(similarity_scores)[::-1][:top_k]

    results = []

    for index in ranked_indices:
        document = knowledge_base[int(index)].copy()
        document["score"] = float(similarity_scores[index])
        results.append(document)

    return results


# =========================================================
# COMPONENT 4: PROMPT BUILDER
# =========================================================

def build_prompt(query, retrieved_documents):
    """Combine instructions, context, and query into one prompt."""

    context_blocks = []

    for document in retrieved_documents:
        context_blocks.append(
            f"Source: {document['source']}\n"
            f"Content: {document['text']}"
        )

    formatted_context = "\n\n".join(context_blocks)

    return f"""
You are an enterprise policy assistant.

Instructions:
1. Answer using only the supplied context.
2. Do not make unsupported assumptions.
3. If the context does not contain the answer, state that the
   required information was not found.
4. Mention the source document used for the answer.

Retrieved context:
{formatted_context}

User question:
{query}

Answer:
""".strip()


# =========================================================
# COMPONENT 5: GENERATOR
# =========================================================

def generate_answer(prompt):
    """
    Placeholder for an actual LLM call.

    In production, this function would send the prompt to an
    LLM provider and return the generated response.
    """

    print("\nPrompt sent to the generator:\n")
    print(prompt)

    return (
        "Employees can claim hotel accommodation for a maximum "
        "of seven consecutive days. An extension requires written "
        "approval from the project manager.\n\n"
        "Source: travel_policy.txt"
    )


# =========================================================
# COMPONENT 6: OUTPUT VALIDATOR
# =========================================================

def validate_answer(answer, retrieved_documents):
    """Perform simple checks before returning the answer."""

    available_sources = {
        document["source"]
        for document in retrieved_documents
    }

    source_is_present = any(
        source in answer
        for source in available_sources
    )

    return {
        "answer": answer,
        "citation_present": source_is_present,
        "retrieved_sources": list(available_sources),
    }


# =========================================================
# END-TO-END EXECUTION
# =========================================================

user_query = (
    "How many days of hotel accommodation can an employee "
    "claim for an approved business trip?"
)

retrieved_documents = retrieve_documents(
    query=user_query,
    top_k=2
)

augmented_prompt = build_prompt(
    query=user_query,
    retrieved_documents=retrieved_documents
)

generated_answer = generate_answer(augmented_prompt)

final_result = validate_answer(
    answer=generated_answer,
    retrieved_documents=retrieved_documents
)

print("\nFinal answer:\n")
print(final_result["answer"])

print("\nCitation present:", final_result["citation_present"])
print("Retrieved sources:", final_result["retrieved_sources"])


Q1. What are the core components of a RAG architecture?

A RAG architecture contains components for data ingestion, knowledge preparation, embedding generation, knowledge storage, retrieval, context selection, prompt construction, response generation, and output validation. The ingestion layer reads external sources and converts them into standardized documents. The processing layer cleans and chunks those documents, while the embedding layer transforms the chunks into searchable vector representations.

When a user submits a query, the retriever searches the knowledge base for relevant chunks. A reranker or filter may refine the results, after which the prompt builder combines the selected context with the user’s query and system instructions. The generator produces the answer, and post-processing components add citations, validate grounding, apply security rules, and format the final response. LangChain’s component architecture similarly separates input processing, embedding and storage, retrieval, generation, and orchestration.

Q2. Why should RAG components be modular?

The components should be modular because different parts of the RAG pipeline have different responsibilities and failure modes. If the generated answer is incorrect, developers need to determine whether the problem occurred during document parsing, chunking, embedding, retrieval, reranking, prompt construction, or generation. A modular design makes each component independently testable and observable.

Modularity also allows components to be replaced without redesigning the entire application. For example, an organization can test a new embedding model while keeping the same vector database, add a reranker without changing the generator, or replace the LLM while retaining the existing indexing pipeline. This improves maintainability and allows controlled performance optimization.

Q3. Which RAG component has the greatest impact on answer quality?

No single component determines the complete quality of a RAG answer because the components operate as a connected pipeline. However, the retriever is particularly important because the generator cannot use information that was not retrieved. If the retriever returns irrelevant or incomplete evidence, even a powerful LLM may produce an inaccurate answer.

The final quality also depends on source-document quality, chunking strategy, embedding suitability, metadata accuracy, reranking, prompt design, and the generator’s ability to follow instructions. Therefore, a production system should evaluate retrieval quality and generation quality separately rather than measuring only whether the final answer appears fluent.

Q4. What is the difference between a retriever, prompt builder, and generator?

The retriever searches the knowledge base and returns evidence relevant to the user’s question. It does not normally write the final answer. Its responsibility is to identify suitable chunks using techniques such as semantic search, keyword search, hybrid retrieval, metadata filtering, or graph traversal.

The prompt builder organizes the retrieved evidence, user’s question, system instructions, conversation history, and response requirements into a structured augmented prompt. The generator is the LLM that reads this prompt and creates the final natural-language answer. Keeping these components separate enables developers to improve search, prompt design, and generation independently.


Chapter 3: Naive RAG

a) Defintion

* Naive RAG is the simplest implementation of Retrieval-Augmented Generation, following a direct Retrieve, Augment, and Generate process. When a user submits a question, the system converts it into an embedding, retrieves the Top-K semantically similar chunks from a vector database, inserts those chunks into a prompt, and sends the prompt to an LLM for response generation. It is called “naive” because it uses a straightforward, mostly fixed pipeline without advanced capabilities such as query rewriting, hybrid retrieval, reranking, context compression, iterative retrieval, or self-evaluation.

* A Naive RAG system normally contains two separate stages: offline indexing and online retrieval-generation. During offline indexing, documents are loaded, divided into chunks, converted into embeddings, and stored in a vector database. During online execution, the same embedding model converts the user’s question into a vector, after which similarity search identifies the closest document chunks and forwards them to the generator.

* The architecture is commonly described as a Retrieve-Read pipeline because the system performs a single retrieval operation and then allows the LLM to read the returned context. It does not normally determine whether retrieval is required, reformulate an ambiguous query, perform multiple searches, or verify whether the retrieved chunks truly answer the question. The RAG survey categorizes Naive RAG as the foundational form from which Advanced and Modular RAG evolved.

* Naive RAG should be understood as a baseline architecture, not necessarily a low-quality or unusable architecture. It can work effectively for small, clean, and well-structured knowledge bases where questions are direct and the required answer exists inside a single retrievable chunk. However, its fixed retrieval and prompt-construction strategy becomes less reliable for ambiguous, multi-hop, time-sensitive, or complex enterprise queries.

b) Purpose

* The primary purpose of Naive RAG is to give an LLM access to external information using the simplest possible retrieval pipeline. Instead of changing the LLM’s parameters through additional training, the architecture retrieves the required information at inference time and supplies it as context. This enables the model to answer questions about private documents, organization-specific information, and knowledge that was not available during its training.

* Naive RAG is commonly used as the starting point or baseline when developing a RAG application. It allows developers to verify document extraction, chunking, embedding generation, vector search, prompt construction, and LLM integration before adding advanced optimizations. Once the baseline is evaluated, its specific weaknesses can be identified and addressed using techniques such as query transformation, metadata filtering, hybrid search, reranking, or context compression.

* The architecture also limits the amount of information sent to the LLM. Instead of inserting the entire knowledge base into the model’s context window, Naive RAG retrieves only a limited number of chunks, known as Top-K results. This reduces prompt size and token consumption while helping the LLM focus on content that is semantically related to the question.

* Naive RAG is most appropriate when the knowledge base is relatively small, source documents are clean, user questions are direct, and semantic similarity is sufficient for locating the answer. For example, it may be suitable for a basic question-answering assistant over a small employee handbook. It is less suitable when questions require information from multiple documents, precise metadata filtering, complex reasoning, strict authorization, or high factual reliability.

c) Architecture Diagram

	              NAIVE RAG ARCHITECTURE
                     ======================

                   A. OFFLINE INDEXING FLOW

           PDF | Word | Website | Text Documents
                              |
                              v
                     Document Loading
                              |
                              v
                       Text Chunking
                              |
                              v
                       Embedding Model
                              |
                              v
                       Vector Database
                [Embeddings + Text + Metadata]


                    B. ONLINE QUERY FLOW

                        User Question
                              |
                              v
                       Query Embedding
                              |
                              v
                  Vector Similarity Search
                              |
                              v
                   Top-K Document Chunks
                              |
                              v
                       Context Injection
                              |
             +----------------+----------------+
             |                                 |
        User Question                  Retrieved Context
             |                                 |
             +----------------+----------------+
                              |
                              v
                       Augmented Prompt
                              |
                              v
                         LLM Generator
                              |
                              v
                      Generated Response

d) Code Example:

The following example demonstrates a basic Naive RAG architecture without using an orchestration framework. It creates document embeddings, performs Top-K retrieval, injects the retrieved context into a prompt, and represents the final LLM-generation step.

from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
import numpy as np


# =========================================================
# 1. KNOWLEDGE BASE
# =========================================================

documents = [
    {
        "id": "DOC-001",
        "source": "travel_policy.txt",
        "text": (
            "Employees travelling for approved business purposes "
            "can claim hotel accommodation for a maximum of seven "
            "consecutive days. Any extension requires written "
            "approval from the project manager."
        ),
    },
    {
        "id": "DOC-002",
        "source": "leave_policy.txt",
        "text": (
            "Permanent employees receive twelve paid sick-leave "
            "days during each calendar year. Unused sick leave "
            "cannot be carried forward."
        ),
    },
    {
        "id": "DOC-003",
        "source": "certification_policy.txt",
        "text": (
            "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees per financial "
            "year for approved technical certifications."
        ),
    },
    {
        "id": "DOC-004",
        "source": "remote_work_policy.txt",
        "text": (
            "Employees may work remotely for two days every week "
            "with approval from their reporting manager."
        ),
    },
]


# =========================================================
# 2. OFFLINE INDEXING
# =========================================================

embedding_model = SentenceTransformer("all-MiniLM-L6-v2")

document_texts = [
    document["text"]
    for document in documents
]

document_embeddings = embedding_model.encode(
    document_texts,
    normalize_embeddings=True
)


# =========================================================
# 3. TOP-K RETRIEVAL
# =========================================================

def retrieve_top_k(query, top_k=2):
    """Retrieve the Top-K documents using cosine similarity."""

    query_embedding = embedding_model.encode(
        [query],
        normalize_embeddings=True
    )[0]

    similarity_scores = np.dot(
        document_embeddings,
        query_embedding
    )

    top_indices = np.argsort(
        similarity_scores
    )[::-1][:top_k]

    retrieved_documents = []

    for index in top_indices:
        result = documents[int(index)].copy()
        result["score"] = float(similarity_scores[index])
        retrieved_documents.append(result)

    return retrieved_documents


# =========================================================
# 4. CONTEXT INJECTION AND PROMPT AUGMENTATION
# =========================================================

def build_augmented_prompt(query, retrieved_documents):
    """Inject retrieved chunks into a fixed prompt template."""

    context = "\n\n".join(
        [
            f"Source: {document['source']}\n"
            f"Content: {document['text']}"
            for document in retrieved_documents
        ]
    )

    prompt = f"""
You are an enterprise policy assistant.

Use only the retrieved context to answer the user's question.
If the answer is unavailable in the context, say:
"The required information was not found."

Retrieved context:
{context}

User question:
{query}

Answer:
""".strip()

    return prompt


# =========================================================
# 5. RESPONSE GENERATION
# =========================================================

def generate_response(prompt):
    """
    Placeholder representing an LLM call.

    In a real application, send this prompt to an LLM such as
    Azure OpenAI or another supported language model.
    """

    print("PROMPT SENT TO THE LLM")
    print("=" * 60)
    print(prompt)

    return (
        "Employees can claim hotel accommodation for a maximum "
        "of seven consecutive days during an approved business "
        "trip. Any extension requires written approval from the "
        "project manager.\n\n"
        "Source: travel_policy.txt"
    )


# =========================================================
# 6. EXECUTE THE NAIVE RAG PIPELINE
# =========================================================

user_query = (
    "How many days of hotel accommodation can an employee "
    "claim during a business trip?"
)

retrieved_results = retrieve_top_k(
    query=user_query,
    top_k=2
)

augmented_prompt = build_augmented_prompt(
    query=user_query,
    retrieved_documents=retrieved_results
)

final_response = generate_response(
    prompt=augmented_prompt
)

print("\nFINAL RESPONSE")
print("=" * 60)
print(final_response)


Q1. What is Naive RAG, and how does it work?

Naive RAG is the simplest form of Retrieval-Augmented Generation and follows a direct Retrieve, Augment, and Generate workflow. Documents are first divided into chunks, converted into embeddings, and stored in a vector database. When a user submits a question, the system converts the query into an embedding and retrieves the Top-K chunks with the highest semantic similarity.

The retrieved chunks are then directly inserted into a prompt together with the user’s question and instructions. This augmented prompt is sent to an LLM, which generates the final response. Naive RAG normally does not include advanced features such as query rewriting, hybrid retrieval, reranking, multi-step retrieval, context compression, or answer verification.

Q2. Why is it called Naive RAG?

It is called Naive RAG because it uses a simple and fixed pipeline that assumes the original query is suitable for retrieval, the initial Top-K chunks are relevant, and the LLM can produce a grounded answer from those chunks. It does not perform sophisticated processing before or after retrieval.

The term “naive” does not mean the architecture is always ineffective. It means that it represents the basic or foundational implementation of RAG. It can work well for simple document question-answering applications, but its assumptions often become unreliable when the knowledge base grows or the queries become ambiguous and complex.

Q3. What is the difference between Naive RAG and Advanced RAG?

Naive RAG performs direct semantic retrieval and passes the initial Top-K results to the LLM. It normally uses one user query, one retrieval step, a fixed prompt template, and one generation step. Its simplicity makes it easy to implement, but answer quality depends strongly on the initial query and retrieved chunks.

Advanced RAG introduces optimizations before and after retrieval. Pre-retrieval techniques include query rewriting, query expansion, metadata extraction, routing, and improved indexing. Post-retrieval techniques include reranking, filtering, deduplication, context compression, and relevance grading. These additions improve retrieval precision and make the supplied context more useful to the generator.

Q4. What are the main failure points in a Naive RAG architecture?

The first major failure point is document preparation. If the documents are incorrectly parsed or important information is divided across unsuitable chunk boundaries, the retriever may not locate complete evidence. The second failure point is retrieval because embedding similarity may identify topically related content that does not actually answer the question.

The third failure point is context injection, where irrelevant or redundant Top-K chunks consume the LLM’s context window and distract it from useful evidence. The fourth failure point is generation because the LLM may ignore the context, combine unrelated statements, or add unsupported information. These limitations explain why production systems often evolve from Naive RAG toward Advanced, Modular, or Agentic RAG architectures.


Chapter 4: Advanced RAG

i) Topic 1: Advanced RAG Architecture

a) Definition:

* Advanced RAG is an enhanced Retrieval-Augmented Generation architecture that introduces additional processing and quality-control mechanisms throughout 
  the RAG pipeline. 

* Unlike Naive RAG, which typically embeds the original query, retrieves a fixed Top-K set of chunks, and sends them directly to the LLM, Advanced RAG 
  improves the query, combines retrieval strategies, refines the retrieved evidence, and validates the generated answer. These improvements help the system 
  handle ambiguous queries, noisy knowledge bases, multiple document versions, exact identifiers, and questions requiring evidence from multiple sources.

* Advanced RAG normally applies improvements across four areas: indexing, pre-retrieval, post-retrieval, and generation. Indexing improvements include 
  semantic chunking and metadata enrichment; pre-retrieval improvements include Query Rewriting and Query Expansion; post-retrieval improvements include Re-
  Ranking and Context Compression; and generation improvements include grounding instructions, citation generation, response validation, and fallback 
  handling.

b) Purpose:

* The main purpose of Advanced RAG is to overcome the assumptions made in Naive RAG. A basic pipeline assumes that the original question is suitable for 
  retrieval, the first search returns the right documents, the initial ranking is correct, and every retrieved chunk is useful. Advanced RAG evaluates and 
  improves each of these stages rather than accepting them without verification.

* Advanced RAG improves retrieval precision and recall by selecting the appropriate strategy for each query. Semantic search finds conceptually related 
  information, keyword search identifies exact terminology, metadata filtering restricts the search to eligible documents, and Re-Ranking identifies which 
  candidates directly answer the question. These methods complement one another rather than competing as mutually exclusive choices.

* Another important purpose is to improve efficiency. Retrieving and sending excessive content to the LLM increases latency, input tokens, and cost. 
  Advanced RAG uses candidate filtering, deduplication, Re-Ranking, Context Compression, and token-budget controls to provide fewer but more relevant pieces 
  of evidence.

c) Architecture Diagram


External Data Sources
        |
        v
Document Parsing and Semantic Chunking
        |
        v
Metadata Enrichment and Embedding
        |
        v
Vector Index + Keyword Index + Structured Sources
        |
        v
User Question
        |
        v
Query Rewriting / Expansion / Decomposition
        |
        v
Hybrid or Multi-Stage Retrieval
        |
        v
Metadata Filtering and Re-Ranking
        |
        v
Context Compression
        |
        v
Prompt Construction
        |
        v
LLM Generation
        |
        v
Grounding, Citation and Safety Validation
        |
        v
Final Answer

d) Core Components

* Advanced indexing components include structure-aware parsers, semantic text splitters, embedding models, metadata extractors, dense vector indexes, sparse 
  indexes, and mechanisms for synchronizing updated documents.

* Query-side components include intent detection, Query Rewriting, Query Expansion, source routing, metadata-filter extraction, and question decomposition. 
  These components improve how the information requirement is represented before retrieval.

* Context-selection and generation components include multiple retrievers, fusion mechanisms, Re-Rankers, Context Compressors, prompt builders, LLMs, 
  citation processors, grounding validators, and evaluation pipelines.

e) Code Example:
from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import StateGraph, START, END

class RAGState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    rewritten_query: str
    documents: list
    context: str
    answer: str

def rewrite_query(state):
    query = state["question"].replace(
        "latest policy", "active 2026 policy"
    )
    return {"rewritten_query": query}

def retrieve(state):
    # Replace this with a LangChain vector-store retriever.
    documents = [
        {
            "source": "travel_policy_2026.txt",
            "text": (
                "Employees may claim hotel accommodation for "
                "seven consecutive days."
            ),
        }
    ]
    return {"documents": documents}

def build_context(state):
    context = "\n\n".join(
        f"Source: {doc['source']}\n{doc['text']}"
        for doc in state["documents"]
    )
    return {"context": context}

def generate(state):
    # Replace with ChatOpenAI or another chat model.
    answer = (
        "Employees may claim hotel accommodation for seven "
        "consecutive days. Source: travel_policy_2026.txt"
    )
    return {"answer": answer}

graph = StateGraph(RAGState)
graph.add_node("rewrite", rewrite_query)
graph.add_node("retrieve", retrieve)
graph.add_node("context", build_context)
graph.add_node("generate", generate)

graph.add_edge(START, "rewrite")
graph.add_edge("rewrite", "retrieve")
graph.add_edge("retrieve", "context")
graph.add_edge("context", "generate")
graph.add_edge("generate", END)

advanced_rag = graph.compile()

result = advanced_rag.invoke(
    {
        "question": "What does the latest policy allow?",
        "rewritten_query": "",
        "documents": [],
        "context": "",
        "answer": "",
    }
)

print(result["answer"])

f) Interview Q&A

Q1. What distinguishes Advanced RAG from Naive RAG?

Advanced RAG improves the query, retrieval method, result selection, context construction, and response validation. Naive RAG normally performs one vector search and directly injects the resulting Top-K chunks. Advanced RAG may use Query Rewriting, Hybrid Search, metadata filtering, Re-Ranking, Context Compression, Multi-Hop Retrieval, and grounding evaluation.

Q2. Does Advanced RAG require every available optimization?

No. Advanced RAG should be evaluation-driven. Techniques should be added only when they address a measured failure, such as low recall, poor ranking, excessive context, outdated documents, or multi-document reasoning.

Q3. What are its main trade-offs?

Advanced RAG generally improves accuracy and robustness but increases architectural complexity, model calls, latency, monitoring requirements, and cost. The system should balance quality improvements against operational constraints.

Topic 2: Multi-Stage Retrieval

a) Definition

* Multi-Stage Retrieval processes documents through multiple successive retrieval and ranking stages. A fast first-stage retriever searches the complete 
  corpus and produces a broad candidate collection, while subsequent stages apply stronger but more computationally expensive models to filter and rank 
  those candidates. This is commonly called a retrieve-and-rerank architecture.

* The first stage is optimized for high recall, meaning that it attempts to include the required evidence somewhere in the candidate set. The later stages 
  are optimized for high precision, meaning that they identify which candidates directly answer the question and should be supplied to the LLM.

* Multi-Stage Retrieval is different from Multi-Hop Retrieval. Multi-Stage Retrieval refines documents returned for the same information need, whereas 
  Multi-Hop Retrieval performs new searches using evidence discovered during earlier searches.

b) Purpose

* A fast embedding model or BM25 retriever can search millions of documents efficiently, but its first ranking may not precisely reflect answer relevance. A 
  cross-encoder provides stronger relevance judgement but is too expensive to apply to the entire corpus. Multi-Stage Retrieval divides these   
  responsibilities between separate stages.

* It allows the first stage to retrieve broadly without placing every candidate in the LLM prompt. The second stage reduces the candidate collection to a 
  smaller, more precise context, improving answer relevance and reducing token consumption.

* This architecture is especially useful for large document collections, similar documents, policy versions, and questions requiring precise distinctions 
  involving dates, entities, conditions, or negations.

c) Architecture Diagram

User Query
    |
    v
Fast First-Stage Retriever
BM25 / Vector / Hybrid Search
    |
    v
Top 20-100 Candidates
    |
    v
Metadata Filtering and Deduplication
    |
    v
Cross-Encoder Re-Ranking
    |
    v
Final Top 3-5 Documents
    |
    v
Prompt Builder and LLM

d) Core Components

* The candidate retriever performs fast large-scale retrieval using BM25, embeddings, or Hybrid Search. Its candidate depth should be large enough to 
  include the correct evidence.
* The filtering and fusion layer removes duplicates, enforces metadata conditions, and combines candidates from multiple retrieval methods.
* The Re-Ranker and selector assign precise relevance scores and reduce the broad candidate set to a small final evidence collection.

e) Code Example:

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_community.vectorstores import FAISS
from langchain_community.cross_encoders import HuggingFaceCrossEncoder
from langchain_huggingface import HuggingFaceEmbeddings
from langchain_classic.retrievers.contextual_compression import (
    ContextualCompressionRetriever,
)
from langchain_classic.retrievers.document_compressors import (
    CrossEncoderReranker,
)

documents = [
    Document(
        page_content="Hotel accommodation is allowed for seven days.",
        metadata={"source": "travel_policy.txt"},
    ),
    Document(
        page_content="Travel requests require manager approval.",
        metadata={"source": "travel_approval.txt"},
    ),
    Document(
        page_content="Travel expenses must be submitted in 15 days.",
        metadata={"source": "expense_policy.txt"},
    ),
]

embeddings = HuggingFaceEmbeddings(
    model_name="sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2"
)

store = FAISS.from_documents(documents, embeddings)

# Stage 1: retrieve broadly.
base_retriever = store.as_retriever(
    search_kwargs={"k": 3}
)

# Stage 2: rerank precisely.
cross_encoder = HuggingFaceCrossEncoder(
    model_name="cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2"
)

reranker = CrossEncoderReranker(
    model=cross_encoder,
    top_n=1,
)

multi_stage_retriever = ContextualCompressionRetriever(
    base_retriever=base_retriever,
    base_compressor=reranker,
)

results = multi_stage_retriever.invoke(
    "How many hotel days can an employee claim?"
)

for document in results:
    print(document.metadata["source"])
    print(document.page_content)


Interview Q&A

Q1. Why is the first stage recall-oriented?

* Its responsibility is to ensure that relevant evidence reaches the later stages. Some irrelevant documents are acceptable in the candidate set because the Re-Ranker will refine the results.

Q2. Why not use a cross-encoder directly on the entire corpus?

* A cross-encoder must jointly process every query-document pair. Applying it to millions of documents would create excessive computation and latency.

Q3. How should candidate depth be selected?

* It should be tuned through evaluation. A small candidate set may miss the answer, while a very large set increases Re-Ranking cost and latency.

Topic 3: Query Expansion

a) Definition
* Query Expansion broadens a query by adding synonyms, alternative expressions, abbreviations, related concepts, or multiple meaning-preserving 
  formulations. It improves retrieval when users and documents describe the same concept using different terminology.

* Expansion may create one enriched query or several separate queries. In Multi-Query Retrieval, every query variant is searched independently, after which 
  the results are merged and deduplicated.

* Other forms include domain-dictionary expansion, acronym expansion, multilingual alternatives, HyDE, and RAG-Fusion. HyDE generates a hypothetical answer-
  like passage and embeds it for retrieval rather than embedding only the original question.

b) Purpose
* Query Expansion addresses the vocabulary mismatch between conversational user language and formal document language.
* It improves Recall@K because alternative formulations may retrieve relevant documents from different semantic neighborhoods.
* Excessive expansion can produce query drift, where additional terms broaden or alter the original intention. The final candidates should therefore be Re-
  Ranked against the original question.

c) Architecture Diagram

Original Query
     |
     v
Expansion Generator
Synonyms / Multi-Query / HyDE
     |
     v
Multiple Search Queries
     |
     v
Retrieve for Each Query
     |
     v
Merge and Deduplicate
     |
     v
Reciprocal Rank Fusion
     |
     v
Re-Ranking and Final Context

d) Core Components

* The expansion generator creates controlled alternatives while preserving entities, dates, identifiers, restrictions, and negations.
* The multi-query retriever executes separate searches for the original and expanded queries.
* The fusion and Re-Ranking layer deduplicates results, combines ranks, and evaluates the candidates against the original query.

e) Code Example:

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_community.vectorstores import FAISS
from langchain_huggingface import HuggingFaceEmbeddings

documents = [
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees must re-register multi-factor authentication "
            "after replacing their mobile device."
        ),
        metadata={"source": "mfa_guide.txt"},
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "A new authenticator device can be configured from the "
            "security information portal."
        ),
        metadata={"source": "authenticator_guide.txt"},
    ),
]

embeddings = HuggingFaceEmbeddings(
    model_name="sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2"
)

store = FAISS.from_documents(documents, embeddings)

original_query = "How do I reset MFA after changing my phone?"

expanded_queries = [
    original_query,
    "How to re-register multi-factor authentication?",
    "How to configure a new authenticator device?",
    "How to update the verification method after phone replacement?",
]

unique_results = {}

for query in expanded_queries:
    for rank, document in enumerate(
        store.similarity_search(query, k=2),
        start=1,
    ):
        source = document.metadata["source"]

        if source not in unique_results:
            unique_results[source] = {
                "document": document,
                "score": 0.0,
            }

        unique_results[source]["score"] += 1 / (60 + rank)

ranked_results = sorted(
    unique_results.values(),
    key=lambda item: item["score"],
    reverse=True,
)

for result in ranked_results:
    print(result["document"].metadata["source"])

Q1. What problem does Query Expansion solve?
--> It solves vocabulary mismatch and improves retrieval coverage when the user and document do not use the same terminology.

Q2. What is Query Drift?
--> Query Drift occurs when the expanded query moves away from the original intention and retrieves related but inappropriate content.

Q3. How is Query Expansion evaluated?
--> It should be compared against a single-query baseline using Recall@K, Precision@K, ranking quality, final-answer accuracy, latency, and cost.

Topic 4: Query Rewriting

a) Definition

* Query Rewriting converts a raw question into a clearer, self-contained, retrieval-friendly query. It resolves pronouns, missing entities, conversational 
  references, informal phrasing, spelling problems, and unnecessary background information.
* It is especially important for conversational RAG. A follow-up such as "What is its limit?" may become "What is the annual reimbursement limit under the 
  2026 certification policy?"
* The rewritten query is generally used for retrieval, while the original question is retained for final generation. The rewrite is a search representation, 
  not factual evidence.

b) Purpose
* Query Rewriting makes incomplete questions understandable outside their original conversation.
* It translates informal user language into terminology that better matches the indexed documents.
* It must preserve critical details such as entities, policy versions, dates, identifiers, quantities, and negations.

c) Architecture Diagram

Chat History + Current Query
            |
            v
Query Context Analysis
            |
            v
Standalone Query Rewriter
            |
            v
Entity and Constraint Validation
            |
      +-----+-----+
      |           |
    Valid       Invalid
      |           |
      v           v
Rewritten Query  Original Query
      |           |
      +-----+-----+
            |
            v
         Retrieval


d) Core Components:
* The conversation-context selector identifies relevant prior turns without passing unnecessary history.
* The rewriting model may use business rules, dictionaries, or an LLM with structured output.
* The validator and fallback mechanism check intent preservation and avoid pipeline failure if rewriting produces an invalid result.

e) Practical Example:

from langchain_core.prompts import ChatPromptTemplate
from langchain_openai import ChatOpenAI
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field

class RewrittenQuery(BaseModel):
    search_query: str = Field(
        description="Standalone retrieval query"
    )

prompt = ChatPromptTemplate.from_messages(
    [
        (
            "system",
            """
Rewrite the current question as a standalone search query.

Rules:
- Preserve the original meaning.
- Resolve pronouns using the conversation.
- Preserve names, years, numbers and negations.
- Do not answer the question.
""",
        ),
        (
            "human",
            """
Conversation:
{history}

Current question:
{question}
""",
        ),
    ]
)

llm = ChatOpenAI(
    model="gpt-4.1-mini",
    temperature=0,
)

rewriter = prompt | llm.with_structured_output(RewrittenQuery)

result = rewriter.invoke(
    {
        "history": (
            "The user is discussing the 2026 professional "
            "certification reimbursement policy."
        ),
        "question": "What is the maximum amount?",
    }
)

print(result.search_query)

Interview Q&A

Q1. How does Query Rewriting differ from Query Expansion?
Rewriting produces a clearer search representation, whereas expansion broadens retrieval using additional terms or multiple formulations.

Q2. When should rewriting be skipped?
It can be skipped when the original query is already clear, self-contained, specific, and rich in searchable information.

Q3. What are its major risks?
The model may remove a negation, change an entity, alter a date, introduce assumptions, or answer the question rather than rewriting it.

Topic 5: Context Compression

a) Definition

* Context Compression removes irrelevant documents or irrelevant portions of documents after retrieval but before prompt construction. It helps the 
  generator receive only the evidence needed for the current question.
* Compression can include document filtering, relevant-sentence extraction, duplicate removal, sub-chunk selection, or summarization.
* The operation is query-dependent. The same travel document may be compressed differently for questions about hotels, meals, transportation, or approvals.

b) Purpose

* Large retrieved chunks often contain relevant and irrelevant text. Compression removes the irrelevant portions that can distract the LLM.
* It reduces input tokens, generation latency, and cost while allowing the initial retriever to search broadly.
* Aggressive compression can remove exceptions, conditions, headings, or important surrounding context, so evidence preservation must be evaluated.

c) Architecture Diagram

User Query
    |
    v
Base Retriever
    |
    v
Broad Candidate Set
    |
    v
Contextual Compressor
Filter / Extract / Summarize
    |
    v
Compressed Evidence
    |
    v
Prompt Builder and LLM

d) Core Components

* The base retriever supplies a broad candidate collection.
* The compressor removes complete documents or extracts relevant statements.
* The metadata and evaluation layer preserves citations and checks whether necessary evidence was accidentally removed.

e) Code Example:

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_community.vectorstores import FAISS
from langchain_openai import OpenAIEmbeddings, ChatOpenAI
from langchain_classic.retrievers import (
    ContextualCompressionRetriever,
)
from langchain_classic.retrievers.document_compressors import (
    LLMChainExtractor,
)

documents = [
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Travel must be approved before booking. Employees can "
            "claim hotel accommodation for seven days. Meal expenses "
            "use city-specific limits. Extensions require written "
            "manager approval."
        ),
        metadata={"source": "travel_policy.txt"},
    )
]

store = FAISS.from_documents(
    documents,
    OpenAIEmbeddings(model="text-embedding-3-small"),
)

base_retriever = store.as_retriever(
    search_kwargs={"k": 3}
)

compressor = LLMChainExtractor.from_llm(
    ChatOpenAI(model="gpt-4.1-mini", temperature=0)
)

compression_retriever = ContextualCompressionRetriever(
    base_retriever=base_retriever,
    base_compressor=compressor,
)

results = compression_retriever.invoke(
    "How many hotel days are allowed and what is needed "
    "for an extension?"
)

for document in results:
    print(document.page_content)

Interview Q&A

Q1. What is the difference between Re-Ranking and Context Compression?
* Re-Ranking changes the document order and selection. Context Compression changes the content inside selected documents or filters documents completely.

Q2. What is extractive compression?
* Extractive compression preserves original sentences or passages instead of rewriting them, making it safer for evidence-sensitive applications.

Q3. What should be measured?
* Measure context relevance, evidence recall, compression ratio, token reduction, answer faithfulness, latency, and citation correctness.

Topic 6: Re-Ranking

a) Definition
* Re-Ranking evaluates the candidates returned by an initial retriever and assigns more accurate relevance scores. It changes the result order so that the 
  strongest evidence appears first.
* A cross-encoder is commonly used because it processes the query and each candidate document jointly rather than comparing independently generated 
  embeddings.
* Re-Ranking is a post-retrieval operation. It does not search the entire corpus or generate the final answer.

b) Purpose
* Vector similarity can identify topical similarity without determining whether a passage actually answers the question.
* Re-Ranking allows fast retrieval to prioritize recall and the second-stage model to prioritize precision.
* It improves context ordering, reduces irrelevant evidence, and can improve answer faithfulness.

c) Architectural Diagram

User Query
    |
    v
Vector or Hybrid Retriever
    |
    v
Top 20 Candidates
    |
    v
Cross-Encoder
Query + Document Pair Scoring
    |
    v
New Relevance Order
    |
    v
Final Top 3-5 Documents

d) Core Components
* The bi-encoder retriever independently embeds queries and documents for efficient search.
* The cross-encoder jointly processes query-document pairs for accurate scoring.
* The Top-N selector controls final context size and may enforce score thresholds.

e) Practical Code Example

from langchain_community.cross_encoders import HuggingFaceCrossEncoder
from langchain_classic.retrievers.document_compressors import (
    CrossEncoderReranker,
)
from langchain_classic.retrievers.contextual_compression import (
    ContextualCompressionRetriever,
)

cross_encoder = HuggingFaceCrossEncoder(
    model_name="cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2"
)

reranker = CrossEncoderReranker(
    model=cross_encoder,
    top_n=3,
)

reranking_retriever = ContextualCompressionRetriever(
    base_retriever=base_retriever,
    base_compressor=reranker,
)

documents = reranking_retriever.invoke(
    "How many hotel days can an employee claim?"
)

for position, document in enumerate(documents, start=1):
    print(position, document.metadata["source"])

Interview Q&A

Q1. Why are cross-encoders not normally used for first-stage search?
--> They must process every query-document pair, making them unsuitable for direct search over millions of documents.

Q2. Can a Re-Ranker recover a missing document?
--> No. It can reorder only the candidates supplied by the first-stage retriever.

Q3. What metrics evaluate Re-Ranking?
--> Common metrics include Precision@K, Mean Reciprocal Rank, NDCG, context relevance, latency, and final-answer quality.

Topic 7: Metadata Filtering

a) Definition
* Metadata Filtering restricts retrieval according to structured attributes attached to documents. Examples include source, year, status, region, 
  department, language, document type, tenant, and access level.
* Semantic search determines which eligible documents are meaningfully similar, while metadata filtering determines which documents are allowed to 
  participate.
* Filtering may occur before, during, or after similarity search, depending on the vector database.

b) Purpose
* It prevents outdated, geographically incorrect, draft, or contextually inappropriate documents from entering the prompt.
* It supports tenant, department, role, and document-level restrictions when combined with trusted authorization information.
* It narrows the search space and may reduce irrelevant retrieval and Re-Ranking workload.

c) Architectural Diagram

User Query + Trusted Identity
            |
            v
Extract Query Constraints
            |
            v
Build Metadata Filter
            |
            v
Validate Fields and Permissions
            |
            v
Filtered Vector Search
            |
            v
Eligible Relevant Documents


d) Core Components
* The metadata schema defines consistent fields and acceptable values.
* The filter builder maps natural-language requirements and trusted user attributes to structured conditions.
* The vector-store filtering layer enforces conditions during search, while evaluation tests for improper exclusion or unauthorized leakage.

e) Code Example

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_chroma import Chroma
from langchain_huggingface import HuggingFaceEmbeddings

documents = [
    Document(
        page_content="Hotel accommodation is allowed for seven days.",
        metadata={
            "country": "India",
            "year": 2026,
            "status": "active",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content="Hotel accommodation was allowed for five days.",
        metadata={
            "country": "India",
            "year": 2024,
            "status": "archived",
        },
    ),
]

embeddings = HuggingFaceEmbeddings(
    model_name="sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2"
)

store = Chroma.from_documents(
    documents,
    embeddings,
    collection_name="policies",
)

filter_expression = {
    "$and": [
        {"country": {"$eq": "India"}},
        {"year": {"$eq": 2026}},
        {"status": {"$eq": "active"}},
    ]
}

retriever = store.as_retriever(
    search_kwargs={
        "k": 3,
        "filter": filter_expression,
    }
)

results = retriever.invoke(
    "How many hotel days are currently allowed?"
)

for document in results:
    print(document.metadata)
    print(document.page_content)

Interview Q&A

Q1. How does Metadata Filtering differ from semantic search?
--> Semantic search compares meaning; Metadata Filtering enforces exact structured conditions.

Q2. What is pre-filtering?
--> Pre-filtering restricts the eligible collection before similarity ranking. It is preferred for strict permission and version requirements.

Q3. Can metadata filters replace authorization?
--> No. Security filters must be based on trusted identity and should complement proper authorization, tenant isolation, encryption, and audit controls.


Topic 8: Hybrid Search

a) Definition
* Hybrid Search combines sparse keyword retrieval and dense vector retrieval. Keyword retrieval identifies exact terms, while vector retrieval identifies 
  meaning-based similarity.
* BM25 is commonly used for the keyword branch because it considers term frequency, inverse document frequency, and document length.
* The two ranked result lists are merged through weighted fusion or a rank-based method such as Reciprocal Rank Fusion.

b) Purpose
* Hybrid Search handles queries containing both exact identifiers and natural-language descriptions.
* BM25 is effective for error codes, product names, policy references, and uncommon domain terms.
* Vector retrieval is effective for synonyms, paraphrases, and informal language. Combining them improves coverage.

c) Architectural Diagram
                   User Query
                       |
           +-----------+-----------+
           |                       |
           v                       v
       BM25 Search            Vector Search
       Exact Terms           Semantic Meaning
           |                       |
           +-----------+-----------+
                       |
                 Result Fusion
                       |
                 Deduplication
                       |
                   Re-Ranking
                       |
                 Final Evidence


d) Core Components
* The BM25 retriever handles lexical matching.
* The vector retriever handles semantic similarity.
* The fusion and Re-Ranking layer merges duplicates, combines rankings, and selects the final context.

e) Code Example
from langchain_community.retrievers import BM25Retriever
from langchain_community.vectorstores import FAISS
from langchain_huggingface import HuggingFaceEmbeddings

bm25 = BM25Retriever.from_documents(documents)
bm25.k = 4

embeddings = HuggingFaceEmbeddings(
    model_name="sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2"
)

store = FAISS.from_documents(documents, embeddings)
vector_retriever = store.as_retriever(
    search_kwargs={"k": 4}
)

query = "What causes ERR-AUTH-4017 after changing a phone?"

ranked_lists = [
    bm25.invoke(query),
    vector_retriever.invoke(query),
]

fused = {}

for result_list in ranked_lists:
    for rank, document in enumerate(result_list, start=1):
        document_id = document.metadata["id"]

        if document_id not in fused:
            fused[document_id] = {
                "document": document,
                "score": 0.0,
            }

        fused[document_id]["score"] += 1 / (60 + rank)

final_results = sorted(
    fused.values(),
    key=lambda result: result["score"],
    reverse=True,
)

for result in final_results[:3]:
    print(result["document"].metadata["source"])

f) Interview Q&A

Q1. Why combine BM25 with vector search?
--> BM25 provides exact lexical matching, while vector search provides semantic understanding. Their strengths are complementary.

Q2. How can the result lists be combined?
--> Use normalized weighted-score fusion or rank-based fusion such as RRF.

Q3. Does Hybrid Search remove the need for Re-Ranking?
--> No. Fusion improves candidate coverage, but a Re-Ranker may still be needed to determine which candidates directly answer the question.


Topic 9: Multi-Hop Retrieval

a) Definition
* Multi-Hop Retrieval performs multiple dependent searches when the answer requires evidence distributed across several documents or data sources. 
  Information discovered in one hop is used to create or guide the next search.
* The first hop may identify an entity or relationship, while the second hop retrieves a policy or value associated with that entity.
* Multi-Hop Retrieval is different from Multi-Stage Retrieval because it performs new evidence-dependent searches rather than only refining one candidate 
  set.


b) Purpose
* It answers compositional questions that cannot be solved from one retrieved chunk.
* It supports retrieval across vector databases, relational databases, APIs, and knowledge graphs.
* It prevents the system from attempting a final search before discovering the entity or relationship required to formulate that search.

c) Architectural Diagram
Original Question
       |
       v
Question Decomposition
       |
       v
Hop 1 Retrieval
       |
       v
Intermediate Entity or Relationship
       |
       v
Generate Next Query
       |
       v
Hop 2 Retrieval
       |
       v
Evidence Sufficiency Check
       |
  +----+----+
  |         |
No Evidence  Sufficient
  |         |
Next Hop     Evidence Aggregation
                 |
                 v
             Final Answer


d) Core Components
* The planner decomposes the original question into dependent subqueries.
* The iterative retriever and shared state execute searches and preserve discovered entities.
* The evidence evaluator and aggregator determine when sufficient evidence exists and combine source documents for generation.

e) Code Example
from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import StateGraph, START, END

class MultiHopState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    department: str
    evidence: list[str]
    answer: str

def retrieve_project(state):
    evidence = (
        "Project Orion is owned by the Data Engineering department."
    )
    return {
        "department": "Data Engineering",
        "evidence": [evidence],
    }

def retrieve_policy(state):
    policy = (
        f"Employees in {state['department']} may claim 30,000 "
        "rupees annually for approved certifications."
    )
    return {
        "evidence": state["evidence"] + [policy],
    }

def generate_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": "\n".join(state["evidence"])
    }

graph = StateGraph(MultiHopState)
graph.add_node("retrieve_project", retrieve_project)
graph.add_node("retrieve_policy", retrieve_policy)
graph.add_node("generate", generate_answer)

graph.add_edge(START, "retrieve_project")
graph.add_edge("retrieve_project", "retrieve_policy")
graph.add_edge("retrieve_policy", "generate")
graph.add_edge("generate", END)

multi_hop_rag = graph.compile()

result = multi_hop_rag.invoke(
    {
        "question": (
            "What is the certification limit for the department "
            "that owns Project Orion?"
        ),
        "department": "",
        "evidence": [],
        "answer": "",
    }
)

print(result["answer"])

Interview Q&A

Q1. What is the defining feature of Multi-Hop Retrieval?
Later searches depend on facts obtained during earlier searches.

Q2. What is its primary risk?
Error propagation. A wrong entity retrieved during Hop 1 can send every later hop down an incorrect path.

Q3. How should it be controlled?
Use per-hop validation, source preservation, repeated-query detection, confidence thresholds, and a maximum-hop limit.


Topic 10: Retrieval Optimization

a) Definition
* Retrieval Optimization is the systematic process of improving how a RAG system finds, ranks, filters, and selects evidence from a knowledge base. It 
  covers the complete retrieval journey, including document parsing, chunking, embedding generation, query transformation, search configuration, Re-Ranking, 
  and final context selection.

* Retrieval Optimization is not a single algorithm or component. It combines several techniques such as Query Rewriting, Query Expansion, Hybrid Search, 
  metadata filtering, Top-K tuning, Re-Ranking, and Context Compression, depending on the retrieval problems observed in the application.

* The purpose is to return documents that are relevant enough to answer the question while meeting acceptable limits for latency, token usage, 
  infrastructure cost, and security. A retriever that produces accurate results but takes too long may still be unsuitable for a real-time enterprise 
  assistant.

* Retrieval and generation should be evaluated separately because they have different failure modes. If the correct evidence is absent, the problem is 
  retrieval-related; if the evidence is present but incorrectly used, the problem may be in context construction, prompting, or generation.

b) Purpose
* The primary purpose of Retrieval Optimization is to ensure that the correct supporting document appears within the retrieved candidate set. Even when the 
  answer exists in the knowledge base, retrieval can fail because of poor parsing, inappropriate chunking, an unsuitable embedding model, weak query 
  formulation, or an incorrect search strategy.

* Retrieval Optimization balances recall and precision. Recall measures whether the system finds the relevant documents, while precision measures how many 
  retrieved documents are actually useful. Increasing Top-K may improve recall but can reduce precision by introducing irrelevant information into the 
  candidate collection.

* It also controls operational performance by reducing unnecessary searches, Re-Ranking operations, and prompt tokens. For example, the system may retrieve 
  20 candidates for good recall, Re-Rank them, and send only the best 4 chunks to the LLM, producing focused evidence without losing retrieval coverage.

* Different query types require different retrieval strategies. Conceptual questions may work well with semantic search, error codes may require BM25, 
  policy-version questions need metadata filtering, and relationship-based questions may require Multi-Hop Retrieval. Optimization helps select the 
  appropriate technique for each failure pattern.

c) Architecture Diagram

                   RETRIEVAL OPTIMIZATION CYCLE
                   ============================

              Evaluation Dataset
       Questions + Expected Documents + Answers
                         |
                         v
                 Baseline Retriever
                         |
                         v
             Measure Retrieval Performance
       Recall@K | Precision@K | MRR | NDCG
