                         |
                         v
                Analyze Failure Patterns
     Missing Evidence | Poor Ranking | Excessive Noise
                         |
                         v
                Apply Targeted Improvement
  Chunking | Query Rewrite | Hybrid Search | Re-Ranking
       Metadata Filtering | Context Compression
                         |
                         v
                 Optimized Retriever
                         |
                         v
          Evaluate Against the Same Dataset
                         |
                         v
         Compare Quality, Latency, Cost and Safety
                         |
              +----------+----------+
              |                     |
              v                     v
       Improvement Found       No Improvement
              |                     |
              v                     v
        Deploy and Monitor     Modify or Roll Back

d) Core Components

1. Evaluation Dataset and Ground Truth
The evaluation dataset contains representative user questions, expected relevant document IDs, and optionally reference answers. It acts as the ground truth against which retrieval configurations are compared, preventing developers from optimizing the system based only on a few manually selected examples.

2. Retrieval Pipeline
The retrieval pipeline includes document chunking, embeddings, vector indexes, keyword indexes, query processing, metadata filters, Re-Rankers, and Context Compressors. Each component should be independently configurable so that developers can identify which modification produces a measurable improvement.

3. Retrieval Metrics
Retrieval metrics provide objective measurements of candidate coverage and ranking quality. Recall@K determines whether relevant evidence was retrieved, Precision@K measures the amount of relevant content in the results, MRR measures how early the first correct document appears, and NDCG evaluates the complete ranking.

4. Monitoring and Experimentation
Offline evaluation compares configurations before deployment, while online monitoring measures behaviour on real production queries. LangSmith supports tracing retrieval and generation separately, comparing experiments, monitoring latency and cost, and collecting human or automated evaluation results.

e) Code Example
import time
from statistics import mean

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_community.vectorstores import FAISS
from langchain_huggingface import HuggingFaceEmbeddings


# =========================================================
# 1. CREATE THE KNOWLEDGE BASE
# =========================================================

documents = [
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "The active travel policy allows employees to claim "
            "hotel accommodation for seven consecutive days."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "TRAVEL-2026",
            "source": "travel_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "travel",
            "status": "active",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "The archived travel policy allowed employees to claim "
            "hotel accommodation for five consecutive days."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "TRAVEL-2024",
            "source": "travel_policy_2024.txt",
            "topic": "travel",
            "status": "archived",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees can claim up to 25,000 rupees per financial "
            "year for approved professional certifications."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "CERT-2026",
            "source": "certification_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "certification",
            "status": "active",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Permanent employees receive twelve paid sick-leave "
            "days during each calendar year."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "LEAVE-2026",
            "source": "leave_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "leave",
            "status": "active",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees must re-register multi-factor authentication "
            "after replacing their mobile device."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "MFA-DEVICE",
            "source": "mfa_device_guide.txt",
            "topic": "identity",
            "status": "active",
        },
    ),
]


# =========================================================
# 2. CREATE THE EMBEDDING MODEL AND VECTOR STORE
# =========================================================

embedding_model = HuggingFaceEmbeddings(
    model_name="sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2",
    encode_kwargs={
        "normalize_embeddings": True,
    },
)

vector_store = FAISS.from_documents(
    documents=documents,
    embedding=embedding_model,
)


# =========================================================
# 3. CREATE THE EVALUATION DATASET
# =========================================================

evaluation_dataset = [
    {
        "question": (
            "Under the current policy, how many hotel days "
            "can an employee claim?"
        ),
        "relevant_ids": {"TRAVEL-2026"},
        "topic": "travel",
    },
    {
        "question": (
            "What is the annual professional certification "
            "reimbursement limit?"
        ),
        "relevant_ids": {"CERT-2026"},
        "topic": "certification",
    },
    {
        "question": (
            "How many paid sick-leave days are available?"
        ),
        "relevant_ids": {"LEAVE-2026"},
        "topic": "leave",
    },
    {
        "question": (
            "What must an employee do after changing the phone "
            "used for multi-factor authentication?"
        ),
        "relevant_ids": {"MFA-DEVICE"},
        "topic": "identity",
    },
]


# =========================================================
# 4. DEFINE RETRIEVAL METRICS
# =========================================================

def precision_at_k(retrieved_ids, relevant_ids):
    if not retrieved_ids:
        return 0.0

    relevant_count = sum(
        document_id in relevant_ids
        for document_id in retrieved_ids
    )

    return relevant_count / len(retrieved_ids)


def recall_at_k(retrieved_ids, relevant_ids):
    if not relevant_ids:
        return 0.0

    relevant_count = sum(
        document_id in relevant_ids
        for document_id in retrieved_ids
    )

    return relevant_count / len(relevant_ids)


def reciprocal_rank(retrieved_ids, relevant_ids):
    for rank, document_id in enumerate(
        retrieved_ids,
        start=1,
    ):
        if document_id in relevant_ids:
            return 1.0 / rank

    return 0.0


# =========================================================
# 5. BASELINE RETRIEVER
# =========================================================

def baseline_retrieve(question, top_k=2):
    return vector_store.similarity_search(
        query=question,
        k=top_k,
    )


# =========================================================
# 6. OPTIMIZED RETRIEVER
# =========================================================

def optimized_retrieve(
    question,
    topic,
    top_k=2,
):
    """
    Retrieve a broader candidate set using MMR and then
    retain active documents from the expected topic.
    """

    candidates = vector_store.max_marginal_relevance_search(
        query=question,
        k=5,
        fetch_k=len(documents),
        lambda_mult=0.75,
    )

    filtered_documents = [
        document
        for document in candidates
        if document.metadata["status"] == "active"
        and document.metadata["topic"] == topic
    ]

    return filtered_documents[:top_k]


# =========================================================
# 7. EVALUATE A RETRIEVER
# =========================================================

def evaluate_retriever(name, retrieval_function):
    evaluation_rows = []

    for example in evaluation_dataset:
        start_time = time.perf_counter()

        if name == "baseline":
            retrieved_documents = retrieval_function(
                example["question"]
            )
        else:
            retrieved_documents = retrieval_function(
                example["question"],
                example["topic"],
            )

        latency_ms = (
            time.perf_counter() - start_time
        ) * 1000

        retrieved_ids = [
            document.metadata["id"]
            for document in retrieved_documents
        ]

        evaluation_rows.append(
            {
                "question": example["question"],
                "retrieved_ids": retrieved_ids,
                "precision": precision_at_k(
                    retrieved_ids,
                    example["relevant_ids"],
                ),
                "recall": recall_at_k(
                    retrieved_ids,
                    example["relevant_ids"],
                ),
                "reciprocal_rank": reciprocal_rank(
                    retrieved_ids,
                    example["relevant_ids"],
                ),
                "latency_ms": latency_ms,
            }
        )

    return {
        "name": name,
        "rows": evaluation_rows,
        "average_precision": mean(
            row["precision"]
            for row in evaluation_rows
        ),
        "average_recall": mean(
            row["recall"]
            for row in evaluation_rows
        ),
        "mrr": mean(
            row["reciprocal_rank"]
            for row in evaluation_rows
        ),
        "average_latency_ms": mean(
            row["latency_ms"]
            for row in evaluation_rows
        ),
    }


# =========================================================
# 8. RUN BOTH EVALUATIONS
# =========================================================

baseline_results = evaluate_retriever(
    name="baseline",
    retrieval_function=baseline_retrieve,
)

optimized_results = evaluate_retriever(
    name="optimized",
    retrieval_function=optimized_retrieve,
)


# =========================================================
# 9. DISPLAY THE COMPARISON
# =========================================================

def display_results(results):
    print("\nConfiguration:", results["name"])
    print("=" * 70)

    for row in results["rows"]:
        print("\nQuestion:", row["question"])
        print("Retrieved IDs:", row["retrieved_ids"])
        print("Precision@K:", round(row["precision"], 3))
        print("Recall@K:", round(row["recall"], 3))
        print(
            "Reciprocal Rank:",
            round(row["reciprocal_rank"], 3),
        )
        print(
            "Latency:",
            round(row["latency_ms"], 2),
            "ms",
        )

    print("\nAggregate Results")
    print("-" * 70)
    print(
        "Average Precision@K:",
        round(results["average_precision"], 3),
    )
    print(
        "Average Recall@K:",
        round(results["average_recall"], 3),
    )
    print(
        "Mean Reciprocal Rank:",
        round(results["mrr"], 3),
    )
    print(
        "Average Latency:",
        round(results["average_latency_ms"], 2),
        "ms",
    )


display_results(baseline_results)
display_results(optimized_results)


Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Retrieval Optimization in Advanced RAG?

* Retrieval Optimization is the evaluation-driven process of improving how a RAG system prepares documents, understands queries, searches the knowledge 
  base, ranks candidates, applies filters, and selects the final context. It includes techniques such as chunking optimization, embedding selection, Query 
  Rewriting, Query Expansion, Hybrid Search, metadata filtering, Re-Ranking, and Context Compression.

* The objective is not only to maximize retrieval accuracy. The system must balance Recall@K, Precision@K, ranking quality, latency, prompt size, 
  operational cost, and security. Optimization should begin with a measurable baseline and use representative evaluation questions rather than depending on 
  assumptions.

Q2. How do you identify whether a RAG failure comes from retrieval or generation?

* First, inspect the retrieved context and determine whether the correct supporting document is present. If the required evidence is absent, the problem is 
  primarily retrieval-related and may require better chunking, Query Rewriting, Hybrid Search, metadata filtering, candidate depth, or embedding selection.

* If the correct evidence is present but the response is incorrect, the problem may be related to context ordering, prompt construction, conflicting 
  evidence, or the generator’s failure to follow instructions. Retrieval and generation should therefore be traced and evaluated independently.

Q3. How should Top-K be optimized?

* A small Top-K value keeps the context focused and reduces latency, but it may exclude relevant evidence. A large Top-K value improves candidate recall but 
  increases Re-Ranking cost, prompt tokens, and contextual noise. There is no single Top-K value that works for every knowledge base.

* A practical architecture separates candidate_k from final_k. The first-stage retriever may collect 20 candidates, after which a Re-Ranker selects the best 
  3 to 5 chunks for generation. Both values should be tuned using Recall@K, Precision@K, answer faithfulness, latency, and cost.

Q4. What is the correct process for optimizing retrieval?

* The process begins by creating a representative evaluation dataset containing questions and expected supporting documents. Developers then run a baseline 
  retriever, calculate retrieval metrics, inspect failed cases, and classify the failures according to their probable cause.

* A targeted optimization is then introduced, such as changing chunk size, adding Query Expansion, using Hybrid Search, applying metadata filters, or 
  introducing a Re-Ranker. The modified retriever is evaluated against the same dataset, and it is deployed only if it produces a measurable improvement 
  without unacceptable regressions in latency, cost, or security.


Chapter 5: Context Window Management


a. Topic 1: Token Limits

i) Defintion
* A token limit is the maximum number of tokens an LLM can process within a single request. Tokens are the smaller units into which the model’s tokenizer 
  divides input text, and a token may represent a complete word, part of a word, punctuation mark, number, or special symbol.

* The model’s context window contains all tokens supplied during one invocation, including system instructions, conversation history, retrieved RAG 
  documents, tool outputs, the current user query, and sometimes reserved space for the generated answer. Therefore, the context window is not available 
  exclusively for retrieved documents.

* Input and output tokens normally share the available token budget. If an application sends a large prompt and reserves insufficient space for generation, 
  the model may produce a shortened response or the provider may reject the request for exceeding the supported context length.

* A token limit differs from a character count or word count because tokenization depends on the model’s tokenizer and the text being processed. Technical 
  identifiers, code, uncommon words, and some languages may use more tokens than ordinary English sentences of a similar visible length.

ii) Purpose
* Token limits create a practical boundary on how much information the model can examine during one generation request. Without proper management, a RAG 
  application may attempt to include system instructions, a long chat history, and too many retrieved chunks, causing context overflow or leaving 
  insufficient tokens for the answer.

* Understanding token limits helps developers calculate a prompt budget before constructing the final LLM request. The total budget must be divided among 
  fixed instructions, conversation history, retrieved evidence, the current question, and reserved answer capacity rather than allowing one component to 
  consume the entire context window.

* Token management also controls application cost and latency because most hosted LLM services charge according to processed input and output tokens. Even 
  if a model supports a large context window, sending unnecessary content can increase response time, cost, and the possibility that useful evidence becomes 
  diluted by irrelevant information.

* In a RAG application, the goal is not to fill the complete context window but to select the smallest collection of evidence that sufficiently answers the 
  question. Re-Ranking, Context Compression, message trimming, summarization, and dynamic Top-K selection can help the application remain within its token 
  budget.

iii) Context-Window Budget Formula

Total model context window
        =
System instructions
        +
Conversation history
        +
Retrieved RAG context
        +
Current user question
        +
Tool results and formatting
        +
Reserved output tokens


A simplified context budget can be calculated as:

Available input budget
    =
Model context limit
    -
Reserved output tokens
    -
Safety buffer

The budget available specifically for retrieved documents is:

Retrieval context budget
    =
Available input budget
    -
System prompt tokens
    -
Conversation-history tokens
    -
User-query tokens
    -
Tool and formatting tokens

Example calculation

Model context limit          = 16,000 tokens
Reserved answer capacity     =  2,000 tokens
Safety buffer                =    500 tokens
System prompt                =    700 tokens
Conversation history         =  3,000 tokens
Current user query           =    300 tokens
Tool and formatting content  =    500 tokens
                              ----------------
Available retrieval budget   =  9,000 tokens


iv) Architectural Diagram

                     USER QUESTION
                           |
                           v
                Retrieve Candidate Chunks
                           |
                           v
                 Re-Rank by Relevance
                           |
                           v
                Count Tokens in Each Part
                           |
           +---------------+---------------+
           |                               |
           v                               v
  System Instructions             Conversation History
           |                               |
           +---------------+---------------+
                           |
                           v
                Calculate Remaining Budget
                           |
                           v
              Select Chunks Within Budget
                           |
                           v
                  Construct Final Prompt
                           |
                           v
               Prompt Fits Context Window?
                    /              \
                  No                Yes
                  |                  |
                  v                  v
       Trim, Compress or       Send Prompt
         Summarize Context        to LLM
                  |                  |
                  +--------<---------+
                                     |
                                     v
                              Generated Answer


v) Core Components

1. Tokenizer and Token Counter
The tokenizer converts text into the token units recognized by the selected model, while the token counter measures how many tokens a prompt component consumes. Model-specific counting is preferable because tokenization and chat-message overhead can differ across models and providers.

2. Token-Budget Allocator
The budget allocator reserves capacity for the generated answer and divides the remaining context among system instructions, conversation history, retrieved evidence, and the current question. It should retain a safety margin rather than constructing a prompt exactly equal to the model’s maximum limit.

3. History and Context Manager
The history manager decides which previous conversation messages remain relevant to the current interaction. The context manager separately selects retrieved documents using relevance scores, metadata, token size, source diversity, and the remaining prompt budget.

4. Overflow-Handling Mechanism
The overflow handler is activated when the assembled prompt exceeds its allowed budget. It can remove low-priority evidence, compress large passages, summarize older conversation turns, or use a larger-context model when the additional expense is justified.

vi) Code Example

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_core.messages import (
    AIMessage,
    HumanMessage,
    SystemMessage,
    trim_messages,
)
from langchain_core.prompts import ChatPromptTemplate
from langchain_core.output_parsers import StrOutputParser
from langchain_openai import ChatOpenAI


# =========================================================
# 1. CONFIGURE THE MODEL AND TOKEN BUDGETS
# =========================================================

llm = ChatOpenAI(
    model="gpt-4.1-mini",
    temperature=0,
)

# Educational values used to demonstrate budget allocation.
MODEL_CONTEXT_LIMIT = 8_000
RESERVED_OUTPUT_TOKENS = 1_000
SAFETY_BUFFER = 300
MAX_HISTORY_TOKENS = 1_500

available_input_budget = (
    MODEL_CONTEXT_LIMIT
    - RESERVED_OUTPUT_TOKENS
    - SAFETY_BUFFER
)


# =========================================================
# 2. CREATE A LONG CONVERSATION HISTORY
# =========================================================

messages = [
    SystemMessage(
        content=(
            "You are an enterprise policy assistant. Answer only "
            "from approved policy evidence and cite the source."
        )
    ),
    HumanMessage(
        content=(
            "I am reviewing the company business-travel policy."
        )
    ),
    AIMessage(
        content=(
            "I can help with accommodation, meal, transport, "
            "approval, and expense-submission rules."
        )
    ),
    HumanMessage(
        content=(
            "First explain the approval requirement for travel."
        )
    ),
    AIMessage(
        content=(
            "Business travel must be approved by the reporting "
            "manager before tickets and accommodation are booked."
        )
    ),
    HumanMessage(
        content=(
            "Now I want to understand the hotel-accommodation rule."
        )
    ),
]


# =========================================================
# 3. TRIM HISTORY TO ITS ASSIGNED TOKEN BUDGET
# =========================================================

trimmed_messages = trim_messages(
    messages,
    max_tokens=MAX_HISTORY_TOKENS,
    token_counter=llm,
    strategy="last",
    include_system=True,
    start_on="human",
    allow_partial=False,
)


# =========================================================
# 4. CREATE RETRIEVED DOCUMENT CANDIDATES
# =========================================================

retrieved_documents = [
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees travelling for approved business purposes "
            "can claim hotel accommodation for seven consecutive "
            "days. Any extension requires written approval from "
            "the project manager."
        ),
        metadata={
            "source": "travel_policy_2026.txt",
            "relevance_score": 0.96,
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees must submit travel expense claims within "
            "fifteen working days after completing the journey."
        ),
        metadata={
            "source": "expense_submission_policy.txt",
            "relevance_score": 0.71,
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Meal expenses are reimbursed according to the limit "
            "assigned to the employee's destination city."
        ),
        metadata={
            "source": "meal_policy_2026.txt",
            "relevance_score": 0.58,
        },
    ),
]


# =========================================================
# 5. DEFINE A MODEL-AWARE TOKEN-COUNTING HELPER
# =========================================================

def count_text_tokens(text: str) -> int:
    message = HumanMessage(content=text)
    return llm.get_num_tokens_from_messages([message])


# =========================================================
# 6. CALCULATE THE RETRIEVAL-CONTEXT BUDGET
# =========================================================

question = (
    "How many hotel-accommodation days can an employee claim, "
    "and what is required for an extension?"
)

system_prompt = (
    "Answer using only the supplied evidence. Preserve numbers "
    "and approval conditions, and mention the source."
)

history_tokens = llm.get_num_tokens_from_messages(
    trimmed_messages
)

fixed_prompt_tokens = (
    count_text_tokens(system_prompt)
    + count_text_tokens(question)
)

retrieval_budget = (
    available_input_budget
    - history_tokens
    - fixed_prompt_tokens
)

if retrieval_budget <= 0:
    raise ValueError(
        "No token budget remains for retrieved context."
    )


# =========================================================
# 7. SELECT DOCUMENTS WITHIN THE AVAILABLE BUDGET
# =========================================================

def select_documents_within_budget(
    documents: list[Document],
    token_budget: int,
) -> listselected_documents = []
    used_tokens = 0

    ranked_documents = sorted(
        documents,
        key=lambda document: document.metadata[
            "relevance_score"
        ],
        reverse=True,
    )

    for document in ranked_documents:
        document_tokens = count_text_tokens(
            document.page_content
        )

        if used_tokens + document_tokens > token_budget:
            continue

        selected_documents.append(document)
        used_tokens += document_tokens

    return selected_documents


selected_documents = select_documents_within_budget(
    documents=retrieved_documents,
    token_budget=retrieval_budget,
)


# =========================================================
# 8. FORMAT THE SELECTED CONTEXT
# =========================================================

def format_documents(
    documents: list[Document],
) -> str:
    return "\n\n".join(
        (
            f"Source: {document.metadata['source']}\n"
            f"Content: {document.page_content}"
        )
        for document in documents
    )


selected_context = format_documents(
    selected_documents
)


# =========================================================
# 9. CREATE THE FINAL LANGCHAIN PROMPT
# =========================================================

prompt = ChatPromptTemplate.from_messages(
    [
        (
            "system",
            """
You are an enterprise policy assistant.

Rules:
1. Answer using only the supplied evidence.
2. Preserve all numbers and approval conditions.
3. Do not add unsupported information.
4. Mention the supporting source.
5. If the answer is unavailable, state that the required
   information was not found.
""",
        ),
        (
            "human",
            """
Conversation summary:
The user is reviewing the business-travel policy.

Retrieved evidence:
{context}

Current question:
{question}
""",
        ),
    ]
)


# =========================================================
# 10. CREATE AND EXECUTE THE GENERATION CHAIN
# =========================================================

rag_chain = (
    prompt
    | llm
    | StrOutputParser()
)

answer = rag_chain.invoke(
    {
        "context": selected_context,
        "question": question,
    }
)


# =========================================================
# 11. DISPLAY TOKEN-BUDGET INFORMATION
# =========================================================

print("TOKEN BUDGET")
print("=" * 70)
print("Model context limit:", MODEL_CONTEXT_LIMIT)
print("Reserved output tokens:", RESERVED_OUTPUT_TOKENS)
print("Safety buffer:", SAFETY_BUFFER)
print("Trimmed history tokens:", history_tokens)
print("Retrieval context budget:", retrieval_budget)

print("\nSELECTED DOCUMENTS")
print("=" * 70)

for document in selected_documents:
    print(
        document.metadata["source"],
        "| Score:",
        document.metadata["relevance_score"],
        "| Tokens:",
        count_text_tokens(document.page_content),
    )

print("\nFINAL ANSWER")
print("=" * 70)
print(answer)

vii) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a token limit, and why is it important in RAG?

A token limit is the maximum number of tokens an LLM can process in a single request. The limit includes system instructions, conversation history, retrieved documents, the current user question, tool outputs, formatting overhead, and the model’s generated response.

It is important in RAG because retrieval may return more document content than the model can process safely. The application must reserve output capacity and allocate the remaining budget intelligently, otherwise the request may fail, the context may be truncated, or important evidence may be excluded.

Q2. What happens when a prompt exceeds the model’s context window?

Depending on the model provider and application, the request may be rejected with a context-length error, content may be truncated, or insufficient capacity may remain for the generated answer. Silent truncation is especially risky because an important instruction or supporting document may be removed without the user realizing it.

A reliable system should count tokens before invoking the model and use a safety buffer. If the prompt is too large, it should remove low-priority chunks, trim old conversation turns, compress retrieved passages, or summarize historical information while preserving critical facts.

Q3. How should the token budget be divided in a RAG application?

The application should first reserve enough tokens for the expected answer and a safety margin. From the remaining input budget, it must allocate space for system instructions, the current user query, necessary conversation history, retrieved evidence, tool results, and prompt-formatting overhead.

The allocation should be dynamic rather than fixed for every request. A standalone question may provide most of the budget to retrieved evidence, while a conversational question may need more space for history. Long retrieved documents may require Re-Ranking or Context Compression before being inserted.

Q4. Is using a larger context-window model enough to solve token-limit problems?

A larger context window reduces the probability of immediate overflow, but it does not remove the need for context management. Sending excessive information still increases input-token cost, latency, irrelevant evidence, and the difficulty of identifying which parts of the prompt are important.

A well-designed RAG system should retrieve selectively and prioritize useful evidence even when a large context window is available. Larger windows should be treated as additional capacity, not as permission to send the complete knowledge base or unlimited conversation history.


Topic 2: Lost-in-the-Middle

i) Definition

* Lost-in-the-Middle is a long-context problem where an LLM uses information less reliably when it appears near the middle of a lengthy prompt. Research 
  observed that models often perform better when relevant evidence appears near the beginning or end of the context, even when the model technically 
  supports the complete input length.

* The problem produces a pattern similar to a U-shaped performance curve. Information at the beginning benefits from primacy, information near the end 
  benefits from recency, and evidence placed between many other passages may receive less effective attention during generation.

* Lost-in-the-Middle does not necessarily mean that the prompt exceeded the model’s token limit. The complete context may fit technically, but the model may 
  still fail to identify or correctly use the important evidence because it is surrounded by long, repetitive, irrelevant, or competing information.

* In RAG, this problem can occur when many retrieved chunks are concatenated into a single prompt without careful ordering. The correct document may be 
  retrieved successfully but placed in the middle, causing the generator to overlook it and produce an incomplete or unsupported answer.

ii) Purpose of Understanding Lost-in-the-Middle

* Understanding this problem helps distinguish context overflow from context-utilization failure. Context overflow occurs when the prompt exceeds the 
  supported token limit, while Lost-in-the-Middle occurs when the prompt fits but the model does not use centrally positioned evidence reliably.

* It explains why simply increasing Top-K or selecting a model with a larger context window may not improve RAG accuracy. Retrieving more documents may 
  increase recall, but the added distractors can bury the strongest evidence and make it harder for the model to identify the answer-bearing passage.

* The concept also emphasizes the importance of Re-Ranking, Context Compression, document ordering, and evidence prioritization. A RAG system should 
  organize context intentionally instead of assuming that the LLM will treat every retrieved token with equal effectiveness.

* Lost-in-the-Middle is particularly important for multi-document question answering, long policy analysis, legal-document review, research assistants, and 
  lengthy conversation histories. These applications often include many passages, while only a small portion contains the evidence required for the current 
  question.

iii) Architectural Diagram 

                    LOST-IN-THE-MIDDLE PROBLEM
                    ==========================

                         User Question
                               |
                               v
                         RAG Retriever
                               |
                               v
                  Multiple Retrieved Documents
                               |
                               v
               Documents Added in Retrieval Order

       Beginning              Middle                 End
   +----------------+  +--------------------+  +----------------+
   | Relevant Doc A |  | Distractor Doc B   |  | Recent Doc F   |
   | Strongly used  |  | Relevant Doc C     |  | Strongly used  |
   |                |  | Distractor D and E |  |                |
   +----------------+  +--------------------+  +----------------+
                               |
                               v
                   Relevant Doc C Is Overlooked
                               |
                               v
                   Incomplete or Incorrect Answer


                       MITIGATION WORKFLOW

                     Retrieved Candidates
                               |
                               v
                 Re-Rank and Remove Distractors
                               |
                               v
                Prioritize Strongest Evidence
                               |
                               v
           Place Important Evidence at Prompt Boundaries
                               |
                               v
                  Add Evidence-Focused Instructions
                               |
                               v
                         LLM Generation

iv) Components

1. Candidate Retriever and Re-Ranker
The candidate retriever searches broadly to ensure that the correct evidence enters the candidate set. A Re-Ranker then performs a more accurate query-document comparison and identifies which chunks directly answer the question instead of relying only on initial vector-similarity scores.

2. Context Filter and Compressor
The context filter removes documents that are irrelevant, duplicated, outdated, or below a relevance threshold. A compressor can shorten remaining documents by preserving only query-relevant sentences, reducing the chance that useful evidence becomes hidden among unnecessary content.

3. Position-Aware Context Organizer
The context organizer arranges evidence according to importance and position rather than preserving arbitrary retrieval order. Important passages can be distributed near the beginning and end of the prompt, where long-context models have often demonstrated stronger information utilization.

4. Prompt Builder and Evaluator
The prompt builder clearly separates source documents, labels their metadata, and instructs the LLM to answer only from supplied evidence. The evaluator tests whether answer quality changes when the same relevant passage is moved to the beginning, middle, and end of the context.

5. Mitigation Strategies
Re-Ranking should be applied before document ordering so that the application knows which chunks contain the strongest evidence. The highest-scoring document should not be buried among lower-quality results merely because it was returned later by the initial vector search.

Context Compression and deduplication reduce the length and noise of the prompt. If two chunks repeat the same information, only the clearer or more authoritative version may be needed. Removing redundant information leaves fewer distractors around the answer-bearing passage.

Position-aware reordering distributes highly relevant documents near the prompt boundaries. LangChain provides LongContextReorder, a document transformer designed to reorder long contexts based on the observation that models may use centrally positioned evidence less effectively.

Position-based evaluation should test the actual model and application. The same supporting passage can be placed at different positions while keeping all other prompt content unchanged. Measuring answer accuracy across these variations reveals whether the selected model is sensitive to evidence position.

v) Code Example:

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_core.prompts import ChatPromptTemplate
from langchain_core.output_parsers import StrOutputParser

from langchain_community.vectorstores import FAISS
from langchain_community.document_transformers import (
    LongContextReorder,
)

from langchain_huggingface import HuggingFaceEmbeddings
from langchain_openai import ChatOpenAI


# =========================================================
# 1. CREATE THE KNOWLEDGE-BASE DOCUMENTS
# =========================================================

documents = [
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees must obtain reporting-manager approval "
            "before booking tickets for business travel."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-001",
            "source": "travel_approval_policy.txt",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Business-travel meal expenses are reimbursed according "
            "to the limit defined for the destination city."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-002",
            "source": "travel_meal_policy.txt",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees travelling for approved business purposes "
            "can claim hotel accommodation for a maximum of seven "
            "consecutive days. Any extension requires written "
            "approval from the project manager."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-003",
            "source": "travel_accommodation_policy.txt",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Valid receipts must be submitted for airport transfers "
            "and local transportation used for business meetings."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-004",
            "source": "transportation_policy.txt",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees must submit completed travel-expense reports "
            "within fifteen working days after the journey."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-005",
            "source": "expense_submission_policy.txt",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "International travel insurance must be arranged before "
            "an employee begins an approved overseas assignment."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-006",
            "source": "travel_insurance_policy.txt",
        },
    ),
]


# =========================================================
# 2. CREATE THE VECTOR STORE AND RETRIEVER
# =========================================================

embedding_model = HuggingFaceEmbeddings(
    model_name="sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2",
    encode_kwargs={
        "normalize_embeddings": True,
    },
)

vector_store = FAISS.from_documents(
    documents=documents,
    embedding=embedding_model,
)

retriever = vector_store.as_retriever(
    search_type="similarity",
    search_kwargs={
        "k": 6,
    },
)


# =========================================================
# 3. RETRIEVE THE CANDIDATE DOCUMENTS
# =========================================================

user_question = (
    "How many hotel-accommodation days can an employee claim, "
    "and what approval is required for an extension?"
)

retrieved_documents = retriever.invoke(
    user_question
)


# =========================================================
# 4. APPLY LONG-CONTEXT REORDERING
# =========================================================

reordering_transformer = LongContextReorder()

reordered_documents = (
    reordering_transformer.transform_documents(
        retrieved_documents
    )
)


# =========================================================
# 5. FORMAT THE ORIGINAL AND REORDERED CONTEXT
# =========================================================

def format_documents(documents_to_format):
    context_blocks = []

    for position, document in enumerate(
        documents_to_format,
        start=1,
    ):
        context_blocks.append(
            f"Document {position}\n"
            f"Source: {document.metadata['source']}\n"
            f"Content: {document.page_content}"
        )

    return "\n\n".join(context_blocks)


original_context = format_documents(
    retrieved_documents
)

reordered_context = format_documents(
    reordered_documents
)


# =========================================================
# 6. CREATE THE EVIDENCE-FOCUSED PROMPT
# =========================================================

prompt = ChatPromptTemplate.from_messages(
    [
        (
            "system",
            """
You are an enterprise policy assistant.

Answer using only the supplied evidence.

Rules:
1. Examine every supplied document before answering.
2. Preserve all numbers and approval conditions.
3. Do not add unsupported information.
4. Mention the source supporting the answer.
5. If the answer is unavailable, state that the required
   information was not found.
""",
        ),
        (
            "human",
            """
Question:
{question}

Evidence:
{context}
""",
        ),
    ]
)


# =========================================================
# 7. CREATE THE GENERATION CHAIN
# =========================================================

generation_model = ChatOpenAI(
    model="gpt-4.1-mini",
    temperature=0,
)

rag_chain = (
    prompt
    | generation_model
    | StrOutputParser()
)


# =========================================================
# 8. GENERATE FROM THE REORDERED CONTEXT
# =========================================================

answer = rag_chain.invoke(
    {
        "question": user_question,
        "context": reordered_context,
    }
)


# =========================================================
# 9. DISPLAY DOCUMENT POSITIONS
# =========================================================

print("ORIGINAL RETRIEVAL ORDER")
print("=" * 70)

for position, document in enumerate(
    retrieved_documents,
    start=1,
):
    print(
        position,
        document.metadata["source"],
    )


print("\nPOSITION-AWARE REORDERED CONTEXT")
print("=" * 70)

for position, document in enumerate(
    reordered_documents,
    start=1,
):
    print(
        position,
        document.metadata["source"],
    )


print("\nFINAL ANSWER")
print("=" * 70)
print(answer)

vi) Interview Question and Answers

Q1. What is the Lost-in-the-Middle problem?

Lost-in-the-Middle is a long-context problem in which an LLM may use information less effectively when the relevant evidence appears near the middle of a lengthy prompt. Research evaluating multi-document question answering and key-value retrieval observed that model performance was often stronger when relevant information appeared near the beginning or end of the context.

The issue may occur even when the complete prompt fits within the model’s supported context window. It is therefore a context-utilization problem rather than only a token-overflow problem. In RAG, the correct chunk may be retrieved but still overlooked because it is buried among many distractors.

Q2. Why does increasing Top-K sometimes reduce RAG accuracy?

Increasing Top-K can improve retrieval recall because more candidate documents are returned. However, it may also introduce irrelevant, redundant, outdated, or conflicting passages into the prompt. The answer-bearing evidence can become located among distractors and may receive less effective attention from the model.

A better strategy is to retrieve broadly for candidate recall, then apply metadata filtering, Re-Ranking, deduplication, and Context Compression. Only a small set of high-quality evidence should be arranged and supplied to the generator.

Q3. How can Lost-in-the-Middle be reduced in a RAG system?

The system should first improve evidence quality through Re-Ranking, relevance filtering, deduplication, and Context Compression. These techniques reduce the number of distractors and prevent the relevant passage from being surrounded by unnecessary content.

After selecting the final evidence, position-aware ordering can place important passages near the prompt’s beginning or end. The prompt should also label individual sources and instruct the model to examine all evidence. These methods reduce the risk but do not guarantee that every model will use long context perfectly.

Q4. How would you evaluate Lost-in-the-Middle?

Create evaluation prompts containing the same question, the same relevant passage, and the same distractor documents. Move only the relevant passage between the beginning, middle, and end while keeping the remaining prompt content as consistent as possible.

Run each arrangement several times and compare answer accuracy, faithfulness, citation correctness, and evidence extraction. A significant reduction in performance when the passage is in the middle indicates position sensitivity. This evaluation should be repeated for the actual model, prompt, and document distribution used by the application.

Topic 3: Context Selection

i) Definition

* Context Selection is the process of choosing the most useful information to include in an LLM’s prompt for a particular request. The available information may include retrieved document chunks, conversation history, tool outputs, user information, system instructions, and intermediate results, but only the information needed for the current task should enter the model’s context window.

* In a RAG system, retrieval and Context Selection are related but not identical. Retrieval produces a candidate collection of potentially relevant documents, while Context Selection decides which candidates should actually be given to the generator. The selection step can consider relevance, authority, freshness, permissions, redundancy, evidence coverage, and token size.

* Context Selection is a query-dependent process because the same knowledge base can produce different context for different questions. A travel-policy document may contain accommodation, meals, transportation, and approval rules, but a hotel-related question should receive only the accommodation rules and necessary conditions.

* The objective is not to include the largest possible amount of information. It is to create a context containing enough reliable evidence to answer the question accurately without exceeding the token budget or distracting the model with irrelevant, repetitive, outdated, or contradictory content.

ii) Purpose

* The primary purpose of Context Selection is to ensure that the LLM receives the evidence most relevant to the user’s actual information need. Even when the retriever returns the correct document, the final answer may be poor if that document is surrounded by irrelevant chunks or if a more authoritative source is excluded from the prompt.

* Context Selection also helps manage the model’s finite context window. An LLM cannot ingest an entire enterprise knowledge base in one request, so the application must retrieve and select a smaller collection of relevant information at query time. LangChain identifies finite context as one of the reasons retrieval is required in LLM applications.

* Another purpose is to reduce redundancy within the selected context. Similarity search may return multiple overlapping chunks containing nearly identical information, which wastes tokens without increasing evidence coverage. Selection strategies such as Maximal Marginal Relevance balance query relevance with diversity among chosen documents.

* Context Selection improves answer faithfulness by preferring authoritative, current, and permitted evidence. It can remove archived policies, untrusted content, low-scoring passages, and documents the user is not authorized to access before any information is passed to the prompt builder.

iii) Architectural Diagram

                      CONTEXT SELECTION WORKFLOW
                      ==========================

                           User Question
                                 |
                                 v
                        Candidate Retrieval
              Vector Search | BM25 | Hybrid Search
                                 |
                                 v
                    Broad Candidate Collection
                          Top 10-50 Chunks
                                 |
                                 v
                       Eligibility Filtering
             Permissions | Status | Version | Language
                                 |
                                 v
                       Relevance Re-Ranking
                    Query-Document Comparison
                                 |
                                 v
                 Redundancy and Diversity Analysis
                      Deduplication | MMR
                                 |
                                 v
                       Token-Budget Selection
               Relevance | Coverage | Chunk Size
                                 |
                                 v
                      Final Evidence Context
                                 |
                                 v
                          Prompt Builder
          Instructions + Question + Evidence + Citations
                                 |
                                 v
                           LLM Generator
                                 |
                                 v
                    Grounded Answer with Sources


iv) Core Components

1. Candidate Retriever
The candidate retriever searches the knowledge base and returns a broad set of potentially useful documents. Its purpose is to achieve sufficient recall rather than immediately producing the final context, because later components will remove irrelevant and redundant candidates.

2. Eligibility and Relevance Evaluator
The eligibility evaluator applies metadata, freshness, authority, and authorization requirements before selection. A Re-Ranker then compares each eligible candidate with the query and assigns a more accurate relevance score, helping distinguish directly useful evidence from passages that are merely topically similar.

3. Diversity and Coverage Selector
The diversity selector prevents the final context from containing several chunks that repeat the same information. MMR balances similarity to the query with difference from already selected documents, helping the final evidence cover multiple useful aspects of the request rather than one repeated point.

4. Token-Budget and Context Formatter
The token-budget component calculates how much space remains after accounting for system instructions, conversation history, the current query, and reserved output capacity. The formatter then labels each selected document with its source and arranges the evidence in a clear structure for the LLM.

5. Important Context-Selection Criteria

Relevance
Relevance determines how directly a candidate contributes to answering the user’s question. A document can belong to the correct general topic but still offer no useful evidence, so Re-Ranking often provides a better selection signal than depending only on the initial embedding-similarity score.

Authority and Freshness
When several documents contain similar information, the system should prefer approved, current, and authoritative sources. A current policy should normally be selected over an archived version, while an official technical manual should be prioritized over an unverified note or user-generated comment.

Diversity and Evidence Coverage
The selected chunks should contribute complementary evidence rather than repeat the same statement. For a question asking about both reimbursement limits and approval requirements, the context must cover both parts even if the highest similarity scores are dominated by several chunks discussing only reimbursement.

Token Efficiency
Every selected chunk consumes part of the model’s context window and may increase latency and cost. The selector should therefore consider the information value of a chunk relative to its size, sometimes preferring a concise passage containing the exact answer over a longer but slightly more similar document.

6. Context Selection vs Related Techniques

Context Selection vs Retrieval
Retrieval searches the knowledge base and returns a candidate collection, while Context Selection chooses the subset that will enter the prompt. Retrieval focuses on finding possible evidence, whereas selection focuses on creating the most useful final evidence set within business and token constraints.

Context Selection vs Re-Ranking
Re-Ranking assigns improved relevance scores and changes the order of retrieved documents. Context Selection uses these scores along with permissions, source authority, diversity, evidence coverage, freshness, and token size to decide which documents should actually be included.

Context Selection vs Context Compression
Context Selection chooses which documents or chunks should be included in the final context. Context Compression reduces the content inside selected chunks by extracting relevant sentences or summarizing longer passages, so both techniques can be used together.

Context Selection vs Context Prioritization
Context Selection decides what information enters the prompt, while Context Prioritization decides how selected information should be ordered or emphasized. Selection removes unnecessary content, whereas prioritization gives greater attention to the most important remaining evidence.

v) Code Example

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_core.prompts import ChatPromptTemplate
from langchain_core.output_parsers import StrOutputParser

from langchain_community.vectorstores import FAISS
from langchain_huggingface import HuggingFaceEmbeddings
from langchain_openai import ChatOpenAI


# =========================================================
# 1. CREATE A KNOWLEDGE BASE WITH METADATA
# =========================================================

documents = [
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees travelling for approved business purposes "
            "can claim hotel accommodation for a maximum of seven "
            "consecutive days."
        ),
        metadata={
            "source": "travel_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "accommodation",
            "status": "active",
            "authority": 3,
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Any extension beyond seven hotel-accommodation days "
            "requires written approval from the project manager."
        ),
        metadata={
            "source": "travel_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "accommodation approval",
            "status": "active",
            "authority": 3,
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "The archived travel policy permitted hotel expenses "
            "for five consecutive days."
        ),
        metadata={
            "source": "travel_policy_2024.txt",
            "topic": "accommodation",
            "status": "archived",
            "authority": 1,
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Meal expenses are reimbursed according to the limit "
            "assigned to the destination city."
        ),
        metadata={
            "source": "meal_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "meals",
            "status": "active",
            "authority": 3,
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Business travel must receive manager approval before "
            "tickets and accommodation are booked."
        ),
        metadata={
            "source": "travel_approval_2026.txt",
            "topic": "booking approval",
            "status": "active",
            "authority": 3,
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees must submit business-travel expense reports "
            "within fifteen working days after the journey."
        ),
        metadata={
            "source": "expense_submission_2026.txt",
            "topic": "expense submission",
            "status": "active",
            "authority": 3,
        },
    ),
]


# =========================================================
# 2. CREATE THE EMBEDDING MODEL AND VECTOR STORE
# =========================================================

embedding_model = HuggingFaceEmbeddings(
    model_name="sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2",
    encode_kwargs={
        "normalize_embeddings": True,
    },
)

vector_store = FAISS.from_documents(
    documents=documents,
    embedding=embedding_model,
)


# =========================================================
# 3. CREATE AN MMR RETRIEVER
# =========================================================

# fetch_k controls the initial candidate pool.
# k controls how many diverse candidates MMR returns.
# lambda_mult balances query relevance and diversity.
retriever = vector_store.as_retriever(
    search_type="mmr",
    search_kwargs={
        "fetch_k": 6,
        "k": 5,
        "lambda_mult": 0.7,
    },
)


# =========================================================
# 4. RETRIEVE A DIVERSE CANDIDATE SET
# =========================================================

query = "What are the hotel accommodation rules for business travel?"

retrieved_docs = retriever.invoke(query)

for i, doc in enumerate(retrieved_docs, start=1):
    print(f"\nDocument {i}")
    print("Content:", doc.page_content)
    print("Metadata:", doc.metadata)


Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Context Selection in a RAG system?

Context Selection is the process of choosing which retrieved documents, conversation messages, tool results, and other information should be included in the prompt sent to the LLM. The retriever may return a broad candidate set, but not every candidate is relevant, current, authoritative, permitted, or useful.

The selector evaluates candidates using relevance, source authority, metadata, freshness, permissions, redundancy, evidence coverage, and token size. Its goal is to construct the smallest reliable evidence set that sufficiently supports the answer.

Q2. What is the difference between retrieval and Context Selection?

Retrieval searches the knowledge base and returns documents that may be relevant to the query. It usually focuses on candidate recall, meaning that the required evidence should appear somewhere within the result collection.

Context Selection operates after retrieval and decides which candidates should enter the LLM’s prompt. It focuses on creating a precise, diverse, authoritative, and token-efficient final context. Retrieval finds possible evidence, while Context Selection chooses usable evidence.

Q3. How does MMR help with Context Selection?

Maximal Marginal Relevance selects documents by considering both their relevance to the query and their similarity to documents already selected. A normal similarity search may return several overlapping chunks that repeat the same information, consuming context tokens but adding little new evidence.

MMR penalizes redundancy and promotes candidates that contribute different information while remaining relevant. Its lambda parameter controls the balance, with higher values emphasizing relevance and lower values emphasizing diversity.

Q4. What factors should be considered when selecting context?

The system should consider query relevance, document authority, freshness, policy version, metadata conditions, user permissions, evidence coverage, redundancy, contradiction, chunk size, and the available token budget. Relying only on vector-similarity scores can allow outdated or repetitive documents to enter the prompt.

Selection should also cover every part of a compound question. If the user asks about both a reimbursement limit and an approval condition, the final context must include evidence for both aspects rather than selecting several highly similar passages covering only one.

Topic 4: Context Prioritization

i) Definition
* Context Prioritization is the process of assigning importance to selected information and deciding the order in which it should appear inside the LLM 
  prompt. After Context Selection determines what information enters the prompt, prioritization decides which evidence should receive the most prominent 
  position based on relevance, authority, freshness, and importance.

* Context Prioritization is necessary because an LLM may not use every part of a long prompt with equal effectiveness. The most important system 
  instructions, answer-bearing evidence, and current user query should therefore receive stronger placement than supplementary explanations, duplicated 
  passages, or low-confidence supporting information.

* In a RAG pipeline, priority can be computed from several signals, including retrieval similarity, Re-Ranking score, document authority, metadata, 
  publication date, evidence coverage, and user permissions. The final priority should not depend only on the initial vector score because semantic 
  similarity does not always represent actual answer relevance.

* Prioritization can influence both inclusion and placement. High-priority evidence is included first and placed strategically, medium-priority evidence 
  provides supporting details, and low-priority content is removed when the token budget becomes limited. This helps preserve essential evidence when the 
  prompt must be shortened.

ii) Purpose
* The primary purpose of Context Prioritization is to ensure that the LLM notices and uses the strongest available evidence. Even after irrelevant documents are removed, the remaining context may contain primary rules, exceptions, examples, definitions, and background information that do not have equal importance for answering the question.

* Prioritization helps reduce the Lost-in-the-Middle problem by preventing critical evidence from being buried among supporting passages. Research has shown that model performance can vary according to evidence position, with relevant information near the beginning or end often used more reliably than information placed in the middle of a long context.

* It also supports token-budget management because low-priority information can be discarded first when the final prompt becomes too large. Important system instructions, security requirements, current user needs, answer-bearing evidence, and source information should survive before optional examples or repeated explanations.

* Context Prioritization is particularly useful for compound questions and multi-source answers. If a user asks about both the allowed reimbursement amount and its approval conditions, the final context must prioritize evidence for both parts instead of filling the prompt with several highly similar chunks addressing only one part. 


iii) Architectural Diagram
                  CONTEXT PRIORITIZATION WORKFLOW
                  ===============================

                        User Question
                              |
                              v
                    Retrieve Candidate Chunks
                              |
                              v
                       Context Selection
          Filter Invalid, Irrelevant and Duplicate Content
                              |
                              v
                    Calculate Priority Signals
       Relevance | Authority | Freshness | Coverage | Risk
                              |
                              v
                  Assign Final Priority Scores
                              |
                +-------------+-------------+
                |             |             |
                v             v             v
           High Priority  Medium Priority  Low Priority
          Direct Answer   Supporting Info  Background
                |             |             |
                +-------------+-------------+
                              |
                              v
                    Apply the Token Budget
              Keep High Priority Before Lower Priority
                              |
                              v
                  Position-Aware Context Ordering
             Most Important Evidence at Strong Positions
                              |
                              v
                         Prompt Builder
                              |
                              v
                         LLM Generator
                              |
                              v
                          Grounded

iv) Components

Core Components

1. Priority-Signal Extractor
The priority-signal extractor gathers relevance scores, Re-Ranking scores, metadata, dates, authority levels, document status, and source confidence. It can also identify whether a chunk contains a direct answer, an exception, supporting evidence, or only general background information.

2. Priority-Scoring Engine
The scoring engine combines individual signals into one priority value. A simple implementation may use a weighted formula, while a more advanced application may use a trained ranking model or an LLM-based grader to classify evidence as essential, supportive, optional, or irrelevant.

3. Coverage and Diversity Controller
The coverage controller ensures that the prioritized context answers every part of the question. It also reduces repeated content by penalizing chunks that are highly similar to already selected evidence; MMR follows this principle by balancing query relevance with diversity among selected documents.

4. Budget and Position Manager
The budget manager adds evidence according to priority until the available context budget is reached. The position manager then orders high-priority evidence strategically, preserves source labels, and places low-priority supporting information only when sufficient token capacity remains.

5. Context Priority Levels

High-Priority Context
High-priority context directly answers the user’s question or contains a critical rule, numerical value, exception, security instruction, or required business condition. It should be retained whenever possible and placed where the model can use it effectively.

Medium-Priority Context
Medium-priority context supports or clarifies the primary evidence but may not independently answer the question. It can include related definitions, implementation details, examples, secondary sources, or information that explains why the primary rule applies.

Low-Priority Context
Low-priority context contains general background, repeated evidence, weakly related information, or optional examples. It should be the first category removed when the prompt exceeds its token budget or when it distracts from the direct answer.

Mandatory Context
Mandatory context includes system instructions, safety rules, access restrictions, output requirements, and current user intent. These elements should not compete with retrieved evidence through the same scoring formula because they define how the model must process all other information.

6. Context Selection vs Context Prioritization

Context Selection decides what information is allowed to enter the final prompt. It evaluates relevance, permissions, freshness, authority, redundancy, evidence coverage, and token size to remove unsuitable candidates from the retrieval results.

Context Prioritization decides which selected information is most important and how it should be ordered. It determines which evidence should be retained first when the budget is limited and which passages should receive strategically useful prompt positions.

Selection normally occurs before prioritization because it is inefficient to score and order documents that should have been excluded. For example, an archived policy should first be removed through selection rather than merely assigned a lower priority than the current policy.

Both techniques work with Context Compression and Re-Ranking. Re-Ranking improves relevance order, selection determines eligibility, compression removes unnecessary text, and prioritization organizes the remaining evidence for final prompt construction.

v) Code Example

from langchain_core.documents import Document
from langchain_core.prompts import ChatPromptTemplate
from langchain_core.output_parsers import StrOutputParser

from langchain_community.vectorstores import FAISS
from langchain_huggingface import HuggingFaceEmbeddings
from langchain_openai import ChatOpenAI


# =========================================================
# 1. CREATE DOCUMENTS WITH PRIORITY METADATA
# =========================================================

documents = [
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees travelling for approved business purposes "
            "can claim hotel accommodation for a maximum of seven "
            "consecutive days."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-001",
            "source": "travel_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "accommodation limit",
            "authority": 1.0,
            "freshness": 1.0,
            "status": "active",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Any extension beyond seven hotel-accommodation days "
            "requires written approval from the project manager."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-002",
            "source": "travel_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "extension approval",
            "authority": 1.0,
            "freshness": 1.0,
            "status": "active",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Under the archived travel policy, employees could "
            "claim hotel accommodation for five consecutive days."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-003",
            "source": "travel_policy_2024.txt",
            "topic": "accommodation limit",
            "authority": 0.5,
            "freshness": 0.3,
            "status": "archived",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Business-travel meal expenses are reimbursed according "
            "to the limit assigned to the destination city."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-004",
            "source": "meal_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "meal expenses",
            "authority": 1.0,
            "freshness": 1.0,
            "status": "active",
        },
    ),
    Document(
        page_content=(
            "Employees must submit travel-expense reports within "
            "fifteen working days after completing the journey."
        ),
        metadata={
            "id": "DOC-005",
            "source": "expense_policy_2026.txt",
            "topic": "expense submission",
            "authority": 1.0,
            "freshness": 1.0,
            "status": "active",
        },
    ),
]


# =========================================================
# 2. CREATE THE VECTOR STORE
# =========================================================

embedding_model = HuggingFaceEmbeddings(
    model_name="sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2",
    encode_kwargs={
        "normalize_embeddings": True,
    },
)

vector_store = FAISS.from_documents(
    documents=documents,
    embedding=embedding_model,
)


# =========================================================
# 3. RETRIEVE CANDIDATES WITH RELEVANCE SCORES
# =========================================================

question = (
    "How many hotel-accommodation days can an employee claim, "
    "and what approval is required for an extension?"
)

candidate_results = (
    vector_store.similarity_search_with_relevance_scores(
        query=question,
        k=5,
    )
)


# =========================================================
# 4. CALCULATE PRIORITY SCORES
# =========================================================

def calculate_priority(
    document: Document,
    relevance_score: float,
    required_topics: set[str],
) -> float:
    """
    Weighted educational formula:

    55% retrieval relevance
    20% source authority
    15% freshness
    10% evidence coverage
    """

    authority = document.metadata.get("authority", 0.0)
    freshness = document.metadata.get("freshness", 0.0)

    topic = document.metadata.get("topic", "")
    coverage = 1.0 if topic in required_topics else 0.0

    priority_score = (
        0.55 * relevance_score
        + 0.20 * authority
        + 0.15 * freshness
        + 0.10 * coverage
    )

    return priority_score


required_topics = {
    "accommodation limit",
    "extension approval",
}

prioritized_candidates = []

for document, relevance_score in candidate_results:

    # Archived documents are ineligible, not merely lower priority.
    if document.metadata.get("status") != "active":
        continue

    priority_score = calculate_priority(
        document=document,
        relevance_score=relevance_score,
        required_topics=required_topics,
    )

    document.metadata["relevance_score"] = relevance_score
    document.metadata["priority_score"] = priority_score

    prioritized_candidates.append(document)

prioritized_candidates.sort(
    key=lambda document: document.metadata["priority_score"],
    reverse=True,
)


# =========================================================
# 5. SELECT DOCUMENTS WITHIN THE TOKEN BUDGET
# =========================================================

llm = ChatOpenAI(
    model="gpt-4.1-mini",
    temperature=0,
)


def count_tokens(document: Document) -> int:
    return llm.get_num_tokens(document.page_content)


def select_within_budget(
    prioritized_documents: list[Document],
    token_budget: int,
) -> listselected_documents = []
    selected_topics = set()
    used_tokens = 0

    # First pass: include high-priority evidence that adds
    # coverage for a required answer aspect.
    for document in prioritized_documents:
        topic = document.metadata.get("topic")
        tokens = count_tokens(document)

        if topic not in required_topics:
            continue

        if topic in selected_topics:
            continue

        if used_tokens + tokens > token_budget:
            continue

        selected_documents.append(document)
        selected_topics.add(topic)
        used_tokens += tokens

    # Second pass: add useful supporting context if budget remains.
    for document in prioritized_documents:
        if document in selected_documents:
            continue

        tokens = count_tokens(document)

        if used_tokens + tokens > token_budget:
            continue

        selected_documents.append(document)
        used_tokens += tokens

    return selected_documents


selected_documents = select_within_budget(
    prioritized_documents=prioritized_candidates,
    token_budget=300,
)


# =========================================================
# 6. APPLY POSITION-AWARE ORDERING
# =========================================================

def arrange_by_priority(
    selected: list[Document],
) -> list"""
    For a small final context, keep the strongest evidence first.

    Large contexts may instead distribute highly relevant evidence
    near the beginning and end.
    """

    return sorted(
        selected,
        key=lambda document: document.metadata["priority_score"],
        reverse=True,
    )


ordered_documents = arrange_by_priority(
    selected_documents
)


# =========================================================
# 7. FORMAT THE PRIORITIZED CONTEXT
# =========================================================

def format_context(
    ordered: list[Document],
) -> str:

    context_blocks = []

    for position, document in enumerate(ordered, start=1):
        context_blocks.append(
            f"Priority position: {position}\n"
            f"Source: {document.metadata['source']}\n"
            f"Topic: {document.metadata['topic']}\n"
            f"Evidence: {document.page_content}"
