    "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees per financial "
    "year for approved professional certifications. Manager "
    "approval is required before registration."
)

retrieved_contexts = [
    (
        "Employees may claim reimbursement of up to 25,000 "
        "rupees per financial year for approved professional "
        "certifications."
    ),
    (
        "Manager approval is required before registering for "
        "an approved professional certification."
    ),
    (
        "Employees receive twelve paid sick-leave days during "
        "each calendar year."
    ),
]


# =========================================================
# 2. CREATE THE OPENAI CLIENT
# =========================================================

openai_client = AsyncOpenAI()


# =========================================================
# 3. CREATE THE RAGAS EVALUATOR LLM
# =========================================================

evaluator_llm = llm_factory(
    model="gpt-4o-mini",
    client=openai_client,
)


# =========================================================
# 4. CREATE THE RAGAS EMBEDDING MODEL
# =========================================================

evaluator_embeddings = embedding_factory(
    provider="openai",
    model="text-embedding-3-small",
    client=openai_client,
)


# =========================================================
# 5. CREATE THE FOUR CORE METRIC SCORERS
# =========================================================

context_precision_metric = ContextPrecision(
    llm=evaluator_llm
)

context_recall_metric = ContextRecall(
    llm=evaluator_llm
)

faithfulness_metric = Faithfulness(
    llm=evaluator_llm
)

answer_relevancy_metric = AnswerRelevancy(
    llm=evaluator_llm,
    embeddings=evaluator_embeddings,
)


# =========================================================
# 6. DEFINE THE COMPLETE EVALUATION FUNCTION
# =========================================================

async def evaluate_rag_interaction() -> None:
    """
    Evaluate one RAG interaction with four core metrics.
    """

    context_precision_result = (
        await context_precision_metric.ascore(
            user_input=user_question,
            reference=reference_answer,
            retrieved_contexts=retrieved_contexts,
        )
    )

    context_recall_result = (
        await context_recall_metric.ascore(
            user_input=user_question,
            reference=reference_answer,
            retrieved_contexts=retrieved_contexts,
        )
    )

    faithfulness_result = (
        await faithfulness_metric.ascore(
            user_input=user_question,
            response=generated_response,
            retrieved_contexts=retrieved_contexts,
        )
    )

    answer_relevancy_result = (
        await answer_relevancy_metric.ascore(
            user_input=user_question,
            response=generated_response,
        )
    )

    scores = {
        "Context Precision": (
            context_precision_result.value
        ),
        "Context Recall": (
            context_recall_result.value
        ),
        "Faithfulness": (
            faithfulness_result.value
        ),
        "Answer Relevancy": (
            answer_relevancy_result.value
        ),
    }

    print("=" * 70)
    print("RAG EVALUATION SAMPLE")
    print("=" * 70)

    print("\nUser Question:")
    print(user_question)

    print("\nGenerated Response:")
    print(generated_response)

    print("\nReference Answer:")
    print(reference_answer)

    print("\nRetrieved Contexts:")

    for position, context in enumerate(
        retrieved_contexts,
        start=1,
    ):
        print(f"\nContext {position}:")
        print(context)

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("RAGAS SCORES")
    print("=" * 70)

    for metric_name, score in scores.items():
        print(
            f"{metric_name}: "
            f"{round(float(score), 4)}"
        )

    overall_average = (
        sum(float(score) for score in scores.values())
        / len(scores)
    )

    print("\nSimple Average:")
    print(
        round(overall_average, 4)
    )


# =========================================================
# 7. RUN THE PROGRAM
# =========================================================

if __name__ == "__main__":
    asyncio.run(
        evaluate_rag_interaction()
    )

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is RAGAS?

RAGAS is an evaluation framework for RAG and LLM applications. It provides metrics for retrieval quality, generation grounding, answer alignment, tool use, semantic similarity, and other application-specific evaluation requirements.

For RAG systems, commonly used metrics include Context Precision, Context Recall, Faithfulness, and Answer Relevancy. Together, these metrics show whether the retriever found focused and complete evidence and whether the generator used it correctly.

Q2. What are the four commonly used RAGAS metrics?

Context Precision evaluates whether relevant chunks are ranked above irrelevant chunks. Context Recall assesses whether the retrieved context contains all information required by the reference answer.

Faithfulness determines whether claims in the generated response are supported by the retrieved contexts. Answer Relevancy checks whether the response directly and completely addresses the original user question.

Q3. Does RAGAS require reference answers?

Not every metric requires a reference answer. Context Recall normally needs a reference because it must know which facts should have been retrieved. Reference-based Context Precision also uses an expected answer or reference contexts.

Faithfulness generally requires the response and retrieved contexts, while Answer Relevancy uses the user question and response. This allows some metrics to evaluate production interactions without manually written ideal answers.

Q4. How should RAGAS scores be used in production?

Teams should first build a curated offline evaluation dataset and use it to compare pipeline changes before deployment. Production interactions can then be sampled and evaluated to monitor quality drift and identify new failure patterns.

The metrics should be analyzed separately and by query category. Thresholds must be calibrated using human judgement and business requirements rather than copied from unrelated applications.

Q5. Is RAGAS one combined score?

RAGAS is an evaluation framework containing many separate metrics rather than one universal score. Although some integrations can calculate an aggregate of Context Precision, Context Recall, Faithfulness, and Answer Relevancy, retaining the separate scores provides better diagnostic value.

A combined score can hide an important weakness. For example, excellent Answer Relevancy might offset very low Faithfulness numerically, even though unsupported claims remain unacceptable in an enterprise application.

Topic 7: DeepEval

a) Definition

DeepEval is an open-source evaluation framework designed for testing LLM, RAG, agentic, and conversational applications. It allows developers to represent an LLM interaction as a test case, apply one or more evaluation metrics, define passing thresholds, and use the results as automated quality checks.

DeepEval treats LLM evaluation similarly to software testing. Instead of checking whether two strings are exactly equal, its evaluators can use LLM-as-a-judge techniques to measure semantic qualities such as contextual relevance, Faithfulness, answer relevance, retrieval completeness, and factual consistency.

For RAG systems, DeepEval provides generator-oriented metrics such as AnswerRelevancyMetric and FaithfulnessMetric, along with retriever-oriented metrics such as ContextualPrecisionMetric, ContextualRecallMetric, and ContextualRelevancyMetric. These metrics help developers evaluate retrieval and generation separately.

DeepEval can evaluate one interaction, a collection of test cases, or an automated test suite executed through Pytest and CI/CD. Each metric can be assigned a threshold, and the evaluation can return a numerical score, pass-or-fail result, and natural-language reason explaining the judgement.

b) Purpose

The primary purpose of DeepEval is to make LLM application quality measurable and testable. Developers can compare prompts, retrievers, embedding models, LLM versions, chunking configurations, and Agentic RAG workflows using repeatable tests instead of relying only on manual inspection.

DeepEval helps diagnose failures at different RAG stages. Low Contextual Precision suggests noisy or poorly ordered retrieval, low Contextual Recall indicates missing evidence, low Faithfulness suggests unsupported generation, and low Answer Relevancy means the response does not adequately address the user’s request.

The framework supports regression testing by allowing known questions, expected outputs, and retrieval contexts to be stored as test cases. When a prompt, retriever, or model changes, the same suite can be rerun to determine whether the updated RAG application introduces quality regressions.

DeepEval is useful for CI/CD quality gates because metrics can be assigned application-specific thresholds. A build can fail when retrieval or generation quality falls below an accepted level, although these thresholds should first be calibrated against human judgement and representative business examples.

c) DeepEval Evaluation Architecture

                    DEEPEVAL WORKFLOW
                    =================

                    RAG Evaluation Dataset
                             |
                             v
                      Execute RAG System
                             |
                             v
                    Create LLMTestCase

                 input
                 actual_output
                 expected_output
                 retrieval_context
                 context
                             |
                             v
                   Select Evaluation Metrics

        +--------------------+--------------------+
        |                    |                    |
        v                    v                    v
 Contextual Precision  Contextual Recall   Contextual Relevancy
        |                    |                    |
        +--------------------+--------------------+
                             |
                    +--------+--------+
                    |                 |
                    v                 v
             Faithfulness      Answer Relevancy
                    |                 |
                    +--------+--------+
                             |
                             v
                LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation
                             |
                             v
               Score + Reason + Pass/Fail
                             |
                             v
          Local Testing | Pytest | CI/CD | Monitoring


d) Core Components

1. LLMTestCase
LLMTestCase represents one LLM or RAG interaction and stores fields such as input, actual_output, expected_output, and retrieval_context. The required fields depend on the metrics being applied to that particular test case.

2. Evaluation Metrics
Metrics define which quality characteristic should be measured. DeepEval includes RAG-specific, agentic, safety, conversational, and general-purpose metrics, and developers can also create custom evaluators for application-specific rules.

3. Evaluator Model
Many DeepEval metrics use an LLM as the judge to evaluate relevance, claim support, retrieval completeness, or semantic alignment. The evaluator model can be configured independently from the LLM being tested, helping separate application generation from evaluation.

4. Threshold, Score, and Reason
Each metric can use a threshold that defines the minimum passing score. The result generally contains a numerical score, success status, and optional natural-language reason explaining which parts of the answer or retrieved evidence affected the evaluation.

5. Main DeepEval RAG Metrics

1. Contextual Precision

Contextual Precision evaluates whether relevant retrieved chunks are ranked above irrelevant chunks. It generally requires the input, actual output, expected output, and retrieval context because the evaluator needs to determine which retrieved information contributes to the expected answer.

A low score indicates that irrelevant or weak chunks appear before useful evidence. This usually points toward improvements such as Re-Ranking, Metadata Filtering, deduplication, Hybrid Search, or better retrieval query formulation.

2. Contextual Recall

Contextual Recall evaluates whether the retrieval context contains enough information to support the expected output. It uses LLM-as-a-judge to compare retrieved evidence with the expected answer and can return a score and an explanation for missing information.

A low Contextual Recall score indicates that important evidence was not retrieved. The system may need a larger candidate set, improved chunking, Query Expansion, Multi-Query Retrieval, Hybrid Search, or Multi-Hop Retrieval.

3. Contextual Relevancy

Contextual Relevancy measures the overall relevance of the retrieval context to the user input. It is reference-free and determines whether the retrieved information helps address the question instead of comparing it with an expected answer.

A low score indicates that retrieval produced substantial noise. Unlike Contextual Precision, which emphasizes rank ordering, Contextual Relevancy focuses more generally on how much of the retrieved context is useful for the original input.

4. Faithfulness and Answer Relevancy

Faithfulness evaluates whether the generated answer is supported by the retrieval context, while Answer Relevancy evaluates whether the response addresses the user’s input. These metrics focus primarily on the generator rather than only on the retriever.

They diagnose different failures. A direct but fabricated answer can have high relevance and low Faithfulness, while an accurately grounded response about the wrong policy can have high Faithfulness but low Answer Relevancy.

e) Code Example

from deepeval import evaluate

from deepeval.metrics import (
    AnswerRelevancyMetric,
    ContextualPrecisionMetric,
    ContextualRecallMetric,
    ContextualRelevancyMetric,
    FaithfulnessMetric,
)

from deepeval.test_case import LLMTestCase


# =========================================================
# 1. DEFINE THE ORIGINAL USER QUESTION
# =========================================================

user_question = (
    "What is the annual professional certification "
    "reimbursement limit, and what approval is required?"
)


# =========================================================
# 2. DEFINE THE GENERATED RAG RESPONSE
# =========================================================

actual_output = (
    "Employees may claim reimbursement of up to 25,000 "
    "rupees per financial year for approved professional "
    "certifications. Manager approval is required before "
    "registration."
)


# =========================================================
# 3. DEFINE THE TRUSTED EXPECTED ANSWER
# =========================================================

expected_output = (
    "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees per financial "
    "year for approved professional certifications. Manager "
    "approval is required before registration."
)


# =========================================================
# 4. DEFINE THE ACTUAL RETRIEVED CONTEXT
# =========================================================

retrieval_context = [
    (
        "Employees may claim reimbursement of up to 25,000 "
        "rupees per financial year for approved professional "
        "certifications."
    ),
    (
        "Manager approval is required before registering for "
        "an approved professional certification."
    ),
    (
        "Permanent employees receive twelve paid sick-leave "
        "days during each calendar year."
    ),
]


# =========================================================
# 5. CREATE THE DEEPEVAL TEST CASE
# =========================================================

test_case = LLMTestCase(
    input=user_question,
    actual_output=actual_output,
    expected_output=expected_output,
    retrieval_context=retrieval_context,
)


# =========================================================
# 6. CREATE THE GENERATOR METRICS
# =========================================================

answer_relevancy = AnswerRelevancyMetric(
    threshold=0.7,
    model="gpt-4.1",
    include_reason=True,
)

faithfulness = FaithfulnessMetric(
    threshold=0.7,
    model="gpt-4.1",
    include_reason=True,
)


# =========================================================
# 7. CREATE THE RETRIEVER METRICS
# =========================================================

contextual_precision = ContextualPrecisionMetric(
    threshold=0.7,
    model="gpt-4.1",
    include_reason=True,
)

contextual_recall = ContextualRecallMetric(
    threshold=0.7,
    model="gpt-4.1",
    include_reason=True,
)

contextual_relevancy = ContextualRelevancyMetric(
    threshold=0.7,
    model="gpt-4.1",
    include_reason=True,
)


# =========================================================
# 8. STORE ALL METRICS IN ONE LIST
# =========================================================

metrics = [
    answer_relevancy,
    faithfulness,
    contextual_precision,
    contextual_recall,
    contextual_relevancy,
]


# =========================================================
# 9. RUN THE COMPLETE EVALUATION
# =========================================================

evaluation_result = evaluate(
    test_cases=[test_case],
    metrics=metrics,
)


# =========================================================
# 10. PRINT INDIVIDUAL METRIC RESULTS
# =========================================================

print("=" * 70)
print("DEEPEVAL RAG EVALUATION")
print("=" * 70)

print("\nUser Question:")
print(user_question)

print("\nActual Output:")
print(actual_output)

print("\nExpected Output:")
print(expected_output)

print("\nRetrieved Contexts:")

for position, context in enumerate(
    retrieval_context,
    start=1,
):
    print(f"\nContext {position}:")
    print(context)


print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("METRIC RESULTS")
print("=" * 70)

for metric in metrics:
    print(f"\nMetric: {metric.__class__.__name__}")
    print("Score:", metric.score)
    print("Threshold:", metric.threshold)
    print("Passed:", metric.is_successful())
    print("Reason:", metric.reason)


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is DeepEval?

DeepEval is an open-source framework for evaluating LLM, RAG, agentic, and conversational applications. It represents application interactions as test cases and applies metrics that return scores, explanations, and pass-or-fail results.

For RAG systems, DeepEval provides metrics for Answer Relevancy, Faithfulness, Contextual Precision, Contextual Recall, and Contextual Relevancy. These allow retrieval and generation to be evaluated independently.

Q2. What is an LLMTestCase?

An LLMTestCase is the central evaluation object in DeepEval. It can contain the original input, generated output, expected output, retrieval context, and other supporting fields required by selected metrics.

Different metrics consume different fields. For example, Faithfulness needs the actual output and retrieval context, while Contextual Recall also requires an expected output to determine whether the retriever supplied all necessary information.

Q3. What are the main DeepEval metrics for RAG?

Contextual Precision checks whether relevant chunks appear above irrelevant chunks. Contextual Recall checks whether the retrieved evidence covers the expected answer, while Contextual Relevancy evaluates how useful the overall retrieval context is for the input.

Faithfulness checks whether the generated claims are supported by the retrieval context, and Answer Relevancy determines whether the response directly addresses the user’s question.

Q4. How does DeepEval support CI/CD?

DeepEval integrates with Pytest-style evaluation and allows developers to create automated RAG quality tests. Every metric can have a threshold, and a test fails when the application output does not satisfy the configured quality requirement.

The evaluation suite can be executed whenever prompts, models, retrievers, embeddings, or documents change. This helps detect regressions before a modified RAG application reaches production.

Q5. What is the difference between DeepEval and RAGAS?

Both frameworks evaluate retrieval and generation quality using metrics such as Context Precision, Context Recall, Faithfulness, and Answer Relevancy. RAGAS is strongly focused on RAG evaluation metrics and dataset experiments, while DeepEval presents evaluation through a software-testing-style interface.

DeepEval provides convenient thresholds, reason generation, test cases, Pytest integration, and CI-oriented workflows. RAGAS provides flexible RAG metrics, test-data generation, and experiment-oriented evaluation. Teams may select either framework or use both for different stages.


Chapter 8: Hallucination & Response Quality

Topic 1: Hallucination Causes

a) Definition

* Hallucination occurs when an LLM generates information that sounds fluent and convincing but is factually incorrect, logically inconsistent, fabricated, 
  or unsupported by the available evidence. The response may include invented facts, incorrect numbers, nonexistent citations, or conclusions that cannot be 
  derived from the supplied context.

* Hallucination is not limited to situations where the model knows nothing about the topic. It can also happen when correct information exists in the 
  model’s training data or retrieved context, but the model recalls it incorrectly, combines incompatible facts, misinterprets evidence, or selects an 
  unsupported continuation.

* In a RAG system, hallucination may originate from either the retrieval stage or generation stage. The retriever may return irrelevant, incomplete, 
  outdated, or conflicting evidence, while the generator may ignore correct evidence, distort its meaning, or supplement it with unsupported internal 
  knowledge.

* Hallucination is not a single error with one cause. Research commonly analyzes causes across the complete LLM lifecycle, including training data, model 
  architecture and objectives, alignment, prompting, retrieval, context construction, reasoning, decoding, and external-tool interactions.

b) Purpose of Understanding Hallucination Causes

* Understanding the root cause is necessary because different hallucinations require different solutions. A missing-document problem should be corrected 
  through retrieval improvements, while an unsupported generation problem requires stronger grounding instructions, claim verification, or response 
  correction.

* Cause analysis helps engineering teams avoid replacing the LLM whenever a response is incorrect. The real failure may be poor document parsing, unsuitable 
  chunking, low Context Recall, an incorrect metadata filter, conflicting sources, or a prompt that does not require evidence-based answering.

* It also supports effective evaluation. Context Precision and Context Recall diagnose retrieval failures, while Faithfulness and Groundedness identify 
  unsupported generation. Answer Relevance checks whether the response addresses the question, but it does not independently establish factual accuracy.

* Hallucination analysis is especially important in source-sensitive applications involving enterprise policies, technical manuals, financial records, legal 
  documents, scientific information, or operational procedures. In such systems, plausible but unsupported details can reduce trust and create improper 
  decisions.

c) Hallucination Cause Architecture

                 HALLUCINATION CAUSE PIPELINE
                 ============================

                       User Question
                             |
                             v
                     Query Processing
                Ambiguous or Incorrect Rewrite
                             |
                             v
                      RAG Retrieval
        Missing | Irrelevant | Outdated | Conflicting Evidence
                             |
                             v
                    Context Construction
        Truncation | Noise | Poor Ordering | Lost-in-the-Middle
                             |
                             v
                       LLM Generation
          Parametric Guessing | Reasoning Error | Overconfidence
                             |
                             v
                    Response Verification
                  Missing or Weak Validation
                             |
                             v
                  Unsupported Final Response
                             |
                             v
                        Hallucination

d) Major Causes of Hallucination

4.1 Training Data Problems

LLMs learn from large datasets that may contain incorrect facts, outdated information, contradictory statements, duplicated text, bias, and unreliable sources. The model can reproduce these weaknesses because it learns statistical associations rather than maintaining a perfectly verified database of facts.

Knowledge coverage is uneven across the training corpus. Widely discussed subjects may have rich representation, while private, recent, regional, organization-specific, or highly specialized information may be missing or underrepresented, increasing uncertainty during generation.

Training data also has a time boundary. Information that changed after data collection may not be reflected in model parameters, so questions about current policies, recent events, product versions, or operational status require retrieval from fresh and authoritative sources.

Data quality alone cannot eliminate hallucination because even a clean training corpus may contain different versions of a fact or incomplete explanations. However, high-quality data curation is an important data-centric mitigation strategy within a broader defense-in-depth approach.

4.2 Next-Token Prediction Objective

An LLM is primarily trained to predict the next probable token given the preceding text. This objective rewards fluent and statistically plausible continuations, but it does not inherently require every generated statement to be factually verified against an authoritative source.

When the model lacks sufficient information, it may still produce a probable continuation instead of stopping. The resulting response can appear grammatically correct and confident because linguistic fluency and factual reliability are separate characteristics.

For example, when asked for a policy clause that does not exist, the model may generate a realistic clause number because similar patterns were observed during training. The output follows language patterns correctly even though the specific citation is fabricated.

Grounding, retrieval, abstention instructions, tool use, and post-generation verification are therefore required at inference time. Larger models may improve factual performance, but increased scale alone does not guarantee that every response remains supported.

4.3 Knowledge Gaps and Stale Knowledge

A knowledge gap occurs when the model has insufficient information about the requested subject. This commonly affects private enterprise data, newly introduced policies, uncommon technical systems, niche terminology, regional procedures, and information created after model training.

Stale knowledge occurs when the model remembers an older fact while the real-world value has changed. For example, it may answer using a previous policy limit even though the organization has published a newer document with a different amount or approval condition.

When the prompt does not provide adequate evidence, the model may fill the gap by combining related patterns from its parametric memory. This produces a plausible answer that is not necessarily appropriate for the user’s organization, date, product version, or circumstances.

RAG reduces this problem by supplying appropriate documents at query time. However, the knowledge base itself must be maintained, versioned, and filtered correctly; retrieving an archived document does not solve stale knowledge and may reinforce it.

4.4 Ambiguous or Incomplete Prompts

Ambiguous questions allow several reasonable interpretations. A question such as "What is the limit?" does not specify whether the user means a travel limit, reimbursement amount, token limit, or another restriction, so the model may select the wrong interpretation.

Conversational questions may also depend on entities and conditions established in earlier turns. If relevant history is removed through token trimming or not included in the prompt, pronouns such as "it", "that policy", or "this amount" can be resolved incorrectly.

Poorly designed system prompts may encourage the model to answer every question even when evidence is missing. If the prompt does not explicitly permit abstention, the model may prefer producing a complete-sounding answer instead of stating that the information could not be found.

Query clarification, conversational Query Rewriting, structured inputs, and clear evidence-only instructions reduce ambiguity. However, rewriting must preserve the original intent because an incorrect rewrite can create a new retrieval failure.

4.5 Retrieval Failures

Retrieval failure occurs when the RAG system does not retrieve the evidence required to answer the question. Causes include unsuitable embeddings, poor chunking, vocabulary mismatch, incorrect Top-K, weak metadata, faulty filters, source-routing errors, and an incomplete index.

Low Context Recall means that one or more necessary facts are missing from the retrieved context. The LLM may then complete the answer using internal knowledge or assumptions, creating an answer that appears complete but is only partially supported.

Low Context Precision means that the result list contains substantial irrelevant or weak evidence. Distracting chunks can lead the model toward unrelated details, consume the token budget, and make it difficult to distinguish authoritative evidence from semantically similar content.

Retrieval failures should be corrected using targeted improvements such as Query Expansion, Query Rewriting, Hybrid Search, Metadata Filtering, Re-Ranking, better chunking, larger candidate retrieval, or Multi-Hop Retrieval rather than only modifying the final generation prompt.

4.6 Poor Context Quality

Retrieved context may be relevant but still unsuitable for generation. A chunk can omit its heading, definition, table labels, exception, or preceding condition because the document was divided at an inappropriate boundary during ingestion.

Duplicate and overlapping chunks can cause the model to overemphasize one statement simply because it appears multiple times. Conflicting documents can create another failure when the prompt contains both active and archived policy versions without clearly identifying which one is authoritative.

OCR errors, malformed tables, missing page structure, and incorrect document parsing can change factual meaning before information reaches retrieval. A corrupted amount or detached table value may cause the model to faithfully repeat incorrect evidence rather than hallucinate independently.

Context quality should therefore be evaluated before answer generation. Document status, source authority, parser quality, metadata, version, surrounding structure, and contradiction checks are as important as semantic similarity.

4.7 Excessive or Poorly Ordered Context

Increasing the number of retrieved chunks can improve recall, but it can also introduce distractors and contradictory information. Models do not necessarily use every token equally, so providing more context does not always produce a more accurate answer.

Correct evidence may become lost in the middle when it is positioned between many unrelated passages. Research has observed that long-context models can use information placed near the beginning or end more effectively than the same evidence positioned in the middle.

Poor prioritization can place general background before the answer-bearing policy rule. If the prompt reaches its token limit, lower-positioned evidence may also be truncated, leaving the model without the exact amount, deadline, condition, or exception.

Re-Ranking, Context Selection, Context Compression, token budgeting, and position-aware ordering help reduce this cause. The objective is to supply the smallest sufficient evidence set rather than filling the complete context window.

4.8 Reasoning and Inference Errors

The retrieved documents may contain all required information, but the model can still draw an incorrect conclusion. This can occur during comparisons, calculations, temporal reasoning, negation handling, multi-hop reasoning, or the combination of facts from multiple sources.

For example, one document may identify the department owning a project, while another lists reimbursement limits by department. The model may retrieve both correctly but associate the project with the wrong departmental policy during evidence synthesis.

Logical hallucinations differ from missing-knowledge hallucinations because providing more documents may not solve them. The system may need structured reasoning, deterministic calculations, knowledge-graph traversal, database joins, or explicit verification of intermediate conclusions.

Application-oriented research frequently distinguishes knowledge-related hallucinations from logic- or reasoning-related hallucinations. RAG primarily improves access to evidence, while reasoning controls and tools are required to use that evidence correctly.

4.9 Decoding and Generation Settings

Generation settings influence how the model selects tokens. Higher temperature and broader sampling increase output diversity, which can be useful for creative tasks but may also increase variation and unsupported details in evidence-sensitive question answering.

Even deterministic settings do not guarantee factual accuracy because the most probable continuation may still be incorrect. Low temperature increases repeatability, but it cannot repair missing evidence, an incorrect source, faulty reasoning, or a misleading prompt.

Long and elaborate answer requirements may encourage the model to expand beyond available evidence. If the context supports only a short answer but the prompt requests a detailed explanation, the model may introduce background statements that are not grounded.

Factual RAG applications commonly use conservative decoding settings, concise output instructions, and explicit insufficient-evidence behaviour. These controls should complement retrieval and verification rather than serve as the only hallucination mitigation.

4.10 Tool and Agent Failures

In Agentic RAG, the model may choose the wrong tool, create invalid arguments, misinterpret a tool’s output, or skip a required retrieval action. The final answer can then be based on the wrong source even though the available tools were capable of finding the correct evidence.

Multi-step agents can also propagate an early mistake. An incorrect entity found in the first hop may be used to construct every later query, producing a coherent evidence chain based on the wrong assumption.

Tool descriptions, input schemas, authorization boundaries, and output formats must be explicit. Tool observations should be validated before they are added to the working context or used as inputs to later agent actions.

Agentic workflows require bounded retries, state tracking, evidence grading, and traceability. Self-correction can reduce some failures, but an incorrect evaluator may approve poor evidence or send the workflow through an unnecessary loop.

4.11 Missing Verification and Guardrails

A direct retrieve-and-generate pipeline may return the first answer without checking whether it is supported, complete, relevant, or properly cited. In this design, even a small unsupported claim can reach the user because no component evaluates the response after generation.

Groundedness and Faithfulness evaluators can compare response claims with retrieved evidence. Citation validation can check whether each cited source exists and actually supports the associated statement rather than merely appearing somewhere in the context.

Guardrails can validate structured output, prohibit unsupported recommendations, require mandatory disclaimers, redact sensitive information, and block answers that violate application policies. However, generic safety controls do not automatically verify factual accuracy.

Reliable hallucination mitigation normally uses layered controls rather than a single mechanism. Surveys describe combinations of data curation, alignment, retrieval, grounding, uncertainty estimation, consistency checking, and self-correction as part of a defense-in-depth strategy.


e). Types of Hallucination

1. Intrinsic Hallucination

Intrinsic hallucination occurs when the generated response contradicts the supplied source or context. For example, the policy states that employees can claim 25,000 rupees, but the model responds with 40,000 rupees.

This error indicates that the model received relevant evidence but represented it incorrectly. Better prompting, lower generation randomness, claim verification, and deterministic extraction for critical values can reduce this problem.

2. Extrinsic Hallucination

Extrinsic hallucination occurs when the generated response introduces information that cannot be verified from the supplied context. The new information may be true or false externally, but it is unsupported within the current RAG invocation.

For example, the context states the reimbursement limit, but the answer additionally claims that payment occurs within five days. Since no retrieved source establishes that deadline, the claim is extrinsic and ungrounded.

3. Factual Hallucination

Factual hallucination includes incorrect or fabricated names, values, dates, events, policy numbers, references, or relationships. These errors can arise from stale parametric knowledge, missing retrieval, conflicting context, or incorrect factual synthesis.

Claim-level Groundedness, reference-answer comparison, citation validation, structured data checks, and authoritative-source filtering are useful for detecting factual hallucinations.

4. Logical Hallucination

Logical hallucination occurs when individual facts may be correct but the conclusion connecting them is invalid. It frequently appears in comparisons, calculations, cause-and-effect reasoning, multi-hop retrieval, and temporal analysis.

Deterministic tools, structured intermediate outputs, independent verification, and explicit reasoning constraints can reduce logical errors when ordinary document grounding is insufficient.

f). Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


# =========================================================
# 1. DEFINE THE TRUSTED KNOWLEDGE
# =========================================================

trusted_context = {
    "annual_limit": "25,000 rupees",
    "time_period": "per financial year",
    "approval": "Manager approval is required before registration",
}


# =========================================================
# 2. DEFINE THE LANGGRAPH STATE
# =========================================================

class HallucinationState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    context: dict[str, str]

    draft_claims: list[str]
    supported_claims: list[str]
    unsupported_claims: list[str]

    is_grounded: bool
    final_answer: str


# =========================================================
# 3. CREATE A DRAFT-ANSWER NODE
# =========================================================

def generate_draft_node(
    state: HallucinationState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Create a draft containing supported information and one
    intentionally unsupported claim for demonstration.
    """

    draft_claims = [
        (
            "The annual professional certification "
            "reimbursement limit is 25,000 rupees."
        ),
        (
            "The reimbursement limit applies per "
            "financial year."
        ),
        (
            "Manager approval is required before "
            "registration."
        ),
        (
            "The reimbursement is always paid within "
            "five working days."
        ),
    ]

    return {
        "draft_claims": draft_claims,
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. DEFINE CLAIM-SUPPORT RULES
# =========================================================

def claim_is_supported(
    claim: str,
    context: dict[str, str],
) -> bool:
    """
    Check whether a claim is supported by the trusted context.

    This rule-based implementation is intentionally simple.
    A production system may use an LLM judge, NLI model,
    structured database checks, or citation verification.
    """

    normalized_claim = claim.lower()

    if (
        "25,000 rupees" in normalized_claim
        and context["annual_limit"].lower()
        in normalized_claim
    ):
        return True

    if (
        "financial year" in normalized_claim
        and context["time_period"].lower()
        in normalized_claim
    ):
        return True

    if (
        "manager approval" in normalized_claim
        and "before registration" in normalized_claim
    ):
        return True

    return False


# =========================================================
# 5. CREATE THE VALIDATION NODE
# =========================================================

def validate_claims_node(
    state: HallucinationState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Separate supported claims from unsupported claims.
    """

    supported_claims = []
    unsupported_claims = []

    for claim in state["draft_claims"]:
        if claim_is_supported(
            claim,
            state["context"],
        ):
            supported_claims.append(claim)
        else:
            unsupported_claims.append(claim)

    return {
        "supported_claims": supported_claims,
        "unsupported_claims": unsupported_claims,
        "is_grounded": (
            len(unsupported_claims) == 0
        ),
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. ROUTE AFTER VALIDATION
# =========================================================

def route_after_validation(
    state: HallucinationState,
) -> Literal["accept", "correct"]:
    """
    Accept a fully grounded response or correct a response
    containing unsupported claims.
    """

    if state["is_grounded"]:
        return "accept"

    return "correct"


# =========================================================
# 7. CREATE THE CORRECTION NODE
# =========================================================

def correct_answer_node(
    state: HallucinationState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Remove unsupported claims and retain only verified facts.
    """

    corrected_answer = " ".join(
        state["supported_claims"]
    )

    corrected_answer += (
        "\n\nThe available policy does not provide information "
        "about the reimbursement processing time."
    )

    corrected_answer += (
        "\n\nSource: certification_policy_2026.txt"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": corrected_answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 8. CREATE THE ACCEPTANCE NODE
# =========================================================

def accept_answer_node(
    state: HallucinationState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Return the draft when every claim has been verified.
    """

    final_answer = " ".join(
        state["draft_claims"]
    )

    final_answer += (
        "\n\nSource: certification_policy_2026.txt"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": final_answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 9. BUILD THE LANGGRAPH WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

workflow = StateGraph(HallucinationState)

workflow.add_node(
    "generate_draft",
    generate_draft_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "validate_claims",
    validate_claims_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "correct_answer",
    correct_answer_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "accept_answer",
    accept_answer_node,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    START,
    "generate_draft",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "generate_draft",
    "validate_claims",
)

workflow.add_conditional_edges(
    "validate_claims",
    route_after_validation,
    {
        "accept": "accept_answer",
        "correct": "correct_answer",
    },
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "accept_answer",
    END,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "correct_answer",
    END,
)

hallucination_detection_graph = workflow.compile()


# =========================================================
# 10. RUN THE WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

def run_hallucination_check(
    question: str,
) -> None:
    initial_state: HallucinationState = {
        "question": question,
        "context": trusted_context,

        "draft_claims": [],
        "supported_claims": [],
        "unsupported_claims": [],

        "is_grounded": False,
        "final_answer": "",
    }

    result = hallucination_detection_graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 70)
    print("QUESTION")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["question"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("DRAFT CLAIMS")
    print("=" * 70)

    for claim in result["draft_claims"]:
        print("-", claim)

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("SUPPORTED CLAIMS")
    print("=" * 70)

    for claim in result["supported_claims"]:
        print("-", claim)

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("UNSUPPORTED CLAIMS")
    print("=" * 70)

    for claim in result["unsupported_claims"]:
        print("-", claim)

    total_claims = len(
        result["draft_claims"]
    )

    grounded_claims = len(
        result["supported_claims"]
    )

    groundedness_score = (
        grounded_claims / total_claims
        if total_claims > 0
        else 1.0
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("GROUNDEDNESS SCORE")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(round(groundedness_score, 2))

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL CORRECTED ANSWER")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["final_answer"])


# =========================================================
# 11. PROGRAM ENTRY POINT
# =========================================================

if __name__ == "__main__":
    run_hallucination_check(
        "What is the professional certification "
        "reimbursement policy?"
    )

g) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is hallucination in an LLM?

Hallucination occurs when an LLM generates information that appears coherent and confident but is factually incorrect, fabricated, logically inconsistent, or unsupported by the supplied context. It can include invented facts, altered numbers, nonexistent citations, false relationships, and unsupported explanations.

In RAG, hallucination can originate from poor retrieval, incomplete context, conflicting sources, incorrect reasoning, or generation beyond the available evidence. Therefore, it should be treated as a pipeline-level problem rather than only as a model-level problem.

Q2. What are the major causes of hallucination?

The major causes include noisy or incomplete training data, stale knowledge, next-token prediction, knowledge gaps, ambiguous prompts, retrieval failures, low-quality context, Lost-in-the-Middle effects, reasoning errors, generation settings, incorrect tool use, and missing response validation.

These causes occur at different stages, so they require different mitigations. Retrieval failures require search improvements, whereas unsupported response claims require stronger grounding, verification, or Self-Correction.

Q3. Why can hallucination occur even in a RAG system?

RAG provides external evidence but does not guarantee that the evidence is correct, complete, relevant, current, or properly ranked. If Context Recall is low or archived documents are retrieved, the generator may still lack the information needed for a reliable answer.

Even with perfect retrieval, the model may ignore evidence, alter a value, combine incompatible chunks, or add information from parametric memory. RAG must therefore be combined with Re-Ranking, Context Selection, Groundedness checks, citation validation, and appropriate fallbacks.

Q4. What is the difference between intrinsic and extrinsic hallucination?

Intrinsic hallucination occurs when the response contradicts the supplied context. If the policy states 25,000 rupees but the model answers 40,000 rupees, the generated claim directly conflicts with the evidence.

Extrinsic hallucination occurs when the response adds information that cannot be verified from the supplied context. A claim about a five-day processing period is extrinsic if the retrieved policy does not mention any processing timeline.

Q5. How do you determine whether hallucination is caused by retrieval or generation?

First inspect the retrieved context. If the necessary evidence is absent, the main problem is retrieval and can be measured using Context Recall. If the evidence is irrelevant or poorly ranked, Context Precision and retrieval traces help identify the failure.

If correct evidence is present but the answer contradicts it or adds unsupported claims, the problem is in generation or context use. Faithfulness and Groundedness metrics can detect this failure.

Q6. How can hallucinations be reduced?

Hallucinations can be reduced through authoritative data, better chunking, Hybrid Search, Metadata Filtering, Re-Ranking, complete Context Selection, strict grounding prompts, conservative generation settings, citations, fact verification, and claim-level response evaluation.

Agentic systems can add reflection and Self-Correction loops that rewrite weak queries, retrieve additional evidence, remove unsupported claims, or abstain when trustworthy information cannot be found. No single method is sufficient, so layered mitigation is generally required.

Topic 2: Grounding

a) Definition

Grounding is the process of connecting an LLM’s response to reliable and verifiable sources of information. Instead of allowing the model to answer only from its internal knowledge, the application supplies relevant documents, database records, API results, or tool observations that the model must use as evidence.

In a RAG system, grounding occurs when the retriever fetches relevant external information and the prompt instructs the LLM to answer using that information. The external knowledge base acts as non-parametric memory, enabling the system to access private, current, and domain-specific information during inference.

A response is considered grounded when its important claims are traceable to the supplied evidence. This means that factual values, entities, dates, conditions, conclusions, and citations should either appear directly in the retrieved context or be reasonably inferable from it.

Grounding reduces hallucination risk, but retrieval alone does not guarantee a grounded response. The context may be irrelevant or incomplete, and the generator may ignore it or add unsupported details, so reliable systems also require evidence filtering, grounding instructions, claim verification, and correction mechanisms.

b) Purpose

The primary purpose of grounding is to reduce the LLM’s reliance on parametric memory when the user requires factual, private, recent, or domain-specific information. The model can use an approved enterprise policy or live data source instead of attempting to recall or guess the answer from training patterns.

Grounding improves response trustworthiness by creating a visible connection between generated claims and their supporting sources. Users and reviewers can inspect the evidence, verify the answer, and determine whether the correct document version or system record was used.

It also improves freshness and maintainability. When an enterprise policy changes, the organization can update the indexed knowledge base rather than retraining the complete language model, allowing future responses to use the revised information at retrieval time.

Grounding is particularly valuable for policy assistants, technical support systems, document intelligence, legal search, research applications, and enterprise copilots. These use cases require answers to be based on identifiable evidence rather than on plausible but unverifiable generated content.

c) Grounding Architecture Diagram

                      GROUNDING WORKFLOW
                      ==================

                        User Question
                              |
                              v
                     Query Understanding
              Intent | Entities | Filters | Date
                              |
                              v
                      Source Retrieval
             Vector DB | SQL | API | Knowledge Graph
                              |
                              v
                    Retrieved Evidence
                              |
                              v
                  Evidence Quality Checks
          Relevance | Authority | Freshness | Permissions
                              |
                              v
                    Context Construction
         Question + Instructions + Selected Evidence
                              |
                              v
                         LLM Generator
               Answer Only from Supplied Evidence
                              |
                              v
                   Groundedness Verification
            Extract Claims and Compare with Evidence
                       /                \
                   Grounded          Unsupported
                      |                   |
                      v                   v
               Add Citations       Remove or Correct
                      |                   |
                      +---------+---------+
                                |
                                v
                         Final Response

d) Core Components

1. Trusted Knowledge Sources
Grounding sources may include approved documents, verified databases, authenticated APIs, knowledge graphs, search indexes, and tool results. The source must be appropriate for the question because grounding an answer in an outdated or untrusted document can still produce an operationally incorrect response.

2. Retriever and Evidence Selector
The retriever finds potentially useful information, while the evidence selector removes irrelevant, archived, duplicated, unauthorized, or low-quality results. Re-Ranking and Metadata Filtering help ensure that authoritative and answer-bearing passages receive priority.

3. Grounded Prompt Builder
The prompt builder combines the user question with selected evidence and explicit generation instructions. It should require the model to preserve factual values, avoid unsupported claims, cite sources, and state that information is unavailable when the evidence is insufficient.

4. Grounding Validator
The validator extracts factual claims from the response and checks whether the context supports them. A production validator may use deterministic rules, an LLM judge, a Natural Language Inference model, structured-data comparison, or a combination of these techniques.

5. Types of Grounding

1. Document Grounding

Document grounding connects the answer to retrieved passages from policies, manuals, contracts, reports, research papers, or knowledge articles. The selected chunks should retain source metadata such as document name, page, section, version, and publication date.

This is the most common grounding method in RAG applications. Its effectiveness depends on parsing quality, chunking, retrieval recall, document authority, context selection, and whether the generator follows the supplied evidence.

2. Database Grounding

Database grounding uses structured records retrieved from relational, document, analytical, or transactional databases. It is appropriate when the answer requires exact values, identifiers, counts, dates, relationships, aggregations, or status information.

The model should not generate unrestricted database queries without controls. Production systems should validate schemas, use parameterized queries, enforce authorization, and return structured results that can be verified before generation.

3. API and Tool Grounding

API grounding connects the response to information returned by an approved service or external tool. It is useful for current application status, inventory, ticket information, weather, operational data, and information that changes frequently.

Tool output should be regarded as evidence only after validating the tool’s success status, timestamp, source, authorization, and response schema. An error message or incomplete API response should not be interpreted as verified factual information.

4. Knowledge Graph Grounding

Knowledge graph grounding uses explicit entities and relationships to support answers. It is helpful for questions involving ownership, hierarchy, dependencies, relationships, multi-hop reasoning, and connections distributed across several records.

Graph traversal provides a more explicit evidence path than ordinary semantic retrieval. However, the graph must be current and correctly constructed because errors in entity extraction or relationships can produce a grounded but incorrect conclusion.

6. Grounding Techniques

Retrieval-Augmented Generation
RAG retrieves external evidence at inference time and augments the model prompt with that evidence. It combines the LLM’s language and reasoning capabilities with information stored outside the model, helping address stale knowledge, private data, and knowledge-intensive questions.

Evidence-Only Prompting
The system prompt explicitly instructs the model to answer only from the supplied context. It should prohibit unsupported assumptions, require exact preservation of important values, permit insufficient-evidence responses, and specify an expected citation format.

Claim-Level Verification
The generated response is divided into factual claims, and every claim is compared with the evidence. Supported claims remain in the final answer, disputed claims are corrected, and unverifiable claims are removed or marked as unavailable.

Citation and Attribution
Each factual statement should identify the document, database, API, or tool result that supports it. Citations improve traceability, but they must be validated because merely attaching a source name does not prove that the source establishes the associated claim.

7. Grounding vs Related Concepts

Grounding vs Groundedness

Grounding is the process used to connect generation with external evidence. It includes retrieval, source validation, context construction, evidence-only prompting, and citation generation.

Groundedness is the evaluation result that measures whether the generated response actually stayed within that evidence. A system can implement grounding through RAG but still produce a response with low Groundedness if the model adds unsupported information.

Grounding vs Faithfulness

Grounding describes the architectural process of providing and enforcing external evidence. Faithfulness evaluates whether the generated answer’s claims are factually consistent with the retrieved context.

A grounded workflow supplies evidence, while a Faithfulness score measures how correctly the generator used it. In practice, Groundedness and Faithfulness metrics can overlap because both evaluate claim support.

Grounding vs Fact Verification

Grounding supplies trusted information before or during answer generation. Fact Verification checks claims after retrieval or generation against an independent source, structured record, rule, or additional retrieval operation.

Grounding attempts to prevent unsupported output, while Fact Verification detects factual errors that remain after generation. Reliable applications often use both methods as separate layers.

Grounding vs Source Attribution

Grounding requires the answer to depend on external evidence. Source Attribution identifies exactly where each statement came from by attaching document names, pages, URLs, record identifiers, or tool references.

A response can be grounded without displaying citations internally, but user-facing attribution improves transparency and auditability. Conversely, a response can contain citations without being genuinely grounded if the cited sources do not support its claims.

e) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


# =========================================================
# 1. CREATE A SMALL TRUSTED KNOWLEDGE BASE
# =========================================================

knowledge_base = {
    "certification": {
        "source": "certification_policy_2026.txt",
        "content": (
            "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees per "
            "financial year for approved professional "
            "certifications. Manager approval is required "
            "before registration."
        ),
        "supported_facts": [
            "25,000 rupees",
            "per financial year",
            "approved professional certifications",
            "manager approval",
            "before registration",
        ],
    },
    "travel": {
        "source": "travel_policy_2026.txt",
        "content": (
            "Employees travelling for approved business purposes "
            "can claim hotel accommodation for seven consecutive "
            "days. Any extension requires written approval from "
            "the project manager."
        ),
        "supported_facts": [
            "seven consecutive days",
            "approved business purposes",
            "written approval",
            "project manager",
        ],
    },
}


# =========================================================
# 2. DEFINE THE LANGGRAPH STATE
# =========================================================

class GroundingState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    selected_topic: str

    source: str
    retrieved_context: str
    supported_facts: list[str]

    draft_claims: list[str]
    grounded_claims: list[str]
    unsupported_claims: list[str]

    is_grounded: bool
    final_answer: str


# =========================================================
# 3. IDENTIFY THE REQUIRED KNOWLEDGE SOURCE
# =========================================================

def select_source_node(
    state: GroundingState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Select the appropriate trusted source for the question.
    """

    question = state["question"].lower()

    certification_keywords = [
        "certification",
        "reimbursement",
        "training",
    ]

    travel_keywords = [
        "travel",
        "hotel",
        "accommodation",
    ]

    if any(
        keyword in question
        for keyword in certification_keywords
    ):
        selected_topic = "certification"

    elif any(
        keyword in question
        for keyword in travel_keywords
    ):
        selected_topic = "travel"

    else:
        selected_topic = "unknown"

    return {
        "selected_topic": selected_topic,
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. RETRIEVE THE TRUSTED EVIDENCE
# =========================================================

def retrieve_evidence_node(
    state: GroundingState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Retrieve evidence from the selected knowledge source.
    """

    topic = state["selected_topic"]

    if topic not in knowledge_base:
        return {
            "source": "",
            "retrieved_context": "",
            "supported_facts": [],
        }

    selected_document = knowledge_base[topic]

    return {
        "source": selected_document["source"],
        "retrieved_context": selected_document["content"],
        "supported_facts": (
            selected_document["supported_facts"]
        ),
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. ROUTE ACCORDING TO EVIDENCE AVAILABILITY
# =========================================================

def route_after_retrieval(
    state: GroundingState,
) -> Literal["generate_draft", "not_found"]:
    """
    Continue only when trusted evidence was retrieved.
    """

    if state["retrieved_context"]:
        return "generate_draft"

    return "not_found"


# =========================================================
# 6. GENERATE A SAMPLE DRAFT ANSWER
# =========================================================

def generate_draft_node(
    state: GroundingState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Create a draft containing policy claims.

    The processing-time statement is intentionally unsupported
    so the grounding validator can detect and remove it.
    """

    if state["selected_topic"] == "certification":
        draft_claims = [
            (
                "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees "
                "for approved professional certifications."
            ),
            (
                "The limit applies per financial year."
            ),
            (
                "Manager approval is required before registration."
            ),
            (
                "Reimbursement is processed within five days."
            ),
        ]

    elif state["selected_topic"] == "travel":
        draft_claims = [
            (
                "Employees can claim hotel accommodation for "
                "seven consecutive days."
            ),
            (
                "Extensions require written approval from the "
                "project manager."
            ),
        ]

    else:
        draft_claims = []

    return {
        "draft_claims": draft_claims,
    }


# =========================================================
# 7. CHECK WHETHER ONE CLAIM IS GROUNDED
# =========================================================

def claim_is_grounded(
    claim: str,
    supported_facts: list[str],
) -> bool:
    """
    Check whether a claim contains information established
    by the retrieved source.

    This simplified function uses known factual patterns.
    """

    normalized_claim = claim.lower()

    certification_claim_rules = [
        (
            "25,000 rupees" in normalized_claim
            and "25,000 rupees" in supported_facts
        ),
        (
            "financial year" in normalized_claim
            and "per financial year" in supported_facts
        ),
        (
            "manager approval" in normalized_claim
            and "before registration" in normalized_claim
            and "manager approval" in supported_facts
            and "before registration" in supported_facts
        ),
    ]

    travel_claim_rules = [
        (
            "seven consecutive days" in normalized_claim
            and "seven consecutive days" in supported_facts
        ),
        (
            "written approval" in normalized_claim
            and "project manager" in normalized_claim
            and "written approval" in supported_facts
            and "project manager" in supported_facts
        ),
    ]

    return any(
        certification_claim_rules
        + travel_claim_rules
    )


# =========================================================
# 8. VALIDATE EVERY GENERATED CLAIM
# =========================================================

def validate_grounding_node(
    state: GroundingState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Separate grounded and unsupported response claims.
    """

    grounded_claims = []
    unsupported_claims = []

    for claim in state["draft_claims"]:
        if claim_is_grounded(
            claim=claim,
            supported_facts=state["supported_facts"],
        ):
            grounded_claims.append(claim)
        else:
            unsupported_claims.append(claim)

    return {
        "grounded_claims": grounded_claims,
        "unsupported_claims": unsupported_claims,
        "is_grounded": (
            len(unsupported_claims) == 0
        ),
    }


# =========================================================
# 9. ROUTE AFTER GROUNDING VALIDATION
# =========================================================

def route_after_validation(
    state: GroundingState,
) -> Literal["accept_answer", "correct_answer"]:
    """
    Accept a fully grounded draft or correct unsupported claims.
    """

    if state["is_grounded"]:
        return "accept_answer"

    return "correct_answer"


# =========================================================
# 10. ACCEPT A FULLY GROUNDED ANSWER
# =========================================================

def accept_answer_node(
    state: GroundingState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Return the complete draft when every claim is grounded.
    """

    answer = " ".join(
        state["draft_claims"]
    )

    answer += (
        f"\n\nSource: {state['source']}"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 11. CORRECT AN UNGROUNDED ANSWER
# =========================================================

def correct_answer_node(
    state: GroundingState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Remove unsupported claims and explain missing evidence.
    """

    answer = " ".join(
        state["grounded_claims"]
    )

    if state["unsupported_claims"]:
        answer += (
            "\n\nThe available source does not provide "
            "information supporting the following claim(s):"
        )

        for claim in state["unsupported_claims"]:
            answer += f"\n- {claim}"

    answer += (
        f"\n\nSource: {state['source']}"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 12. HANDLE MISSING EVIDENCE
# =========================================================

def not_found_node(
    state: GroundingState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Abstain when no trusted grounding source is available.
    """

    return {
        "final_answer": (
            "The available trusted knowledge sources do not "
            "contain enough information to answer this question."
        )
    }


# =========================================================
# 13. BUILD THE LANGGRAPH WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

workflow = StateGraph(GroundingState)

workflow.add_node(
    "select_source",
    select_source_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "retrieve_evidence",
    retrieve_evidence_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "generate_draft",
    generate_draft_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "validate_grounding",
    validate_grounding_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "accept_answer",
    accept_answer_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "correct_answer",
    correct_answer_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "not_found",
    not_found_node,
)


workflow.add_edge(
    START,
    "select_source",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "select_source",
    "retrieve_evidence",
)

workflow.add_conditional_edges(
    "retrieve_evidence",
    route_after_retrieval,
    {
        "generate_draft": "generate_draft",
        "not_found": "not_found",
    },
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "generate_draft",
    "validate_grounding",
)

workflow.add_conditional_edges(
    "validate_grounding",
    route_after_validation,
    {
        "accept_answer": "accept_answer",
        "correct_answer": "correct_answer",
    },
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "accept_answer",
    END,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "correct_answer",
    END,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "not_found",
    END,
)

grounding_graph = workflow.compile()


# =========================================================
# 14. RUN THE GROUNDING WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

def run_grounding_workflow(
    question: str,
) -> None:
    initial_state: GroundingState = {
        "question": question,
        "selected_topic": "",

        "source": "",
        "retrieved_context": "",
        "supported_facts": [],

        "draft_claims": [],
        "grounded_claims": [],
        "unsupported_claims": [],

        "is_grounded": False,
        "final_answer": "",
    }

    result = grounding_graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 70)
    print("QUESTION")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["question"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("SELECTED SOURCE")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(
        result["source"]
        or "No source selected"
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("RETRIEVED CONTEXT")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(
        result["retrieved_context"]
        or "No context retrieved"
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("GROUNDED CLAIMS")
    print("=" * 70)

    for claim in result["grounded_claims"]:
        print(f"- {claim}")

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("UNSUPPORTED CLAIMS")
    print("=" * 70)

    if result["unsupported_claims"]:
        for claim in result["unsupported_claims"]:
            print(f"- {claim}")
    else:
        print("No unsupported claims detected.")

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL ANSWER")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["final_answer"])


# =========================================================
# 15. PROGRAM ENTRY POINT
# =========================================================

if __name__ == "__main__":
    run_grounding_workflow(
        "What is the professional certification "
        "reimbursement policy?"
    )

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Grounding in an LLM application?

Grounding is the process of connecting an LLM’s response to reliable and verifiable external information. Instead of allowing the model to produce an answer only from its training knowledge, the application supplies relevant documents, database records, API outputs, or tool observations.

A well-grounded answer restricts its factual claims to that evidence, preserves important values and conditions, and provides source attribution. Grounding improves trustworthiness, but it still requires retrieval evaluation and response validation.

Q2. How does RAG provide Grounding?

RAG retrieves relevant evidence from an external knowledge source during inference and inserts that evidence into the prompt supplied to the generator. This provides the model with current, private, or domain-specific information not reliably available in its parameters.

The prompt instructs the LLM to answer from the retrieved evidence, while later validation checks whether its claims are supported. Retrieval alone is therefore only the first part of a complete grounding workflow.

Q3. What is the difference between Grounding and Groundedness?

Grounding is the engineering process used to connect responses with evidence. It includes source retrieval, filtering, context construction, evidence-only prompting, source attribution, and response validation.

Groundedness is the evaluation metric or quality characteristic showing whether the final response actually remained supported by that evidence. A system may implement RAG grounding but still produce a response with low Groundedness.

Q4. Does a grounded answer always mean a correct answer?

No. A grounded answer is supported by the supplied evidence, but the evidence itself may be outdated, incomplete, incorrect, or inapplicable. For example, an answer may accurately repeat a 2024 policy even though a newer 2026 policy is active.

Grounding must therefore be combined with source-authority checks, metadata filtering, freshness validation, Context Recall, factual verification, and business-rule validation.

Q5. How can Grounding be improved?

Grounding can be improved through better document parsing, authoritative indexing, Hybrid Search, Metadata Filtering, Re-Ranking, Context Selection, strict evidence-only prompts, source citations, and post-generation claim verification.

When evidence is insufficient, the system should retrieve again, use another approved source, ask for clarification, or state that the answer is unavailable. It should not fill evidence gaps with unsupported assumptions.

Q6. Why is source attribution important for Grounding?

Source attribution shows which document, page, database record, API response, or tool observation supports a generated claim. It allows users and reviewers to verify the answer and creates an audit trail for sensitive enterprise workflows.

However, attribution must be validated. A correct-looking citation does not prove grounding unless the cited source actually contains or logically establishes the associated information.


Topic 3: Fact Verification

a) Definition

Fact Verification is the process of checking whether factual claims generated by an LLM are supported, contradicted, or left unresolved by trustworthy evidence. Instead of evaluating the response as one complete paragraph, the system divides it into smaller claims and verifies each claim independently against documents, databases, APIs, or other approved sources.

In a RAG application, Fact Verification commonly occurs after answer generation, although critical values may also be verified before generation. The verifier receives the question, generated claims, supporting context, and source metadata, then produces a verdict such as supported, contradicted, or insufficient evidence.

Fact Verification differs from general response evaluation because it focuses on the truth status and evidential support of individual claims.
