An answer may be relevant and clearly written but still fail verification if it contains an incorrect amount, nonexistent policy clause, unsupported deadline, or invalid relationship.

Retrieval-based fact-checking systems retrieve evidence sentences from a knowledge base and supply the claim and evidence to a model for classification. The output should include the verdict, supporting or contradicting evidence, and an explanation that allows the result to be audited.

b) Purpose

The primary purpose of Fact Verification is to prevent unsupported or incorrect generated information from reaching the user. Grounding reduces hallucination by supplying evidence, but verification provides an additional quality-control layer that checks whether the final claims actually agree with that evidence.

Fact Verification is particularly important for exact factual elements such as amounts, dates, durations, names, versions, eligibility conditions, approval rules, and citations. These details may appear minor linguistically, but one incorrect value can change the operational meaning of an entire answer.

It also helps distinguish between three different situations: the claim is supported, the available evidence directly contradicts it, or the evidence is insufficient to decide. Treating insufficient evidence as false would be incorrect, while treating it as true would encourage hallucination.

Automated verification improves scalability, but it should complement rather than completely replace human review for ambiguous or consequential decisions. Dataset coverage, domain-specific terminology, source quality, and contextual judgement can limit the reliability of fully automated fact-checking.

c) Fact Verification Architecture Diagram

                    FACT VERIFICATION WORKFLOW
                    ==========================

                       User Question
                             |
                             v
                     RAG-Generated Answer
                             |
                             v
                     Claim Decomposition

              Claim 1: Limit is 25,000 rupees
              Claim 2: Approval is required
              Claim 3: Payment takes five days
                             |
                             v
                  Retrieve Evidence per Claim
           Documents | Database | API | Knowledge Graph
                             |
                             v
                     Verify Each Claim
                 /             |             \
                v              v              v
            Supported      Contradicted     Unverified
                |              |              |
                +--------------+--------------+
                               |
                               v
                     Correct or Remove Claims
                               |
                               v
                   Reconstruct Verified Answer
                               |
                               v
                  Add Evidence and Attribution
                               |
                               v
                        Final Response

d) Core Components

1. Claim Extractor

The Claim Extractor divides the generated answer into atomic statements that can be verified independently. It should preserve qualifiers, dates, quantities, negations, and relationships because removing these details can change the meaning of the claim being evaluated.

2. Evidence Retriever

The Evidence Retriever searches trusted sources for passages or records related to each claim. It may use semantic search, BM25, Hybrid Search, SQL, APIs, or knowledge graphs, depending on whether the claim concerns unstructured text, exact records, or entity relationships.

3. Claim Verifier

The Claim Verifier compares one factual claim with its retrieved evidence and assigns a structured verdict. It may use deterministic rules, Natural Language Inference, an LLM judge, database comparison, or a combination of methods according to the claim type.

4. Correction and Attribution Layer

The Correction Layer removes unsupported content, replaces contradicted values with verified ones, or states that sufficient evidence was not found. The Attribution Layer records the document, section, record, or tool result supporting each retained claim.

5. Fact Verification Verdicts

Supported
A claim is supported when reliable evidence directly establishes its meaning. The wording does not have to be identical, but the source must confirm the entity, value, relationship, condition, time period, and other important qualifiers expressed by the claim.

Claim:
The annual certification reimbursement limit is 25,000 rupees.

Evidence:
Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees per financial year
for approved professional certifications.

Verdict:
Supported

Contradicted
A claim is contradicted when the evidence explicitly establishes an incompatible fact. This is more serious than missing evidence because the answer does not merely add information but directly misrepresents the trusted source.

Claim:
The annual reimbursement limit is 40,000 rupees.

Evidence:
The annual reimbursement limit is 25,000 rupees.

Verdict:
Contradicted

Insufficient Evidence
A claim has insufficient evidence when the available sources neither support nor contradict it. The correct response is to identify the information as unverified, perform another approved search, or omit it rather than treating it as true.

Claim:
The payment is processed within five days.

Evidence:
The policy explains the reimbursement limit and approval rule
but does not mention processing time.

Verdict:
Insufficient evidence

6. Types of Fact Verification

1. Evidence-Based Verification
Evidence-based verification retrieves relevant passages and compares them with the generated claim. It is suitable for policy documents, manuals, research articles, reports, and other unstructured sources where the expected fact must be located in text.

2. Structured-Data Verification
Structured-data verification checks factual claims against databases, APIs, JSON records, or business systems. It is more reliable than semantic judgement for exact values such as account status, dates, quantities, identifiers, and transaction records.

3. Cross-Source Verification
Cross-source verification compares a claim against multiple independent sources. Agreement among authoritative sources increases confidence, while disagreement must trigger source-priority rules, freshness checks, or human review instead of arbitrary selection.

4. Rule-Based Verification
Rule-based verification validates claims using deterministic constraints, such as approved value ranges, required fields, date relationships, policy versions, or calculation formulas. It is highly reliable for known structured conditions but cannot handle every open-ended natural-language claim.

7. Fact Verification vs Related Concepts

Fact Verification vs Grounding

Grounding supplies trusted evidence to the model before or during generation. Its purpose is to ensure that the LLM has suitable external information from which to produce the answer.

Fact Verification checks claims against evidence after retrieval or generation. Grounding attempts to prevent factual errors, while verification detects and corrects errors that remain.

Fact Verification vs Groundedness

Groundedness measures what proportion of response claims are supported by the supplied context. Fact Verification provides individual claim-level verdicts, evidence, and correction decisions rather than only an aggregate score.

Fact Verification vs Faithfulness

Faithfulness checks whether the response accurately reflects the retrieved context. Fact Verification may go beyond the original retrieved context by searching additional authoritative sources to determine whether an unsupported claim is true, false, or unresolved.

Fact Verification vs Source Attribution

Fact Verification determines whether a claim is supported or contradicted. Source Attribution identifies the precise document, section, record, or API response responsible for supporting the verified statement.

8. Fact Verification Metrics

Claim Verification Accuracy:

                              Correctly classified claims
Claim Verification Accuracy = ---------------------------
                                Total evaluated claims

This metric requires human-labelled claim verdicts and measures whether the verifier correctly identifies supported, contradicted, and unresolved claims.

Verified Claim Ratio:

                        Supported claims
Verified Claim Ratio = ------------------
                       Total response claims

This ratio measures how much of the generated response can be retained as verified information. It resembles Groundedness but can use newly retrieved evidence rather than only the original prompt context.

Contradiction Rate:

                     Contradicted claims
Contradiction Rate = -------------------
                     Total response claims

A high contradiction rate indicates that the generator misrepresented source information or produced incorrect factual values. Contradictions should normally receive stricter handling than claims lacking evidence.

Evidence Coverage:

                    Claims with retrieved evidence
Evidence Coverage = ------------------------------
                        Total factual claims

Evidence Coverage measures whether the verifier could locate relevant evidence for each claim. Low coverage may indicate an incomplete knowledge base, weak claim retrieval, inadequate source connectors, or overly broad claims.

e) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


# =========================================================
# 1. DEFINE THE TRUSTED EVIDENCE
# =========================================================

trusted_evidence = {
    "source": "certification_policy_2026.txt",
    "annual_limit": "25,000 rupees",
    "period": "per financial year",
    "approval": "Manager approval is required before registration",
    "eligible_item": "approved professional certifications",
}


# =========================================================
# 2. DEFINE THE CLAIM DATA TYPE
# =========================================================

class ClaimResult(TypedDict):
    claim: str

    verdict: Literal[
        "supported",
        "contradicted",
        "insufficient_evidence",
    ]

    evidence: str
    reason: str


# =========================================================
# 3. DEFINE THE LANGGRAPH STATE
# =========================================================

class FactVerificationState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    evidence: dict[str, str]

    generated_claims: list[str]
    verification_results: list[ClaimResult]

    supported_claims: list[str]
    contradicted_claims: list[str]
    unresolved_claims: list[str]

    final_answer: str


# =========================================================
# 4. GENERATE SAMPLE CLAIMS
# =========================================================

def generate_claims_node(
    state: FactVerificationState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Create sample response claims.

    Two claims are supported, one is contradicted, and one
    has insufficient evidence.
    """

    generated_claims = [
        (
            "The annual professional certification "
            "reimbursement limit is 25,000 rupees."
        ),
        (
            "Manager approval is required before "
            "certification registration."
        ),
        (
            "The annual reimbursement limit is "
            "40,000 rupees."
        ),
        (
            "Approved reimbursement claims are paid "
            "within five working days."
        ),
    ]

    return {
        "generated_claims": generated_claims,
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. VERIFY ONE CLAIM
# =========================================================

def verify_claim(
    claim: str,
    evidence: dict[str, str],
) -> ClaimResult:
    """
    Compare one claim with the trusted evidence and return
    a structured verification result.
    """

    normalized_claim = claim.lower()

    correct_limit = evidence[
        "annual_limit"
    ].lower()

    if (
        correct_limit in normalized_claim
        and "limit" in normalized_claim
    ):
        return {
            "claim": claim,
            "verdict": "supported",
            "evidence": (
                f"The policy states that the annual limit is "
                f"{evidence['annual_limit']} "
                f"{evidence['period']}."
            ),
            "reason": (
                "The claim uses the same reimbursement limit "
                "as the trusted policy."
            ),
        }

    if (
        "40,000 rupees" in normalized_claim
        and correct_limit != "40,000 rupees"
    ):
        return {
            "claim": claim,
            "verdict": "contradicted",
            "evidence": (
                f"The policy states {evidence['annual_limit']}, "
                "not 40,000 rupees."
            ),
            "reason": (
                "The generated amount conflicts directly with "
                "the trusted policy."
            ),
        }

    if (
        "manager approval" in normalized_claim
        and "before" in normalized_claim
        and "registration" in normalized_claim
    ):
        return {
            "claim": claim,
            "verdict": "supported",
            "evidence": evidence["approval"],
            "reason": (
                "The policy explicitly requires manager "
                "approval before registration."
            ),
        }

    if (
        "five working days" in normalized_claim
        or "processing time" in normalized_claim
    ):
        return {
            "claim": claim,
            "verdict": "insufficient_evidence",
            "evidence": (
                "The trusted policy does not specify a "
                "reimbursement-processing timeline."
            ),
            "reason": (
                "The available source neither supports nor "
                "contradicts the five-day claim."
            ),
        }

    return {
        "claim": claim,
        "verdict": "insufficient_evidence",
        "evidence": (
            "No matching evidence was found."
        ),
        "reason": (
            "The claim could not be verified from the "
            "trusted policy."
        ),
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. VERIFY ALL GENERATED CLAIMS
# =========================================================

def verify_claims_node(
    state: FactVerificationState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Verify every generated claim independently.
    """

    verification_results = [
        verify_claim(
            claim=claim,
            evidence=state["evidence"],
        )
        for claim in state["generated_claims"]
    ]

    return {
        "verification_results": verification_results,
    }


# =========================================================
# 7. CLASSIFY THE VERIFICATION RESULTS
# =========================================================

def classify_results_node(
    state: FactVerificationState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Separate supported, contradicted, and unresolved claims.
    """

    supported_claims = []
    contradicted_claims = []
    unresolved_claims = []

    for result in state[
        "verification_results"
    ]:
        if result["verdict"] == "supported":
            supported_claims.append(
                result["claim"]
            )

        elif result["verdict"] == "contradicted":
            contradicted_claims.append(
                result["claim"]
            )

        else:
            unresolved_claims.append(
                result["claim"]
            )

    return {
        "supported_claims": supported_claims,
        "contradicted_claims": contradicted_claims,
        "unresolved_claims": unresolved_claims,
    }


# =========================================================
# 8. CREATE THE VERIFIED FINAL ANSWER
# =========================================================

def create_verified_answer_node(
    state: FactVerificationState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Build a final answer using verified evidence only.
    """

    evidence = state["evidence"]

    final_answer = (
        "Employees may claim up to "
        f"{evidence['annual_limit']} "
        f"{evidence['period']} for "
        f"{evidence['eligible_item']}. "
        f"{evidence['approval']}."
    )

    if state["unresolved_claims"]:
        final_answer += (
            "\n\nThe available policy does not specify "
            "how many working days reimbursement processing takes."
        )

    if state["contradicted_claims"]:
        final_answer += (
            "\n\nThe claim that the annual limit is "
            "40,000 rupees is contradicted by the policy."
        )

    final_answer += (
        f"\n\nSource: {evidence['source']}"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": final_answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 9. BUILD THE LANGGRAPH WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

workflow = StateGraph(
    FactVerificationState
)

workflow.add_node(
    "generate_claims",
    generate_claims_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "verify_claims",
    verify_claims_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "classify_results",
    classify_results_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "create_verified_answer",
    create_verified_answer_node,
)


workflow.add_edge(
    START,
    "generate_claims",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "generate_claims",
    "verify_claims",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "verify_claims",
    "classify_results",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "classify_results",
    "create_verified_answer",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "create_verified_answer",
    END,
)


fact_verification_graph = (
    workflow.compile()
)


# =========================================================
# 10. RUN THE FACT-VERIFICATION WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

def run_fact_verification(
    question: str,
) -> None:
    initial_state: FactVerificationState = {
        "question": question,
        "evidence": trusted_evidence,

        "generated_claims": [],
        "verification_results": [],

        "supported_claims": [],
        "contradicted_claims": [],
        "unresolved_claims": [],

        "final_answer": "",
    }

    result = fact_verification_graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 70)
    print("QUESTION")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["question"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("CLAIM VERIFICATION RESULTS")
    print("=" * 70)

    for index, item in enumerate(
        result["verification_results"],
        start=1,
    ):
        print(f"\nClaim {index}:")
        print(item["claim"])

        print("Verdict:")
        print(item["verdict"])

        print("Evidence:")
        print(item["evidence"])

        print("Reason:")
        print(item["reason"])

    total_claims = len(
        result["verification_results"]
    )

    supported_count = len(
        result["supported_claims"]
    )

    contradicted_count = len(
        result["contradicted_claims"]
    )

    verified_ratio = (
        supported_count / total_claims
        if total_claims > 0
        else 1.0
    )

    contradiction_rate = (
        contradicted_count / total_claims
        if total_claims > 0
        else 0.0
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("VERIFICATION METRICS")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(
        "Verified Claim Ratio:",
        round(verified_ratio, 2),
    )
    print(
        "Contradiction Rate:",
        round(contradiction_rate, 2),
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL VERIFIED ANSWER")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["final_answer"])


# =========================================================
# 11. PROGRAM ENTRY POINT
# =========================================================

if __name__ == "__main__":
    run_fact_verification(
        "What is the professional certification "
        "reimbursement policy?"
    )

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Fact Verification in a RAG system?

Fact Verification is the process of extracting factual claims from a generated response and checking each claim against trusted evidence. The verifier determines whether the claim is supported, contradicted, or unresolved.

It may use the original retrieved context or perform additional claim-specific retrieval. The final answer retains verified claims, corrects contradictions, identifies missing evidence, and provides source attribution.

Q2. What is the difference between Grounding and Fact Verification?

Grounding provides external evidence to the model before or during generation and instructs it to use that evidence. It is primarily a hallucination-prevention mechanism.

Fact Verification checks the generated claims against evidence after retrieval or generation. It is primarily a detection and correction mechanism, although critical facts can also be verified before answer synthesis.

Q3. What are the common Fact Verification verdicts?

The most common verdicts are supported, contradicted, and insufficient evidence. A supported claim follows from the available source, while a contradicted claim conflicts with an explicit source statement.

Insufficient evidence means that the available sources neither prove nor disprove the claim. The system should not treat this category as false because the required information may simply be absent from the current knowledge base.

Q4. How would you verify numerical claims?

Numerical claims should be extracted into structured fields and compared with authoritative database values, API results, or document values. The system should preserve units, currencies, time periods, ranges, and qualifiers during comparison.

Deterministic verification is preferable when the exact value is available. An LLM judge may still help identify the claim and its source, but the final numerical comparison should use application logic where possible.

Q5. How do you handle conflicting sources?

The verifier should apply source-priority rules based on authority, status, publication date, version, jurisdiction, and applicability. An active approved policy should normally take precedence over an archived or draft document.

If equally authoritative sources conflict and the system cannot determine which one applies, it should expose the conflict and escalate it rather than silently choosing one value.

Q6. Can Fact Verification eliminate hallucinations?

No. Verification reduces the probability that unsupported claims reach users, but the verifier can miss errors, retrieve weak evidence, or misinterpret complex claims. The supporting source may also be inaccurate or outdated.

Reliable systems use layered controls, including authoritative data, strong retrieval, Grounding, Fact Verification, Confidence Scoring, Source Attribution, Guardrails, Self-Correction, and human review for high-impact cases.


Topic 4: Confidence Scoring

a. Definition

Confidence Scoring is the process of assigning a numerical value that represents how reliable a RAG-generated answer is likely to be. The score is usually derived from several signals, such as retrieval quality, evidence coverage, source authority, claim verification, answer Groundedness, and agreement among retrieved sources.

A confidence score should not be treated as the LLM’s personal feeling of certainty. Language models can produce confident-sounding answers that are incorrect, so asking the model to report "I am 95% confident" is not enough unless that estimate is validated and calibrated against actual correctness.

Confidence can be calculated at different levels, including retrieval-level confidence, answer-level confidence, and claim-level confidence. Claim-level scoring is especially useful because one response may contain strongly supported facts together with another statement that lacks sufficient evidence.

In Agentic RAG, confidence can also act as a control signal rather than only a displayed score. High-confidence answers may be returned directly, medium-confidence answers may trigger additional retrieval or verification, and low-confidence answers may result in abstention or human review.

b. Purpose

The primary purpose of Confidence Scoring is to help the system recognize when its available evidence is insufficient or unreliable. Instead of always returning a definitive answer, the system can indicate uncertainty and select a safer action when retrieval quality, Groundedness, or source agreement is weak.

Confidence Scoring helps prioritize verification effort. Strongly supported answers may pass through the pipeline quickly, while uncertain claims can trigger Query Rewriting, broader retrieval, source switching, Fact Verification, Self-Correction, or escalation to a reviewer.

It also improves transparency by communicating the system’s evidence strength to users and downstream applications. However, displaying a precise percentage can create false trust unless the value has been calibrated against historical correctness using a representative evaluation dataset.

Confidence Scoring is important in policy assistants, enterprise search, document intelligence, and other evidence-sensitive applications. The score should guide answer handling, but it should not replace citations, source inspection, deterministic checks, or human judgement.

c) Confidence-Scoring Architecture

                  CONFIDENCE-SCORING WORKFLOW
                  ===========================

                       User Question
                             |
                             v
                      Retrieve Evidence
                             |
                             v
                   Calculate Retrieval Signals
          Similarity | Ranking | Coverage | Source Quality
                             |
                             v
                     Generate RAG Answer
                             |
                             v
                   Calculate Answer Signals
       Groundedness | Claim Support | Consistency | Relevance
                             |
                             v
                   Combine and Calibrate Scores
                             |
                             v
                  Final Confidence Score: 0-1
                             |
              +--------------+--------------+
              |              |              |
              v              v              v
        High Confidence  Medium Confidence  Low Confidence
          Return Answer    Verify Again       Abstain or
          with Sources     or Retrieve More   Human Review


d) Core Components

1. Retrieval Confidence Estimator
The retrieval estimator evaluates whether the search process found strong and sufficient evidence. It may use similarity scores, rank position, Top-K score distribution, Context Precision, Context Recall, metadata validity, and agreement among retrieved passages.

2. Claim and Answer Evaluator
The answer evaluator breaks the generated response into factual claims and determines whether each claim is supported. Groundedness, Faithfulness, Answer Relevance, completeness, and contradiction results can be converted into response-level confidence signals.

3. Confidence Aggregator
The aggregator combines retrieval and generation signals into one raw score. It may use a weighted average for a simple application or a supervised model trained on examples containing confidence features and human-labelled correctness outcomes.

4. Calibration and Decision Controller
Calibration aligns predicted confidence with observed correctness. The decision controller then maps the calibrated score to actions such as answer, answer with caution, retrieve again, verify claims, abstain, or escalate to human review.

5. Types of Confidence Scores

1. Retrieval Confidence
Retrieval Confidence estimates whether the retriever found evidence that is relevant, complete, and authoritative. It may combine top similarity scores, Context Recall, Re-Ranking scores, result stability across query variations, and source-quality metadata.

High similarity alone does not prove retrieval sufficiency. A retrieved chunk can be semantically similar while lacking the required answer, so retrieval confidence should include evidence coverage and relevance evaluation rather than depending on one vector score.

2. Generation Confidence
Generation Confidence estimates whether the generated response correctly uses the retrieved evidence. Common signals include Groundedness, Faithfulness, claim support, contradiction detection, answer completeness, and compliance with generation instructions.

Token probabilities may provide uncertainty signals for some model APIs, but they are not automatically calibrated measures of factual correctness. Recent research has explored normalized token-probability confidence and self-evaluation for error detection, while emphasizing the need to align confidence with actual outcomes.

3. Claim-Level Confidence
Claim-Level Confidence assigns a separate score to each factual statement in the answer. A response may have high confidence for an exact policy amount but low confidence for a processing deadline that appears in only one weak source.

Claim-level scores provide more useful diagnostic information than one overall score. The system can retain strongly supported claims, qualify uncertain statements, and remove claims whose evidence does not meet the required threshold.

4. End-to-End Confidence
End-to-End Confidence summarizes the overall reliability of the final response. It combines retrieval, generation, source authority, consistency, and verification signals into one score used for workflow routing or user-facing uncertainty communication.

The score should not hide a critical failure through averaging. For example, low Groundedness may need to cap the final confidence even when Answer Relevance and retrieval similarity are high.

5. Confidence Signals

Retrieval Relevance
Retrieval relevance measures how closely the retrieved evidence matches the user’s question. It may use normalized similarity scores, a cross-encoder Re-Ranker, or an LLM relevance grader, but the score must be interpreted according to the selected retrieval system.

Evidence Coverage
Evidence coverage measures whether the retrieved information supports every important part of the user’s request. A compound question should receive lower confidence when the system finds evidence for the reimbursement limit but not for the required approval condition.

Source Authority
Source authority reflects whether evidence comes from an approved, current, and trustworthy source. An active official policy should contribute more confidence than an archived policy, informal note, unverified summary, or document with incomplete metadata.

Groundedness and Consistency
Groundedness measures whether answer claims are supported by evidence, while consistency measures whether the available sources agree. Unsupported claims and conflicting authoritative sources should reduce confidence and may require another verification step.

e) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


# =========================================================
# 1. DEFINE THE LANGGRAPH STATE
# =========================================================

class ConfidenceState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    draft_answer: str
    source: str

    retrieval_relevance: float
    evidence_coverage: float
    source_authority: float
    groundedness: float

    confidence_score: float
    confidence_level: str
    final_answer: str


# =========================================================
# 2. DEFINE A SCORE-VALIDATION HELPER
# =========================================================

def validate_score(score: float) -> float:
    """
    Ensure that every confidence signal remains between 0 and 1.
    """

    return max(
        0.0,
        min(1.0, score),
    )


# =========================================================
# 3. CALCULATE THE COMBINED CONFIDENCE SCORE
# =========================================================

def calculate_confidence_node(
    state: ConfidenceState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Combine retrieval and generation signals using
    educational example weights.
    """

    retrieval_relevance = validate_score(
        state["retrieval_relevance"]
    )

    evidence_coverage = validate_score(
        state["evidence_coverage"]
    )

    source_authority = validate_score(
        state["source_authority"]
    )

    groundedness = validate_score(
        state["groundedness"]
    )

    confidence_score = (
        0.30 * retrieval_relevance
        + 0.25 * evidence_coverage
        + 0.20 * source_authority
        + 0.25 * groundedness
    )

    return {
        "confidence_score": round(
            confidence_score,
            4,
        )
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. CLASSIFY THE CONFIDENCE LEVEL
# =========================================================

def classify_confidence_node(
    state: ConfidenceState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Convert the numerical score into an operational level.
    """

    score = state["confidence_score"]

    if score >= 0.80:
        confidence_level = "high"

    elif score >= 0.50:
        confidence_level = "medium"

    else:
        confidence_level = "low"

    return {
        "confidence_level": confidence_level,
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. ROUTE ACCORDING TO CONFIDENCE
# =========================================================

def route_by_confidence(
    state: ConfidenceState,
) -> Literal[
    "return_answer",
    "request_verification",
    "abstain",
]:
    """
    Select an action using the calculated confidence level.
    """

    if state["confidence_level"] == "high":
        return "return_answer"

    if state["confidence_level"] == "medium":
        return "request_verification"

    return "abstain"


# =========================================================
# 6. HIGH-CONFIDENCE RESPONSE NODE
# =========================================================

def return_answer_node(
    state: ConfidenceState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Return a strongly supported answer with its source.
    """

    final_answer = (
        f"{state['draft_answer']}\n\n"
        f"Confidence: High "
        f"({state['confidence_score']:.2f})\n"
        f"Source: {state['source']}"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": final_answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 7. MEDIUM-CONFIDENCE RESPONSE NODE
# =========================================================

def request_verification_node(
    state: ConfidenceState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Return the available answer with a clear qualification.
    """

    final_answer = (
        f"Available evidence suggests:\n"
        f"{state['draft_answer']}\n\n"
        f"Confidence: Medium "
        f"({state['confidence_score']:.2f})\n"
        "Additional retrieval or claim verification is "
        "recommended before relying on this answer.\n"
        f"Current source: {state['source']}"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": final_answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 8. LOW-CONFIDENCE ABSTENTION NODE
# =========================================================

def abstain_node(
    state: ConfidenceState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Avoid returning a definitive answer when evidence is weak.
    """

    final_answer = (
        "I could not establish the answer with sufficient "
        "confidence from the available evidence.\n\n"
        f"Confidence: Low "
        f"({state['confidence_score']:.2f})\n"
        "Please provide more details or consult an "
        "authoritative source."
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": final_answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 9. BUILD THE LANGGRAPH WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

workflow = StateGraph(
    ConfidenceState
)

workflow.add_node(
    "calculate_confidence",
    calculate_confidence_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "classify_confidence",
    classify_confidence_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "return_answer",
    return_answer_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "request_verification",
    request_verification_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "abstain",
    abstain_node,
)


workflow.add_edge(
    START,
    "calculate_confidence",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "calculate_confidence",
    "classify_confidence",
)

workflow.add_conditional_edges(
    "classify_confidence",
    route_by_confidence,
    {
        "return_answer": "return_answer",
        "request_verification": "request_verification",
        "abstain": "abstain",
    },
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "return_answer",
    END,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "request_verification",
    END,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "abstain",
    END,
)


confidence_graph = workflow.compile()


# =========================================================
# 10. RUN ONE CONFIDENCE-SCORING EXAMPLE
# =========================================================

def run_confidence_scoring() -> None:
    initial_state: ConfidenceState = {
        "question": (
            "What is the annual professional "
            "certification reimbursement limit?"
        ),
        "draft_answer": (
            "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees "
            "per financial year for approved professional "
            "certifications."
        ),
        "source": (
            "certification_policy_2026.txt"
        ),

        "retrieval_relevance": 0.90,
        "evidence_coverage": 0.75,
        "source_authority": 1.00,
        "groundedness": 0.80,

        "confidence_score": 0.0,
        "confidence_level": "",
        "final_answer": "",
    }

    result = confidence_graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 70)
    print("QUESTION")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["question"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("CONFIDENCE SIGNALS")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(
        "Retrieval Relevance:",
        result["retrieval_relevance"],
    )
    print(
        "Evidence Coverage:",
        result["evidence_coverage"],
    )
    print(
        "Source Authority:",
        result["source_authority"],
    )
    print(
        "Groundedness:",
        result["groundedness"],
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("CONFIDENCE RESULT")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(
        "Score:",
        result["confidence_score"],
    )
    print(
        "Level:",
        result["confidence_level"],
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL RESPONSE")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["final_answer"])


# =========================================================
# 11. PROGRAM ENTRY POINT
# =========================================================

if __name__ == "__main__":
    run_confidence_scoring()

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Confidence Scoring in a RAG system?

Confidence Scoring estimates how reliable a RAG-generated answer is likely to be. It combines signals such as retrieval relevance, evidence coverage, source authority, Groundedness, claim verification, and agreement among sources.

The result can guide workflow decisions. High-confidence answers may be returned with citations, medium-confidence answers may trigger additional verification, and low-confidence answers may be withheld or escalated.

Q2. Can vector similarity be used as the final confidence score?

No. Vector similarity measures semantic closeness between a query and a document, but it does not prove that the document contains the answer, that all required evidence was retrieved, or that the generated response used the document correctly.

Similarity can be one confidence feature, but it should be combined with evidence coverage, source authority, Re-Ranking scores, Groundedness, contradiction checks, and answer validation.

Q3. What is confidence calibration?

Confidence calibration is the process of aligning predicted confidence values with observed correctness. If answers assigned 80% confidence are correct approximately 80% of the time, the estimator is considered reasonably calibrated.

Calibration can be measured using Expected Calibration Error, Brier Score, reliability diagrams, and discrimination metrics. Post-hoc methods such as temperature scaling or isotonic regression can improve raw confidence estimates.

Q4. What is the difference between confidence and correctness?

Correctness describes whether an answer is actually right, while confidence estimates the probability or evidence strength associated with that answer. A system can be highly confident and wrong or uncertain and correct.

The purpose of calibration is to make confidence more closely reflect observed correctness. Confidence must still be accompanied by evidence, citations, and verification in source-sensitive applications.

Q5. How should low-confidence answers be handled?

Low-confidence answers should not be presented as definitive facts. The system can rewrite the query, retrieve more evidence, switch sources, verify individual claims, ask the user for clarification, or escalate the case.

If no trustworthy evidence can be found, the system should abstain and clearly state that it cannot establish the answer. This is safer than generating a plausible response to satisfy the request.

Q6. What is the danger of combining metrics into one score?

A weighted average can allow a strong metric to numerically hide a critical failure. For example, high Answer Relevance and source authority may create a good average even when Groundedness is too low.

Production systems should use both an overall score and mandatory minimum thresholds. A response may require Groundedness above a specific level regardless of its final average confidence.

Topic 5: Source Attribution

a) Definition

Source Attribution is the process of connecting each factual statement in an LLM-generated response to the document, passage, database record, API result, or tool output from which the information originated. It enables users to trace generated claims back to evidence rather than accepting the response solely because it sounds convincing.

In a RAG system, attribution starts during document ingestion, where metadata such as source name, document ID, page number, section, version, author, and timestamp is attached to every chunk. This metadata must remain connected to the chunk throughout retrieval, generation, citation creation, and response validation.

Source Attribution is more detailed than displaying a list of documents at the end of an answer. Strong attribution creates a claim-to-source relationship, showing exactly which source supports each statement and, where possible, identifying the precise passage that contains the supporting evidence.

Understanding which retrieved documents influenced the final answer improves RAG explainability, user trust, debugging, and retrieval optimization. However, determining document influence can become difficult when evidence is redundant, complementary, or distributed across several sources.

b) Purpose

The primary purpose of Source Attribution is to make generated answers verifiable and auditable. A user can inspect the cited document and determine whether the source actually contains the amount, condition, deadline, relationship, or conclusion presented by the application.

Attribution also reduces the impact of hallucination by discouraging unsupported claims and exposing statements without reliable evidence. A model may still create a fabricated or incorrect citation, so displaying a source name must be combined with claim-level citation validation.

It helps developers debug RAG failures by revealing which documents influenced an incorrect response. If the cited source is irrelevant, the problem may involve retrieval or ranking; if the source is correct but the answer misrepresents it, the problem is likely related to generation or reasoning.

Source Attribution is especially important in enterprise search, document intelligence, policy assistance, research, compliance, and other applications where users must understand where information came from. It supports transparency but does not replace source-quality or factual-verification checks.

c) Source Attribution Architecture

                  SOURCE ATTRIBUTION WORKFLOW
                  ===========================

                    Documents and Data Sources
                              |
                              v
                     Parse and Create Chunks
                              |
                              v
                        Attach Metadata
       Source ID | Title | Page | Section | Version | Timestamp
                              |
                              v
                    Store Chunks and Metadata
                              |
                              v
                         User Question
                              |
                              v
                    Retrieve Relevant Chunks
                              |
                              v
                   Generate Answer with Citations
                              |
                              v
                  Extract Claims and Citations
                              |
                              v
                Validate Claim-to-Source Support
                       /                \
                   Supported         Unsupported
                      |                  |
                      v                  v
              Retain Citation    Correct or Remove Claim
                      |                  |
                      +--------+---------+
                               |
                               v
                  Final Answer with Sources

d) Core Components

1. Source Metadata Manager
The metadata manager attaches document identifiers, names, pages, sections, versions, authors, URLs, timestamps, and authority levels to indexed chunks. Stable identifiers are important because generated citations must remain resolvable even if document titles are similar.

2. Source-Aware Retriever
The retriever returns both content and metadata rather than plain text alone. It may also enforce source-authority, version, freshness, tenant, department, and permission filters so the generator cites only documents eligible for the current request.

3. Citation-Aware Generator
The generator receives context blocks labelled with source identifiers and is instructed to cite the supporting identifier immediately after each factual claim. It should not generate citations that are absent from the supplied context or attach one citation to several unrelated statements.

4. Attribution Validator
The validator checks whether each cited source exists, whether the claim is supported by the cited passage, and whether important factual claims have citations. It should report citation correctness and citation coverage independently because these represent different failure modes.

e) Citation Quality Dimensions

Citation Correctness
Citation Correctness measures whether a cited passage actually supports its associated claim. A citation is incorrect when it points to a real document that discusses the same topic but does not establish the generated amount, rule, relationship, or conclusion.

Citation Coverage
Citation Coverage measures how many verifiable factual claims in the answer have valid citations. A response may contain two correct citations but still have poor coverage if several other important statements are presented without any attribution.

Citation Completeness
Citation Completeness checks whether all important answer components are traceable to evidence. Critical claims involving amounts, eligibility, deadlines, policy conditions, exceptions, and recommendations should receive stronger citation requirements than conversational statements.

Citation Placement
Citation Placement evaluates whether the citation appears directly after the statement it supports. Placing several citations at the end of a long paragraph creates ambiguity because users cannot determine which source supports each individual claim.

f) Attribution Metrics

Citation Precision:

		                     Correctly supported citations
                Citation Precision = -----------------------------
                                       Total generated citations

Citation Precision answers:“Of all citations produced, how many genuinely support their associated claims?” A high score means the model rarely attaches irrelevant, fabricated, or incorrect sources.

Citation Recall:

		                  Correctly attributed factual claims
               Citation Recall =  ------------------------------------
                                    Total verifiable factual claims

Citation Recall answers:“Of all factual claims in the answer, how many have valid attribution?” A response can have perfect precision but low recall when its few citations are correct while most factual statements remain uncited.


Citation F1 Score:

	                     2 x Citation Precision x Citation Recall
              Citation F1 =  -----------------------------------------
                               Citation Precision + Citation Recall

Citation F1 combines correctness and coverage into one value. The individual precision and recall values should still be retained because the same F1 score can represent different citation problems.

g) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


# =========================================================
# 1. DEFINE RETRIEVED SOURCE PASSAGES
# =========================================================

retrieved_sources = {
    "S1": {
        "source": "certification_policy_2026.txt",
        "page": 4,
        "content": (
            "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees per "
            "financial year for approved professional "
            "certifications."
        ),
    },
    "S2": {
        "source": "certification_policy_2026.txt",
        "page": 5,
        "content": (
            "Manager approval is required before registering "
            "for an approved professional certification."
        ),
    },
    "S3": {
        "source": "certification_claim_process_2026.txt",
        "page": 2,
        "content": (
            "The employee must submit proof of payment and "
            "the completion certificate after completion."
        ),
    },
}


# =========================================================
# 2. DEFINE CLAIM AND EVALUATION TYPES
# =========================================================

class CitedClaim(TypedDict):
    claim: str
    citation: str


class AttributionResult(TypedDict):
    claim: str
    citation: str

    verdict: Literal[
        "correct",
        "incorrect",
        "missing_source",
    ]

    reason: str


# =========================================================
# 3. DEFINE THE LANGGRAPH STATE
# =========================================================

class AttributionState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    sources: dict

    generated_claims: list[CitedClaim]
    attribution_results: list[AttributionResult]

    citation_precision: float
    citation_recall: float

    valid_claims: list[CitedClaim]
    invalid_claims: list[CitedClaim]

    final_answer: str


# =========================================================
# 4. GENERATE SAMPLE CITED CLAIMS
# =========================================================

def generate_cited_claims_node(
    state: AttributionState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Create cited claims.

    The final claim deliberately cites the wrong source.
    """

    claims: list[CitedClaim] = [
        {
            "claim": (
                "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees "
                "per financial year."
            ),
            "citation": "S1",
        },
        {
            "claim": (
                "Manager approval is required before "
                "registration."
            ),
            "citation": "S2",
        },
        {
            "claim": (
                "Employees must submit proof of payment and "
                "a completion certificate."
            ),
            "citation": "S3",
        },
        {
            "claim": (
                "Reimbursement is processed within "
                "five working days."
            ),
            "citation": "S3",
        },
    ]

    return {
        "generated_claims": claims,
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. VERIFY WHETHER A SOURCE SUPPORTS A CLAIM
# =========================================================

def source_supports_claim(
    claim: str,
    source_content: str,
) -> bool:
    """
    Perform simple deterministic claim-to-source matching.

    A production application may use an LLM judge,
    NLI model, or structured source comparison.
    """

    claim_lower = claim.lower()
    source_lower = source_content.lower()

    if (
        "25,000 rupees" in claim_lower
        and "25,000 rupees" in source_lower
        and "financial year" in source_lower
    ):
        return True

    if (
        "manager approval" in claim_lower
        and "manager approval" in source_lower
        and "before" in source_lower
        and "registration" in source_lower
    ):
        return True

    if (
        "proof of payment" in claim_lower
        and "completion certificate" in claim_lower
        and "proof of payment" in source_lower
        and "completion certificate" in source_lower
    ):
        return True

    return False


# =========================================================
# 6. VALIDATE EVERY CLAIM AND CITATION
# =========================================================

def validate_attribution_node(
    state: AttributionState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Check citation identity and claim support.
    """

    results: list[AttributionResult] = []
    valid_claims: list[CitedClaim] = []
    invalid_claims: list[CitedClaim] = []

    for item in state["generated_claims"]:
        claim = item["claim"]
        citation = item["citation"]

        if citation not in state["sources"]:
            results.append(
                {
                    "claim": claim,
                    "citation": citation,
                    "verdict": "missing_source",
                    "reason": (
                        "The cited source ID does not exist "
                        "in the retrieved context."
                    ),
                }
            )

            invalid_claims.append(item)
            continue

        source = state["sources"][citation]

        if source_supports_claim(
            claim=claim,
            source_content=source["content"],
        ):
            results.append(
                {
                    "claim": claim,
                    "citation": citation,
                    "verdict": "correct",
                    "reason": (
                        "The cited passage supports the claim."
                    ),
                }
            )

            valid_claims.append(item)

        else:
            results.append(
                {
                    "claim": claim,
                    "citation": citation,
                    "verdict": "incorrect",
                    "reason": (
                        "The cited passage does not establish "
                        "the generated claim."
                    ),
                }
            )

            invalid_claims.append(item)

    return {
        "attribution_results": results,
        "valid_claims": valid_claims,
        "invalid_claims": invalid_claims,
    }


# =========================================================
# 7. CALCULATE ATTRIBUTION METRICS
# =========================================================

def calculate_metrics_node(
    state: AttributionState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Calculate citation precision and citation recall.

    All four sample claims are considered verifiable claims.
    """

    total_citations = len(
        state["generated_claims"]
    )

    correct_citations = len(
        state["valid_claims"]
    )

    total_verifiable_claims = len(
        state["generated_claims"]
    )

    correctly_attributed_claims = len(
        state["valid_claims"]
    )

    citation_precision = (
        correct_citations / total_citations
        if total_citations > 0
        else 1.0
    )

    citation_recall = (
        correctly_attributed_claims
        / total_verifiable_claims
        if total_verifiable_claims > 0
        else 1.0
    )

    return {
        "citation_precision": citation_precision,
        "citation_recall": citation_recall,
    }


# =========================================================
# 8. CREATE A CORRECTED ATTRIBUTED ANSWER
# =========================================================

def create_final_answer_node(
    state: AttributionState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Retain only correctly attributed claims.
    """

    answer_lines = []

    for item in state["valid_claims"]:
        citation = item["citation"]
        source = state["sources"][citation]

        answer_lines.append(
            f"{item['claim']} "
            f"[{citation}: {source['source']}, "
            f"page {source['page']}]"
        )

    if state["invalid_claims"]:
        answer_lines.append(
            (
                "\nThe available sources do not verify the "
                "five-working-day processing claim."
            )
        )

    final_answer = "\n".join(answer_lines)

    return {
        "final_answer": final_answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 9. BUILD THE LANGGRAPH WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

workflow = StateGraph(AttributionState)

workflow.add_node(
    "generate_cited_claims",
    generate_cited_claims_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "validate_attribution",
    validate_attribution_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "calculate_metrics",
    calculate_metrics_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "create_final_answer",
    create_final_answer_node,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    START,
    "generate_cited_claims",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "generate_cited_claims",
    "validate_attribution",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "validate_attribution",
    "calculate_metrics",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "calculate_metrics",
    "create_final_answer",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "create_final_answer",
    END,
)

attribution_graph = workflow.compile()


# =========================================================
# 10. RUN THE ATTRIBUTION WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

def run_source_attribution(
    question: str,
) -> None:
    initial_state: AttributionState = {
        "question": question,
        "sources": retrieved_sources,

        "generated_claims": [],
        "attribution_results": [],

        "citation_precision": 0.0,
        "citation_recall": 0.0,

        "valid_claims": [],
        "invalid_claims": [],

        "final_answer": "",
    }

    result = attribution_graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 70)
    print("QUESTION")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["question"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("ATTRIBUTION RESULTS")
    print("=" * 70)

    for index, item in enumerate(
        result["attribution_results"],
        start=1,
    ):
        print(f"\nClaim {index}:")
        print(item["claim"])

        print("Citation:")
        print(item["citation"])

        print("Verdict:")
        print(item["verdict"])

        print("Reason:")
        print(item["reason"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("ATTRIBUTION METRICS")
    print("=" * 70)

    print(
        "Citation Precision:",
        round(result["citation_precision"], 2),
    )

    print(
        "Citation Recall:",
        round(result["citation_recall"], 2),
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL ATTRIBUTED ANSWER")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["final_answer"])


# =========================================================
# 11. PROGRAM ENTRY POINT
# =========================================================

if __name__ == "__main__":
    run_source_attribution(
        "What is the professional certification "
        "reimbursement policy?"
    )

h) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Source Attribution in RAG?

Source Attribution is the process of connecting generated factual claims to the documents, passages, records, APIs, or tool outputs from which they originated. It makes the response transparent and allows users to verify the evidence behind each statement.

Strong attribution operates at claim or passage level rather than merely displaying a general source list. It should preserve source metadata and validate that every citation genuinely supports its associated claim.

Q2. What is the difference between Source Attribution and Grounding?

Grounding supplies external evidence to the model and instructs it to use that evidence during generation. It is the broader process of connecting an LLM’s answer to reliable information.

Source Attribution identifies the specific evidence supporting each generated statement. Grounding can exist internally without visible citations, while attribution makes the evidence relationship explicit and traceable.

Q3. What is Citation Correctness?

Citation Correctness measures whether the cited source actually supports the statement to which it is attached. A citation is not correct merely because the document exists, was retrieved, or discusses the same general topic.

Reliable evaluation divides the answer into atomic claims and compares each claim with its exact cited passage. The possible outcomes include supported, contradicted, unsupported, and missing source.

Q4. What is the difference between Citation Precision and Citation Recall?

Citation Precision measures how many generated citations correctly support their associated claims. It detects incorrect, irrelevant, fabricated, or misplaced references.

Citation Recall measures how many verifiable factual claims have valid attribution. A response can have high precision but low recall when its few citations are correct while many factual claims remain uncited.

Q5. How can Source Attribution be implemented?

Attach stable metadata to chunks during indexing, return metadata with retrieval results, label context blocks with source IDs, and instruct the generator to cite those IDs after factual claims.

After generation, extract claims and citations, confirm that every ID exists, compare each claim with its cited passage, and remove or correct unsupported attributions before returning the response.

Q6. Can an answer contain citations and still hallucinate?

Yes. The model may invent a source, cite a retrieved document that does not support the statement, or exaggerate what the passage establishes. Citations can create false confidence when they are not independently validated.

Source Attribution should therefore be combined with Groundedness, Fact Verification, source-authority checks, citation coverage, and claim-level citation correctness.

Topic 6: Guardrails

a) Definition

Guardrails are programmable controls that monitor, validate, restrict, modify, or block inputs, model actions, retrieved information, and generated outputs in an LLM application. They ensure that the system stays within predefined safety, security, factuality, privacy, business, and response-quality boundaries.

Guardrails can operate at multiple stages of a RAG pipeline rather than only checking the final answer. Input guardrails inspect user requests, retrieval guardrails control which documents are accessible, tool guardrails validate agent actions, and output guardrails verify the response before it reaches the user.

A guardrail may be implemented using deterministic rules, classifiers, validation schemas, LLM judges, authorization checks, content filters, or combinations of these methods. Deterministic checks are suitable for exact conditions, while model-based evaluators are useful for semantic and contextual requirements.

NVIDIA NeMo Guardrails is an open-source Python toolkit for adding programmable controls to LLM applications. It can intercept inputs and outputs, perform configurable checks, and block or modify content according to defined policies.

b) Purpose

The primary purpose of guardrails is to prevent unsafe, unauthorized, ungrounded, or noncompliant interactions from moving through the complete LLM pipeline. An invalid request can be rejected before retrieval, while an unsupported or sensitive response can be corrected before delivery.

Guardrails protect external tools and enterprise data sources from uncontrolled agent behaviour. They can validate tool names and arguments, check user permissions, restrict database operations, enforce maximum iterations, and require approval before consequential actions are executed.

They also improve response quality by checking whether the generated answer follows instructions, uses approved evidence, preserves required formatting, includes valid citations, and avoids unsupported claims. Guardrails can therefore address both safety and application-specific quality requirements.

Guardrails reduce risk but do not guarantee that every response is safe or factually correct. They should be treated as one layer within a defense-in-depth architecture that also includes secure retrieval, Fact Verification, Source Attribution, monitoring, evaluation, and human review.

c) Guardrails Architecture Diagram

                      GUARDRAILS WORKFLOW
                      ===================

                         User Request
                              |
                              v
                       Input Guardrails
         Prompt Injection | Scope | PII | Input Validation
                              |
                    +---------+---------+
                    |                   |
                 Blocked             Allowed
                    |                   |
                    v                   v
              Safe Response      Query Processing
                                        |
                                        v
                                Retrieval Guardrails
                    Permissions | Tenant | Source | Version
                                        |
                                        v
                                  Agent or LLM
                                        |
                                        v
                                  Tool Guardrails
                      Tool Allowlist | Arguments | Approval
                                        |
                                        v
                                 Generated Response
                                        |
                                        v
                                  Output Guardrails
                  Grounding | Citations | PII | Format | Policy
                                        |
                           +------------+------------+
                           |                         |
                         Pass                      Fail
                           |                         |
                           v                         v
                     Final Response          Correct, Block,
                                             or Escalate

d) Core Components

i) Policy Definition

The policy defines what the LLM application is allowed and not allowed to do. It may include supported topics, permitted data sources, privacy requirements, prohibited actions, response-format rules, minimum confidence levels, and escalation conditions.

Policies should be explicit and testable rather than expressed only as broad instructions such as “be safe.” For example, a rule can require that every policy amount include a valid source citation or that unauthorized documents never be included in retrieval results.

ii) Input Validator

The Input Validator checks the user request before retrieval or generation. It can enforce data types, maximum length, required fields, supported languages, topic restrictions, prompt-injection patterns, and sensitive-data handling requirements.

Input validation reduces unnecessary model calls and prevents clearly invalid requests from reaching the rest of the system. However, semantic attacks can be difficult to detect, so deterministic validation may be combined with a specialized classifier or model-based evaluator.

iii) Output Validator

The Output Validator checks whether the generated response is grounded, relevant, correctly formatted, properly attributed, and compliant with application policies. It may remove sensitive information, reject unsupported claims, or require revision before delivery.

Output validation should preserve the original evidence and intermediate results so failures can be explained. A blocked response should have a clear reason, such as missing evidence, low Groundedness, invalid citation, or prohibited information.

iv) Guardrail Controller

The Guardrail Controller coordinates validation results and selects the appropriate action. Based on policy, it can allow the response, modify it, retrieve again, regenerate it, request clarification, ask for human approval, or return a safe fallback.

The controller should track correction counts, execution time, model calls, and tool actions. Without bounded control, an automated correction loop can increase cost or continue indefinitely without producing a reliable response.

e) Types of Guardrails

i) Input Guardrails

Input guardrails evaluate the user request before it reaches the model or retriever. They can validate expected structure, block unsupported requests, detect suspicious instructions, limit input size, and identify sensitive personal or enterprise information.

They can also distinguish raw user content from trusted system instructions. User-provided text should not be allowed to override access controls, tool restrictions, citation requirements, or evidence-only answering rules.

ii) Retrieval Guardrails

Retrieval guardrails determine which knowledge sources and documents are eligible for a request. They apply permissions, tenant boundaries, document status, region, date, version, and authority restrictions before or during search.

Retrieval guardrails should use trusted user identity and application metadata rather than accepting permission claims from the user’s message. A user cannot gain access to a restricted document merely by stating that they belong to an authorized department.

iii) Prompt Guardrails

Prompt guardrails protect system instructions and define how the model should use context. They separate trusted instructions from untrusted retrieved content and tell the model not to follow commands embedded inside documents.

Retrieved documents should be treated as evidence, not executable instructions. A malicious document may contain text requesting the model to ignore policies or expose secrets, so the prompt must explicitly prevent such content from controlling model behaviour.

iv) Tool Guardrails

Tool guardrails validate actions proposed by an agent before executing them. They verify that the tool is approved, arguments are valid, the user is authorized, and the action remains within configured operational limits.

Consequential actions may require human approval even when the tool call is structurally valid. Read-only retrieval may proceed automatically, while modifying records, sending messages, or changing permissions may require explicit authorization.

v) Output Guardrails

Output guardrails inspect the generated response before it reaches the user. They can check Groundedness, Fact Verification, Source Attribution, response format, sensitive-data exposure, prohibited content, and required business wording.

When validation fails, the guardrail can remove the problematic content, regenerate from evidence, return an insufficient-information message, or escalate the response for review. It should not silently replace a factual answer with an unverified guess.

vi) Dialog Guardrails

Dialog guardrails control how a conversation progresses across multiple turns. They can require clarification when intent is ambiguous, prevent unsupported topic changes, preserve mandatory workflow steps, and ensure that required information is collected before an operation occurs.

For example, a policy assistant may require the policy year and region before answering a version-sensitive question. The guardrail can ask for missing information rather than selecting a document based on an unsupported assumption.

f) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


# =========================================================
# 1. DEFINE THE TRUSTED KNOWLEDGE BASE
# =========================================================

knowledge_base = {
    "certification": {
        "source_id": "CERT-2026",
        "source": "certification_policy_2026.txt",
        "status": "active",
        "content": (
            "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees per "
            "financial year for approved professional "
            "certifications. Manager approval is required "
            "before registration."
        ),
    }
}


# =========================================================
# 2. DEFINE THE LANGGRAPH STATE
# =========================================================

class GuardrailState(TypedDict):
    question: str

    input_allowed: bool
    input_reason: str

    source_id: str
    source_name: str
    context: str

    draft_claims: list[str]
    supported_claims: list[str]
    unsupported_claims: list[str]

    citation_valid: bool
    output_allowed: bool
    output_reason: str

    final_answer: str


# =========================================================
# 3. INPUT GUARDRAIL NODE
# =========================================================

def input_guardrail_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Validate the user request before retrieval.
    """

    question = state["question"].strip()
    normalized_question = question.lower()

    blocked_patterns = [
        "ignore previous instructions",
        "reveal confidential",
        "bypass access control",
        "show secret data",
    ]

    if not question:
        return {
            "input_allowed": False,
            "input_reason": (
                "The user question is empty."
            ),
        }

    if len(question) > 500:
        return {
            "input_allowed": False,
            "input_reason": (
                "The user question exceeds the allowed length."
            ),
        }

    if any(
        pattern in normalized_question
        for pattern in blocked_patterns
    ):
        return {
            "input_allowed": False,
            "input_reason": (
                "The request contains an instruction that "
                "violates the application's access policy."
            ),
        }

    return {
        "input_allowed": True,
        "input_reason": (
            "The request passed input validation."
        ),
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. ROUTE AFTER INPUT VALIDATION
# =========================================================

def route_after_input_check(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> Literal["retrieve", "block_input"]:
    """
    Continue only when the input guardrail allows the request.
    """

    if state["input_allowed"]:
        return "retrieve"

    return "block_input"


# =========================================================
# 5. RETRIEVAL NODE
# =========================================================

def retrieval_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Retrieve an active and approved policy document.
    """

    question = state["question"].lower()

    if (
        "certification" not in question
        and "reimbursement" not in question
    ):
        return {
            "source_id": "",
            "source_name": "",
            "context": "",
        }

    document = knowledge_base["certification"]

    # Retrieval guardrail:
    # archived or inactive documents cannot be used.
    if document["status"] != "active":
        return {
            "source_id": "",
            "source_name": "",
            "context": "",
        }

    return {
        "source_id": document["source_id"],
        "source_name": document["source"],
        "context": document["content"],
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. ROUTE AFTER RETRIEVAL
# =========================================================

def route_after_retrieval(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> Literal["generate_draft", "no_evidence"]:
    """
    Generate only when approved evidence exists.
    """

    if state["context"]:
        return "generate_draft"

    return "no_evidence"
