# =========================================================
# 7. GENERATE A SAMPLE DRAFT
# =========================================================

def generate_draft_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Generate sample claims.

    The final claim is deliberately unsupported so the
    output guardrail can detect and remove it.
    """

    draft_claims = [
        (
            "Employees may claim up to 25,000 rupees "
            "per financial year."
        ),
        (
            "The reimbursement applies to approved "
            "professional certifications."
        ),
        (
            "Manager approval is required before registration."
        ),
        (
            "Reimbursement is processed within five "
            "working days."
        ),
    ]

    return {
        "draft_claims": draft_claims,
    }


# =========================================================
# 8. VERIFY WHETHER A CLAIM IS SUPPORTED
# =========================================================

def claim_is_supported(
    claim: str,
    context: str,
) -> bool:
    """
    Perform simple deterministic support checks.
    """

    claim_lower = claim.lower()
    context_lower = context.lower()

    if (
        "25,000 rupees" in claim_lower
        and "25,000 rupees" in context_lower
        and "financial year" in context_lower
    ):
        return True

    if (
        "approved professional certifications" in claim_lower
        and "approved professional certifications"
        in context_lower
    ):
        return True

    if (
        "manager approval" in claim_lower
        and "before registration" in claim_lower
        and "manager approval" in context_lower
        and "before registration" in context_lower
    ):
        return True

    return False


# =========================================================
# 9. OUTPUT GROUNDING GUARDRAIL
# =========================================================

def output_grounding_guardrail_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Separate supported and unsupported response claims.
    """

    supported_claims = []
    unsupported_claims = []

    for claim in state["draft_claims"]:
        if claim_is_supported(
            claim=claim,
            context=state["context"],
        ):
            supported_claims.append(claim)
        else:
            unsupported_claims.append(claim)

    return {
        "supported_claims": supported_claims,
        "unsupported_claims": unsupported_claims,
    }


# =========================================================
# 10. CITATION GUARDRAIL
# =========================================================

def citation_guardrail_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Verify that the selected source exists in the trusted
    knowledge base and matches the retrieved source.
    """

    trusted_document = knowledge_base["certification"]

    citation_valid = (
        state["source_id"]
        == trusted_document["source_id"]
        and state["source_name"]
        == trusted_document["source"]
        and trusted_document["status"] == "active"
    )

    return {
        "citation_valid": citation_valid,
    }


# =========================================================
# 11. FINAL OUTPUT DECISION
# =========================================================

def output_decision_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Decide whether to allow the answer unchanged, correct it,
    or block it.
    """

    if not state["citation_valid"]:
        return {
            "output_allowed": False,
            "output_reason": (
                "The response does not contain a valid "
                "approved source citation."
            ),
        }

    if not state["supported_claims"]:
        return {
            "output_allowed": False,
            "output_reason": (
                "None of the generated claims are supported "
                "by the retrieved policy."
            ),
        }

    if state["unsupported_claims"]:
        return {
            "output_allowed": True,
            "output_reason": (
                "The response can be returned after "
                "unsupported claims are removed."
            ),
        }

    return {
        "output_allowed": True,
        "output_reason": (
            "All generated claims and the citation passed "
            "output validation."
        ),
    }


# =========================================================
# 12. ROUTE AFTER OUTPUT DECISION
# =========================================================

def route_after_output_check(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> Literal[
    "return_answer",
    "correct_answer",
    "block_output",
]:
    """
    Choose the final output path.
    """

    if not state["output_allowed"]:
        return "block_output"

    if state["unsupported_claims"]:
        return "correct_answer"

    return "return_answer"


# =========================================================
# 13. RETURN A FULLY VALID ANSWER
# =========================================================

def return_answer_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Return the complete answer when every check passes.
    """

    answer = " ".join(
        state["draft_claims"]
    )

    answer += (
        f"\n\nSource: {state['source_name']} "
        f"[{state['source_id']}]"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 14. CORRECT A PARTIALLY VALID ANSWER
# =========================================================

def correct_answer_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Remove unsupported claims and retain verified content.
    """

    answer = " ".join(
        state["supported_claims"]
    )

    answer += (
        "\n\nThe available policy does not specify the "
        "reimbursement processing time."
    )

    answer += (
        f"\n\nSource: {state['source_name']} "
        f"[{state['source_id']}]"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 15. BLOCK AN INVALID INPUT
# =========================================================

def block_input_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Return a safe response for blocked input.
    """

    return {
        "final_answer": (
            "I cannot process this request.\n\n"
            f"Reason: {state['input_reason']}"
        )
    }


# =========================================================
# 16. HANDLE MISSING EVIDENCE
# =========================================================

def no_evidence_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Abstain when no approved evidence is available.
    """

    return {
        "final_answer": (
            "I could not find an active and approved source "
            "containing the requested information."
        )
    }


# =========================================================
# 17. BLOCK AN INVALID OUTPUT
# =========================================================

def block_output_node(
    state: GuardrailState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Prevent an invalid generated response from reaching
    the user.
    """

    return {
        "final_answer": (
            "The generated response could not be returned "
            "because it failed output validation.\n\n"
            f"Reason: {state['output_reason']}"
        )
    }


# =========================================================
# 18. BUILD THE LANGGRAPH WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

workflow = StateGraph(GuardrailState)

workflow.add_node(
    "input_guardrail",
    input_guardrail_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "retrieve",
    retrieval_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "generate_draft",
    generate_draft_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "grounding_guardrail",
    output_grounding_guardrail_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "citation_guardrail",
    citation_guardrail_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "output_decision",
    output_decision_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "return_answer",
    return_answer_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "correct_answer",
    correct_answer_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "block_input",
    block_input_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "no_evidence",
    no_evidence_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "block_output",
    block_output_node,
)


workflow.add_edge(
    START,
    "input_guardrail",
)

workflow.add_conditional_edges(
    "input_guardrail",
    route_after_input_check,
    {
        "retrieve": "retrieve",
        "block_input": "block_input",
    },
)

workflow.add_conditional_edges(
    "retrieve",
    route_after_retrieval,
    {
        "generate_draft": "generate_draft",
        "no_evidence": "no_evidence",
    },
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "generate_draft",
    "grounding_guardrail",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "grounding_guardrail",
    "citation_guardrail",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "citation_guardrail",
    "output_decision",
)

workflow.add_conditional_edges(
    "output_decision",
    route_after_output_check,
    {
        "return_answer": "return_answer",
        "correct_answer": "correct_answer",
        "block_output": "block_output",
    },
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "return_answer",
    END,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "correct_answer",
    END,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "block_input",
    END,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "no_evidence",
    END,
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "block_output",
    END,
)

guardrail_graph = workflow.compile()


# =========================================================
# 19. RUN THE GUARDRAIL WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

def run_guardrail_workflow(
    question: str,
) -> None:
    initial_state: GuardrailState = {
        "question": question,

        "input_allowed": False,
        "input_reason": "",

        "source_id": "",
        "source_name": "",
        "context": "",

        "draft_claims": [],
        "supported_claims": [],
        "unsupported_claims": [],

        "citation_valid": False,
        "output_allowed": False,
        "output_reason": "",

        "final_answer": "",
    }

    result = guardrail_graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 70)
    print("QUESTION")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["question"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("INPUT GUARDRAIL")
    print("=" * 70)
    print("Allowed:", result["input_allowed"])
    print("Reason:", result["input_reason"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("SOURCE")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(
        result["source_name"]
        or "No source retrieved"
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("SUPPORTED CLAIMS")
    print("=" * 70)

    if result["supported_claims"]:
        for claim in result["supported_claims"]:
            print("-", claim)
    else:
        print("No supported claims.")

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("UNSUPPORTED CLAIMS")
    print("=" * 70)

    if result["unsupported_claims"]:
        for claim in result["unsupported_claims"]:
            print("-", claim)
    else:
        print("No unsupported claims.")

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("OUTPUT GUARDRAIL")
    print("=" * 70)
    print("Citation valid:", result["citation_valid"])
    print("Output allowed:", result["output_allowed"])
    print("Reason:", result["output_reason"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL RESPONSE")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["final_answer"])


# =========================================================
# 20. PROGRAM ENTRY POINT
# =========================================================

if __name__ == "__main__":
    run_guardrail_workflow(
        "What is the professional certification "
        "reimbursement policy?"
    )

g) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What are Guardrails in an LLM application?

Guardrails are programmable controls that monitor and enforce how an LLM application processes user input, retrieves data, invokes tools, and generates responses. They can allow, block, modify, verify, or escalate an interaction according to safety and business policies.

Guardrails can use deterministic rules, classifiers, LLM judges, authorization checks, schemas, and response-quality evaluators. Their purpose is to constrain probabilistic model behaviour within explicit application boundaries.

Q2. What are the major types of Guardrails?

The major types include input guardrails, retrieval guardrails, prompt guardrails, tool guardrails, dialog guardrails, and output guardrails. Each category protects a different stage of the application pipeline.

Input guardrails validate requests, retrieval guardrails control document access, tool guardrails validate actions, and output guardrails check Groundedness, privacy, citations, formatting, and other response requirements.

Q3. What is the difference between deterministic and LLM-based Guardrails?

Deterministic guardrails use explicit rules such as schemas, thresholds, regular expressions, authorization policies, source allowlists, and exact-value comparisons. They are predictable, fast, inexpensive, and easy to test.

LLM-based guardrails evaluate semantic requirements such as whether a claim is supported or whether a request falls outside the allowed scope. They are more flexible but less deterministic and require careful calibration.

Q4. Are prompt instructions sufficient as Guardrails?

No. Prompt instructions influence the model but cannot enforce database authorization, restrict operating-system access, validate tool arguments, confirm citations, or guarantee that the output contains no unsupported information.

Critical restrictions should be enforced by application code outside the LLM. Prompts can guide behaviour, but deterministic authorization and validation must remain independent of model compliance.

Q5. How do Guardrails work in RAG?

RAG guardrails validate the user query, enforce retrieval permissions, restrict sources by metadata, protect the prompt from document-based injection, check generated claims, and validate source citations.

When validation fails, the system may rewrite the query, retrieve again, remove unsupported content, abstain, or escalate. This creates a controlled RAG pipeline rather than a simple retrieve-and-generate chain.

Q6. What is NeMo Guardrails?

NeMo Guardrails is NVIDIA’s open-source toolkit for adding programmable guardrails to LLM-based applications. It can intercept inputs and outputs and enforce configured policies through safety checks, custom actions, YAML configuration, and Colang flows.

Its guardrail catalog includes capabilities related to content safety, jailbreak detection, topic control, PII handling, agentic security, fact-checking, and hallucination-related validation.

Q7. Can Guardrails completely prevent hallucinations?

No. Guardrails can detect and reduce unsupported claims, but they may miss subtle factual errors or make incorrect evaluation decisions. The retrieved evidence itself may also be incomplete, outdated, or incorrect.

Reliable hallucination mitigation combines high-quality retrieval, Grounding, Fact Verification, Confidence Scoring, Source Attribution, Guardrails, Self-Correction, evaluation, and human review for uncertain or consequential cases.

Q8. How should Guardrails be evaluated?

Guardrails should be tested using valid requests, invalid requests, ambiguous cases, adversarial inputs, unauthorized access attempts, unsupported answers, invalid citations, and tool-call failures. Both detection rate and false-positive rate must be measured.

Testing should also measure latency, cost, correction success, escalation rate, and policy coverage. Guardrail evaluation must continue after deployment because new prompts, tools, documents, and attack patterns can expose previously unseen failures.


Chapter 9: Advanced Enterprise RAG

a) Definition

* Multi-Hop RAG is a retrieval architecture that performs multiple connected retrieval steps before generating the final answer. Each step, called a hop, retrieves one piece of evidence, and that evidence may provide the entity, relationship, or constraint needed to perform the next search.

* Traditional RAG commonly retrieves documents once using the original query. Multi-Hop RAG supports questions whose answer requires combining evidence from multiple documents, and existing RAG methods can struggle with both retrieving and reasoning over such distributed evidence.

* A hop is not simply another independent search. In a dependent multi-hop workflow, the result of Hop 1 changes or completes the query used in Hop 2, creating an evidence chain that connects the original question with the final conclusion.

* Multi-Hop RAG may use vector databases, keyword search, SQL, APIs, or knowledge graphs across different hops. The final generator receives the collected evidence and synthesizes an answer only after the system determines that the evidence is sufficient.

b) Purpose

* The main purpose of Multi-Hop RAG is to answer questions whose evidence is distributed across multiple documents or systems. A single retrieval query may find one relevant fact but fail to discover the related fact needed to complete the answer.

* It supports relationship-oriented questions such as identifying an entity in one source and retrieving that entity’s policy, owner, location, or status from another source. The intermediate answer becomes a bridge between otherwise disconnected pieces of information.

* Multi-Hop RAG improves evidence coverage for comparison, dependency, temporal, and cause-effect questions. However, each additional hop creates another opportunity for retrieval failure, so intermediate results must be verified before they guide later searches.

* Recent research continues to explore planned and adaptive multi-hop retrieval because static retrieval plans can fail when intermediate evidence changes, while iterative systems can follow an incorrect early result and amplify the error across later hops.

c) Architecture Diagram           
		
	          MULTI-HOP RAG WORKFLOW
                    ======================

                        User Question
                              |
                              v
                    Question Decomposition
                              |
                              v
                         Hop 1 Query
                  Identify Project Owner
                              |
                              v
                    Retrieve Evidence 1
               Project Orion -> Data Engineering
                              |
                              v
               Extract Intermediate Information
                  Department = Data Engineering
                              |
                              v
                         Hop 2 Query
           Find Data Engineering Certification Policy
                              |
                              v
                    Retrieve Evidence 2
             Annual Limit = 25,000 Rupees
                              |
                              v
                 Evidence Sufficiency Check
                       /              \
                     No                Yes
                     |                  |
                     v                  v
               Another Hop       Combine Evidence
                                        |
                                        v
                               Generate Final Answer
                                        |
                                        v
                                Source Attribution

d) Core Components

1. Query Decomposer or Planner
The planner analyzes the original question and identifies the facts needed to answer it. It determines the first search, dependencies between steps, expected intermediate outputs, and conditions for stopping retrieval.

2. Multi-Hop Retriever
The retriever executes one query at a time against a vector store, keyword index, database, API, or graph. Later queries may include entities or constraints extracted from evidence returned by earlier retrieval operations.

3. Intermediate State and Evidence Store
Shared state preserves the original question, current hop, intermediate entities, retrieved documents, and completed steps. This prevents later hops from losing the reasoning chain and supports debugging when a final answer is incorrect.

4. Evidence Evaluator and Answer Synthesizer
The evaluator checks whether each intermediate fact is reliable and whether the collected evidence is sufficient. The synthesizer combines only validated evidence, explains the relationship between facts, and includes source attribution in the final response. 


e) Types of Multi-Hop RAG

i) Sequential Multi-Hop RAG

Sequential retrieval executes hops in dependency order. Hop 2 cannot begin until Hop 1 discovers the information needed to construct the second query, making this pattern suitable for entity-linking and relationship questions.

ii) Parallel Multi-Hop RAG

Parallel retrieval executes independent subqueries simultaneously and merges their evidence later. It is suitable for comparisons where each topic can be searched without waiting for another retrieval result.

iii) Iterative Multi-Hop RAG

Iterative retrieval continues until the evidence evaluator determines that sufficient information has been collected. The system may rewrite a weak query, retrieve another document, or stop after reaching its maximum hop count.

iv) Graph-Based Multi-Hop RAG

Graph-based retrieval traverses explicit entity relationships, such as Project → Department → Policy. It is effective when the knowledge base contains structured relationships, although constructing and maintaining the graph introduces additional complexity.

f) Code Example

from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


# =========================================================
# 1. DEFINE THE PROJECT KNOWLEDGE BASE
# =========================================================

project_knowledge_base = {
    "project orion": {
        "department": "Data Engineering",
        "source": "project_registry_2026.txt",
    },
    "project atlas": {
        "department": "Finance",
        "source": "project_registry_2026.txt",
    },
}


# =========================================================
# 2. DEFINE THE POLICY KNOWLEDGE BASE
# =========================================================

policy_knowledge_base = {
    "data engineering": {
        "annual_limit": "25,000 rupees",
        "approval": (
            "Manager approval is required before registration."
        ),
        "source": "data_engineering_learning_policy_2026.txt",
    },
    "finance": {
        "annual_limit": "20,000 rupees",
        "approval": (
            "Department-head approval is required before "
            "registration."
        ),
        "source": "finance_learning_policy_2026.txt",
    },
}


# =========================================================
# 3. DEFINE THE LANGGRAPH STATE
# =========================================================

class MultiHopState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    project_name: str

    department: str
    project_source: str

    annual_limit: str
    approval: str
    policy_source: str

    hop_1_completed: bool
    hop_2_completed: bool

    final_answer: str


# =========================================================
# 4. EXTRACT THE PROJECT NAME
# =========================================================

def identify_project_node(
    state: MultiHopState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Identify the project mentioned in the user question.
    """

    question = state["question"].lower()
    project_name = ""

    for project in project_knowledge_base:
        if project in question:
            project_name = project
            break

    return {
        "project_name": project_name,
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. HOP 1: RETRIEVE THE PROJECT'S DEPARTMENT
# =========================================================

def retrieve_department_node(
    state: MultiHopState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Hop 1 retrieves the department that owns the project.
    """

    project_name = state["project_name"]

    if project_name not in project_knowledge_base:
        return {
            "department": "",
            "project_source": "",
            "hop_1_completed": False,
        }

    project_record = project_knowledge_base[
        project_name
    ]

    return {
        "department": project_record["department"],
        "project_source": project_record["source"],
        "hop_1_completed": True,
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. HOP 2: RETRIEVE THE DEPARTMENT POLICY
# =========================================================

def retrieve_policy_node(
    state: MultiHopState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Hop 2 uses the department discovered in Hop 1 to
    retrieve the appropriate learning policy.
    """

    department_key = state["department"].lower()

    if department_key not in policy_knowledge_base:
        return {
            "annual_limit": "",
            "approval": "",
            "policy_source": "",
            "hop_2_completed": False,
        }

    policy = policy_knowledge_base[
        department_key
    ]

    return {
        "annual_limit": policy["annual_limit"],
        "approval": policy["approval"],
        "policy_source": policy["source"],
        "hop_2_completed": True,
    }


# =========================================================
# 7. GENERATE THE FINAL GROUNDED ANSWER
# =========================================================

def generate_answer_node(
    state: MultiHopState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Combine evidence from both retrieval hops.
    """

    if not state["hop_1_completed"]:
        return {
            "final_answer": (
                "The project could not be found in the "
                "project knowledge base."
            )
        }

    if not state["hop_2_completed"]:
        return {
            "final_answer": (
                f"The project belongs to the "
                f"{state['department']} department, but no "
                "applicable certification policy was found.\n\n"
                f"Source: {state['project_source']}"
            )
        }

    project_display_name = (
        state["project_name"].title()
    )

    final_answer = (
        f"{project_display_name} is owned by the "
        f"{state['department']} department. "
        f"The certification reimbursement policy for this "
        f"department allows employees to claim up to "
        f"{state['annual_limit']} per financial year. "
        f"{state['approval']}\n\n"
        f"Sources:\n"
        f"1. {state['project_source']}\n"
        f"2. {state['policy_source']}"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": final_answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 8. BUILD THE LANGGRAPH WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

workflow = StateGraph(MultiHopState)

workflow.add_node(
    "identify_project",
    identify_project_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "retrieve_department",
    retrieve_department_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "retrieve_policy",
    retrieve_policy_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "generate_answer",
    generate_answer_node,
)


workflow.add_edge(
    START,
    "identify_project",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "identify_project",
    "retrieve_department",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "retrieve_department",
    "retrieve_policy",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "retrieve_policy",
    "generate_answer",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "generate_answer",
    END,
)


multi_hop_graph = workflow.compile()


# =========================================================
# 9. RUN THE MULTI-HOP WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

def run_multi_hop_rag(
    question: str,
) -> None:
    initial_state: MultiHopState = {
        "question": question,
        "project_name": "",

        "department": "",
        "project_source": "",

        "annual_limit": "",
        "approval": "",
        "policy_source": "",

        "hop_1_completed": False,
        "hop_2_completed": False,

        "final_answer": "",
    }

    result = multi_hop_graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 70)
    print("QUESTION")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["question"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("HOP 1: PROJECT OWNERSHIP")
    print("=" * 70)
    print("Project:", result["project_name"])
    print("Department:", result["department"])
    print("Source:", result["project_source"])
    print("Completed:", result["hop_1_completed"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("HOP 2: CERTIFICATION POLICY")
    print("=" * 70)
    print("Annual limit:", result["annual_limit"])
    print("Approval:", result["approval"])
    print("Source:", result["policy_source"])
    print("Completed:", result["hop_2_completed"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL ANSWER")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["final_answer"])


# =========================================================
# 10. PROGRAM ENTRY POINT
# =========================================================

if __name__ == "__main__":
    run_multi_hop_rag(
        "What certification reimbursement policy applies "
        "to the department that owns Project Orion?"
    )


g) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Multi-Hop RAG?

Multi-Hop RAG is a retrieval architecture that performs several connected retrieval steps before generating an answer. Evidence discovered in one hop may supply the entity, relationship, or restriction required to formulate the next search.

It is useful when the answer is distributed across multiple documents or data sources. The system stores intermediate results, evaluates their quality, continues retrieval when necessary, and synthesizes the final answer from the complete evidence chain.

Q2. How is Multi-Hop RAG different from standard RAG?

Standard RAG normally searches once using the original user question and generates from the retrieved context. It works well when the answer is directly available in one or more easily retrievable chunks.

Multi-Hop RAG performs multiple dependent searches. The result of an earlier hop can alter the next query, making it suitable for questions involving entity relationships, dependencies, comparisons, and distributed evidence.

Q3. What is the difference between Multi-Hop RAG and Query Decomposition?

Query Decomposition splits a complex question into smaller subquestions. It identifies the information needs but does not necessarily execute retrieval, manage dependencies, or combine evidence.

Multi-Hop RAG includes decomposition plus execution. It retrieves evidence for each subquestion, stores intermediate results, creates later queries from earlier evidence, checks sufficiency, and generates a grounded final response.

Q4. What are the main components of Multi-Hop RAG?

The main components are a query decomposer or planner, one or more retrievers, shared state, an intermediate evidence extractor, an evidence evaluator, a stopping controller, and a final answer synthesizer.

Advanced implementations may also include Query Rewriting, source routing, Re-Ranking, Fact Verification, confidence scoring, and Self-Correction for unsuccessful hops.

Q5. What is error propagation in Multi-Hop RAG?

Error propagation occurs when an incorrect result from one hop becomes input to the next hop. Later retrievers may perform correctly for the supplied query but still return irrelevant evidence because the intermediate entity or assumption was wrong.

The system should validate every important intermediate fact, preserve its source, track confidence, and stop or retry when evidence is weak. Final validation alone may not reveal where the original mistake occurred.

Q6. How do you evaluate Multi-Hop RAG?

Multi-Hop RAG should be evaluated using hop-level retrieval accuracy, supporting-evidence recall, intermediate fact accuracy, path correctness, final answer correctness, Faithfulness, Source Attribution, latency, and total retrieval operations.

Each step must be traced separately. A correct final answer reached through unsupported intermediate reasoning may not be reliable, while an incorrect answer may originate from only one failed hop.

Q7. When should Multi-Hop RAG be avoided?

It should be avoided for simple factual questions where one retrieval operation reliably provides the answer. Adding planning and several hops would increase latency, cost, and failure risk without creating meaningful value.

A query-complexity classifier can route direct questions to standard RAG and reserve Multi-Hop RAG for questions involving dependencies, comparisons, relationships, or evidence distributed across several sources. 

Topic 2: Graph RAG

a) Definition

* Graph RAG is an advanced Retrieval-Augmented Generation architecture that represents information as a graph containing nodes and relationships. Nodes may 
  represent documents, chunks, entities, concepts, events, or communities, while edges describe meaningful relationships among them.

* A traditional vector-based RAG system retrieves chunks primarily according to semantic similarity. Graph RAG additionally uses relationships and graph 
  structure, allowing the system to retrieve connected evidence that may not be semantically similar to the original question.

* Microsoft GraphRAG is a structured, hierarchical RAG approach that extracts a knowledge graph from raw text, detects communities, generates community 
  summaries, and uses these structures during question answering. It is designed to improve reasoning over complex and private datasets.

* Graph RAG is particularly useful for questions requiring global understanding, relationship analysis, thematic summarization, or evidence synthesis across   
  many documents. It can identify patterns and connections that may be missed when chunks are retrieved independently.

b) Purpose

* The primary purpose of Graph RAG is to help the system connect information distributed across separate documents and chunks. Two passages may have low 
  direct semantic similarity but still belong to the same answer path because they mention related people, projects, departments, policies, events, or 
  concepts.

* Graph RAG also supports global questions about an entire dataset, such as identifying major themes, organizational patterns, common risks, or 
  relationships across a document collection. Baseline vector RAG can perform poorly on these questions because no single chunk contains the complete high-
  level answer.

* It improves explainability by representing retrieval as an evidence path. Instead of returning only similar chunks, the system can show how one entity 
  connects to another through explicit relationships, such as Project → Department → Policy.

* Graph RAG is valuable for enterprise data containing connected information, but graph creation can be expensive. Entity extraction, relationship 
  extraction, community detection, summarization, embedding generation, and graph maintenance introduce more complexity than ordinary vector indexing.

c) Architectural Diagram

                     ENTERPRISE DOCUMENTS
                              |
                              v
                     Document Processing
                 Parse | Clean | Split into Chunks
                              |
                              v
                   Entity and Claim Extraction
          People | Projects | Teams | Policies | Events
                              |
                              v
                   Relationship Extraction
           owns | manages | follows | depends_on | affects
                              |
                              v
                      Build Knowledge Graph
                 Nodes + Edges + Source Metadata
                              |
                              v
                      Community Detection
             Group Densely Connected Entities and Topics
                              |
                              v
                    Community Summarization
               Generate Summary for Each Community
                              |
                +-------------+-------------+
                |                           |
                v                           v
           Graph Store                 Vector Store
       Nodes and Relationships    Chunks, Entities and Reports

d) Indexing Architecture

                        User Question
                              |
                              v
                        Query Analysis
               Entities | Intent | Local or Global
                              |
                 +------------+------------+
                 |                         |
                 v                         v
            Local Search              Global Search
       Entity and Neighbor          Community Reports
           Retrieval                and Theme Retrieval
                 |                         |
                 +------------+------------+
                              |
                              v
                     Build Graph Context
           Entities + Relationships + Text Evidence
                              |
                              v
                         LLM Generator
                              |
                              v
                   Grounded Answer with Sources


e) Components

i) Graph Nodes

Nodes represent the important objects extracted from the enterprise corpus. These may include people, departments, projects, policies, products, locations, events, topics, document chunks, and community summaries.

Every node should preserve metadata connecting it to the original source. Without provenance information, an answer may follow a valid graph relationship but fail to show which document established that relationship.

ii) Graph Edges

Edges represent relationships between nodes. They convert a collection of independent objects into connected knowledge and allow the retriever to traverse from an entity mentioned in the question to related evidence.

Edges may be directed or undirected and can contain properties such as confidence, source, timestamp, version, and relationship type. Relationship quality is critical because one incorrect edge can create a misleading retrieval path.

iii) Entity and Relationship Extractor

The extraction component identifies important entities and determines how they are related. It may use an LLM, named-entity recognition model, rules, structured records, or a combination of these methods.

Extraction should resolve aliases and duplicates. For example, Data Engineering, Data Eng., and DE Department may refer to the same entity and should not always become three unrelated nodes.

iv) Community Detection

Community detection identifies groups of densely connected nodes. A community may represent a project portfolio, business domain, research theme, departmental activity, or related set of policies.

Community structures enable hierarchical understanding. Instead of reading every individual node, the query engine can retrieve a community report that summarizes major entities, relationships, and themes within that part of the graph.

v) Community Reports

Community reports summarize the information contained within graph communities. They support global questions that require understanding the broader dataset rather than retrieving one exact entity relationship.

Microsoft GraphRAG generates community reports after graph extraction and community detection, and those reports can be embedded for retrieval during query processing.

vi) Graph Query Engine

The Graph Query Engine interprets the user’s question, finds matching entities or communities, traverses relevant edges, and gathers associated source text. It then converts the graph results into context suitable for an LLM.

The query engine may combine graph traversal with vector similarity. Vector search finds the starting nodes or text units, while graph traversal expands the evidence to connected entities and relationships.

f) Main Graph RAG Search Strategies

i) Local Search
Local Search answers questions about specific entities by retrieving the entity, its relationships, connected nodes, associated chunks, and relevant community information. It is suitable when the question contains a known person, project, organization, product, or policy.

ii) Global Search
Global Search answers broad questions about the entire document collection by using community summaries. It is useful for identifying major themes, risks, patterns, recurring issues, or overall trends.

iii) DRIFT Search

DRIFT-style retrieval combines broad community-level information with more focused local exploration. It begins with a high-level understanding and then follows relevant entities and relationships to collect detailed evidence.

This strategy is useful when the query requires both global context and particular supporting facts. It avoids relying exclusively on either community summaries or one local graph neighborhood.

iv) Hybrid Graph and Vector Search

Hybrid Graph RAG combines semantic vector search with graph traversal. Vector retrieval identifies semantically relevant starting chunks or entities, after which graph relationships expand the result to connected evidence.

This approach is practical because graph traversal alone requires the question to map correctly to entities, while vector search helps resolve paraphrases and natural-language variations.

f) 7. Graph RAG Indexing Process

Step 1: Load and Chunk Documents
Documents are loaded from text files, PDFs, enterprise repositories, databases, or other sources and divided into manageable units. Chunk boundaries should preserve enough context for accurate entity and relationship extraction.

Step 2: Extract Entities
The extraction model identifies entities such as people, projects, departments, policies, locations, events, and technical systems. Every entity retains references to the chunks in which it appeared.

Step 3: Extract Relationships and Claims
Relationships describe how entities interact, while claims represent factual statements about entities or relationships. Examples include ownership, dependency, approval, responsibility, and policy applicability.

Step 4: Build and Normalize the Graph
Extracted entities are deduplicated and aliases are resolved. Relationships are validated, and provenance metadata is attached so graph paths remain connected to the original document evidence.

Step 5: Detect Communities
A graph clustering algorithm groups strongly connected entities into communities. Communities may be organized hierarchically so broad topics contain smaller and more specific subcommunities.

Step 6: Generate Community Reports
An LLM creates summaries of entities, relationships, claims, and themes within each community. These summaries are stored and embedded for global search.

The Microsoft GraphRAG indexing architecture aligns these outputs to a shared knowledge model and provides customizable abstractions for language models, storage, caching, input readers, vector stores, and workflows.

g) Code Example

    question: str
    start_entity: str

    related_nodes: list[str]
    graph_relationships: list[dict[str, str]]
    sources: list[str]

    answer: str

# =========================================================

def identify_entity_node(
    state: GraphRAGState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Find the graph entity mentioned in the question.
    """

    question_lower = state["question"].lower()
    start_entity = ""

    for graph_node in enterprise_graph.nodes:
        if graph_node.lower() in question_lower:
            start_entity = graph_node
            break

    return {
        "start_entity": start_entity,
    }

            start_entity,

    relationships = []

        start_entity,
        *related_nodes,
    ]

I) Evaluation Metrics for Graph RAG

1. Entity Retrieval Accuracy
Entity Retrieval Accuracy measures whether the query engine correctly identifies the nodes required to answer a question. An incorrect starting entity can direct graph traversal into an unrelated subgraph.

                            Correctly retrieved entities
Entity Retrieval Accuracy = ----------------------------
                             Required reference entities

2. Relationship Accuracy
Relationship Accuracy measures whether retrieved edges correctly represent the relationships defined in the source material. It helps detect incorrect graph extraction and misleading paths.


                        Correct retrieved relationships
Relationship Accuracy = -------------------------------
                        Retrieved relationships evaluated

3. Path Accuracy
Path Accuracy determines whether the graph traversal follows the correct sequence of entities and relationships from the starting entity to the answer evidence.



J) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Graph RAG?

Graph RAG is an advanced RAG architecture that represents knowledge using nodes and relationships rather than relying only on independent text chunks. Nodes may represent entities, concepts, documents, events, or communities, while edges capture relationships among them.

During retrieval, the system identifies relevant nodes, traverses connected relationships, collects supporting text, and supplies the resulting graph-aware context to the LLM. This makes it useful for connected, multi-hop, and dataset-level questions.

Q2. How is Graph RAG different from traditional vector RAG?

Traditional vector RAG retrieves chunks according to semantic similarity between query and document embeddings. It works well when answer-bearing passages use language semantically related to the question.

Graph RAG additionally retrieves through entities and relationships. It can discover relevant evidence that has low direct similarity to the query but is connected through ownership, dependency, hierarchy, policy applicability, or another graph path.

Q3. What are Local Search and Global Search in Graph RAG?

Local Search focuses on specific entities and their neighborhoods. It retrieves connected entities, relationships, text units, and relevant community information, making it suitable for questions about a particular person, project, organization, or event.

Global Search uses community summaries to answer broad questions about themes or patterns across the entire corpus. It combines information from several communities rather than depending on one local entity neighborhood.

Q4. What is community detection in Graph RAG?

Community detection groups densely connected nodes into clusters representing related topics, activities, domains, or entity groups. Communities may be hierarchical, with larger communities containing several focused subcommunities.

An LLM can generate a summary report for each community. These reports allow the query engine to answer broad questions without passing every individual entity and document chunk to the generator.

Q5. What is the difference between Graph RAG and Multi-Hop RAG?

Multi-Hop RAG describes a retrieval process containing several dependent searches. It can use vector stores, databases, APIs, or graphs, and it does not require information to be represented in a graph beforehand.

Graph RAG describes an architecture in which graph structures are central to indexing and retrieval. It can support multi-hop traversal but also provides entity neighborhoods, community detection, hierarchical summaries, and global corpus-level search.

Q6. What is the difference between Graph RAG and Knowledge Graph RAG?

Graph RAG is a broad term for RAG systems that use graph structures. The graph may contain entities, document chunks, topics, communities, semantic relationships, and generated summaries.

Knowledge Graph RAG usually relies on a formal graph of domain entities and typed relationships, often managed in a graph database and governed by an ontology or schema. Microsoft GraphRAG automatically derives graph structures from unstructured text, while Knowledge Graph RAG may query an existing curated graph.

Q7. What are the major components of Graph RAG?

The major components are document processing, entity extraction, relationship extraction, graph construction, entity resolution, community detection, community summarization, graph storage, vector storage, graph-aware retrieval, context building, and answer generation.

A production implementation also needs source provenance, authorization, incremental updates, graph-quality evaluation, caching, observability, and groundedness validation.

Q8. When should Graph RAG be used?

Graph RAG should be considered when questions require connecting information across documents, navigating relationships, understanding organizational structures, discovering themes, performing multi-hop reasoning, or summarizing large collections globally.

It may not be appropriate for small collections of direct question-answer pairs or simple factual retrieval. Teams should confirm that relationship-aware retrieval produces measurable benefits before accepting the additional indexing and operational complexity.

Q9. What are the main challenges of Graph RAG?

The main challenges include expensive indexing, entity duplication, relationship extraction errors, graph updates, community-summary quality, retrieval latency, source-provenance preservation, and evaluation of graph paths.

An incorrect edge can be especially dangerous because it may produce a logically coherent path that is not supported by the original documents. Every important relationship should therefore retain source evidence and extraction confidence.

Q10. How would you evaluate Graph RAG?

Graph RAG should be evaluated using entity retrieval accuracy, relationship accuracy, path correctness, supporting-evidence recall, community coverage, final Answer Relevance, Faithfulness, Groundedness, and Source Attribution.

Evaluation should include both local entity questions and global dataset questions. It should also compare Graph RAG with a vector-RAG baseline to determine whether the added graph complexity produces measurable improvements.

Topic 3: Knowledge Graph RAG

a) Definition

Knowledge Graph RAG is a retrieval architecture that grounds LLM responses using entities, properties, and typed relationships stored in a knowledge graph. Instead of retrieving only semantically similar text chunks, it queries structured relationships such as ownership, dependency, approval, location, and policy applicability.

A knowledge graph represents domain knowledge through nodes and edges. Nodes usually represent real-world entities or concepts, while relationships describe how those entities are connected; both nodes and relationships can also store structured properties as key-value pairs.

Knowledge Graph RAG commonly converts the user’s natural-language question into a graph query, such as Cypher for Neo4j. The graph query retrieves relevant entities and relationships, and the resulting subgraph is converted into context for the LLM to generate a grounded answer.

The architecture can combine graph traversal with vector search. Vector retrieval finds semantically relevant documents or entities, while graph traversal expands those results through explicit relationships, supporting both unstructured and structured enterprise knowledge.

b) Purpose

The primary purpose of Knowledge Graph RAG is to answer questions involving relationships, dependencies, hierarchies, and multi-hop paths. These questions are difficult for standard vector RAG because the final answer may not appear directly inside one semantically similar text chunk.

It provides precise retrieval for structured questions. For example, a graph can identify which department owns a project, which policy applies to that department, and what approval that policy requires by traversing typed relationships.

Knowledge Graph RAG also improves explainability. A retrieved graph path can show how the system reached an answer, allowing users and developers to inspect the entities, relationships, properties, and source records involved in the conclusion.

It is most valuable when the enterprise already has structured relationships or when connected information provides measurable retrieval benefits. For direct document questions, ordinary vector RAG is usually simpler and requires less schema design, graph maintenance, and query validation.

c) Architecture Diagram

d) Core Components

i. Domain Ontology or Graph Schema

The ontology defines the kinds of entities, relationships, and properties allowed in the graph. For example, an enterprise graph may contain Project, Department, Employee, Policy, and Certification entity types.

It also defines valid relationships, such as OWNED_BY, MANAGES, FOLLOWS, REQUIRES, and DEPENDS_ON. A controlled schema improves query reliability and prevents the graph from becoming an inconsistent collection of unrelated nodes and edges.

Entity and Relationship Extractor

The extractor transforms raw text or structured records into graph objects. It identifies entities, their types, properties, and relationships, along with the source passage or record that established each fact.

LLM extraction is flexible but may invent relationships or classify entities incorrectly. Critical graph information should therefore be validated using schemas, source evidence, confidence thresholds, deterministic rules, or human-reviewed mappings.

ii. Entity Resolution

Entity Resolution determines whether two mentions refer to the same real-world object. Without resolution, an organization may appear under its complete name, abbreviation, old name, and spelling variations as several unrelated graph nodes.

Resolution may use exact identifiers, normalized names, aliases, embeddings, external master-data records, and human-curated mappings. Over-merging is also dangerous because two entities with similar names may represent different objects.

iii. Graph Database

The graph database stores entities, properties, typed relationships, and provenance. Neo4j is commonly used because it supports property graphs and the Cypher query language, allowing structured graph traversal and relationship filtering.

Knowledge graphs can store structured and unstructured data together. Text embeddings may be stored as node properties, which enables semantic vector search and structured graph traversal in the same retrieval architecture.

iv. Graph Query Planner

The query planner interprets a natural-language request and determines which graph pattern should be queried. It may create Cypher automatically, choose a predefined query template, or combine deterministic routing with LLM-based planning.

Generated graph queries must be validated before execution. Production systems should restrict allowed node labels, relationship types, properties, operations, result size, and execution time rather than giving an LLM unrestricted write access.

v. Hybrid Retriever

A Hybrid Retriever combines vector similarity with graph traversal. Semantic search can find an entity or document related to the user’s wording, after which the graph expands that starting point through connected nodes and relationships.

This approach is useful when users refer to entities indirectly or use language that does not exactly match graph labels. Neo4j and LangChain workflows can combine semantic vector queries and graph queries rather than forcing every question through only one retrieval method.

vi. Context Builder and Generator

The Context Builder converts graph records into a format the LLM can understand. It may serialize graph paths as natural-language statements, JSON objects, tables, triples, or labelled evidence blocks.

The generator receives the user question and graph-derived evidence, then creates a readable response. It should not add relationships or properties absent from the retrieved subgraph.


e) Types of Knowledge Graph RAG

i. Curated Knowledge Graph RAG

A curated graph is designed and maintained using an explicit domain schema. Entities and relationships usually come from authoritative databases, master-data systems, controlled vocabularies, or human-reviewed extraction.

This approach provides stronger precision and governance but requires domain expertise, schema management, data integration, and continuous maintenance.

ii. LLM-Generated Knowledge Graph RAG

An LLM-generated graph is automatically extracted from unstructured documents. The model identifies entities, relationships, claims, and properties that are then inserted into a graph store.

Automatic extraction allows rapid graph construction from large document collections, but it can create duplicate entities, missing relationships, unsupported edges, and inconsistent entity types.

iii. Hybrid Knowledge Graph RAG

A hybrid graph combines curated enterprise entities with relationships extracted from text. For example, official project and department IDs may come from a relational database, while document claims and policy references are extracted using an LLM.

This method balances structural reliability with coverage of unstructured knowledge. The curated entity layer can act as an anchor for resolving entities extracted from text.

iv. Temporal Knowledge Graph RAG
A temporal knowledge graph stores when facts and relationships became valid or stopped being valid. This is important when project ownership, employee roles, policy versions, and business relationships change over time.


e) Code Example

from typing import TypedDict
import networkx as nx
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


# =========================================================
# 1. CREATE A DIRECTED KNOWLEDGE GRAPH
# =========================================================

knowledge_graph = nx.DiGraph()


# =========================================================
# 2. ADD TYPED ENTITY NODES
# =========================================================

knowledge_graph.add_node(
    "Project Orion",
    entity_type="Project",
    source="project_registry_2026.txt",
)

knowledge_graph.add_node(
    "Data Engineering",
    entity_type="Department",
    source="organization_directory_2026.txt",
)

knowledge_graph.add_node(
    "Advanced Learning Policy",
    entity_type="Policy",
    annual_limit=25000,
    currency="INR",
    financial_period="per financial year",
    approval=(
        "Manager approval is required before registration."
    ),
    effective_year=2026,
    status="active",
    source="advanced_learning_policy_2026.txt",
)


# =========================================================
# 3. ADD TYPED RELATIONSHIPS WITH PROVENANCE
# =========================================================

knowledge_graph.add_edge(
    "Project Orion",
    "Data Engineering",
    relationship_type="OWNED_BY",
    source="project_registry_2026.txt",
)

knowledge_graph.add_edge(
    "Data Engineering",
    "Advanced Learning Policy",
    relationship_type="FOLLOWS",
    source="department_policy_mapping_2026.txt",
)


# =========================================================
# 4. DEFINE LANGGRAPH STATE
# =========================================================

class KnowledgeGraphRAGState(TypedDict):
    question: str

    project_name: str
    department_name: str
    policy_name: str

    annual_limit: int
    currency: str
    financial_period: str
    approval_rule: str

    relationship_path: list[str]
    sources: list[str]

    evidence_found: bool
    final_answer: str


# =========================================================
# 5. IDENTIFY THE PROJECT ENTITY
# =========================================================

def identify_entity_node(
    state: KnowledgeGraphRAGState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Find a Project entity mentioned in the user question.
    """

    question_lower = state["question"].lower()
    project_name = ""

    for node_name, attributes in (
        knowledge_graph.nodes(data=True)
    ):
        if (
            attributes.get("entity_type") == "Project"
            and node_name.lower() in question_lower
        ):
            project_name = node_name
            break

    return {
        "project_name": project_name,
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. QUERY THE KNOWLEDGE GRAPH
# =========================================================

def query_knowledge_graph_node(
    state: KnowledgeGraphRAGState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Traverse:

    Project --OWNED_BY--> Department --FOLLOWS--> Policy
    """

    project_name = state["project_name"]

    if not project_name:
        return {
            "evidence_found": False,
        }

    department_name = ""
    policy_name = ""
    relationship_path = []
    sources = set()

    project_attributes = knowledge_graph.nodes[
        project_name
    ]

    sources.add(
        project_attributes["source"]
    )

    # -----------------------------------------------------
    # First relationship:
    # Project --OWNED_BY--> Department
    # -----------------------------------------------------

    for target_node in knowledge_graph.successors(
        project_name
    ):
        edge_attributes = knowledge_graph.edges[
            project_name,
            target_node,
        ]

        target_attributes = knowledge_graph.nodes[
            target_node
        ]

        if (
            edge_attributes.get("relationship_type")
            == "OWNED_BY"
            and target_attributes.get("entity_type")
            == "Department"
        ):
            department_name = target_node

            relationship_path.append(
                f"{project_name} "
                f"--OWNED_BY--> "
                f"{department_name}"
            )

            sources.add(
                edge_attributes["source"]
            )

            sources.add(
                target_attributes["source"]
            )

            break

    if not department_name:
        return {
            "evidence_found": False,
        }

    # -----------------------------------------------------
    # Second relationship:
    # Department --FOLLOWS--> Policy
    # -----------------------------------------------------

    for target_node in knowledge_graph.successors(
        department_name
    ):
        edge_attributes = knowledge_graph.edges[
            department_name,
            target_node,
        ]

        target_attributes = knowledge_graph.nodes[
            target_node
        ]

        if (
            edge_attributes.get("relationship_type")
            == "FOLLOWS"
            and target_attributes.get("entity_type")
            == "Policy"
            and target_attributes.get("status")
            == "active"
        ):
            policy_name = target_node

            relationship_path.append(
                f"{department_name} "
                f"--FOLLOWS--> "
                f"{policy_name}"
            )

            sources.add(
                edge_attributes["source"]
            )

            sources.add(
                target_attributes["source"]
            )

            break

    if not policy_name:
        return {
            "department_name": department_name,
            "evidence_found": False,
        }

    policy_attributes = knowledge_graph.nodes[
        policy_name
    ]

    return {
        "department_name": department_name,
        "policy_name": policy_name,
        "annual_limit": policy_attributes[
            "annual_limit"
        ],
        "currency": policy_attributes[
            "currency"
        ],
        "financial_period": policy_attributes[
            "financial_period"
        ],
        "approval_rule": policy_attributes[
            "approval"
        ],
        "relationship_path": relationship_path,
        "sources": sorted(sources),
        "evidence_found": True,
    }


# =========================================================
# 7. GENERATE THE GROUNDED ANSWER
# =========================================================

def generate_answer_node(
    state: KnowledgeGraphRAGState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Convert the retrieved graph path and properties into
    a readable answer.
    """

    if not state["evidence_found"]:
        return {
            "final_answer": (
                "The knowledge graph does not contain enough "
                "verified information to answer the question."
            )
        }

    source_list = "\n".join(
        f"{index}. {source}"
        for index, source in enumerate(
            state["sources"],
            start=1,
        )
    )

    final_answer = (
        f"{state['project_name']} is owned by the "
        f"{state['department_name']} department. "
        f"This department follows the "
        f"{state['policy_name']}.\n\n"
        f"The policy allows certification reimbursement "
        f"up to {state['currency']} "
        f"{state['annual_limit']:,} "
        f"{state['financial_period']}. "
        f"{state['approval_rule']}\n\n"
        f"Sources:\n{source_list}"
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": final_answer,
    }


# =========================================================
# 8. BUILD THE LANGGRAPH WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

workflow = StateGraph(
    KnowledgeGraphRAGState
)

workflow.add_node(
    "identify_entity",
    identify_entity_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "query_knowledge_graph",
    query_knowledge_graph_node,
)

workflow.add_node(
    "generate_answer",
    generate_answer_node,
)


workflow.add_edge(
    START,
    "identify_entity",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "identify_entity",
    "query_knowledge_graph",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "query_knowledge_graph",
    "generate_answer",
)

workflow.add_edge(
    "generate_answer",
    END,
)


knowledge_graph_rag = workflow.compile()


# =========================================================
# 9. RUN THE KNOWLEDGE GRAPH RAG WORKFLOW
# =========================================================

def run_knowledge_graph_rag(
    question: str,
) -> None:
    initial_state: KnowledgeGraphRAGState = {
        "question": question,

        "project_name": "",
        "department_name": "",
        "policy_name": "",

        "annual_limit": 0,
        "currency": "",
        "financial_period": "",
        "approval_rule": "",

        "relationship_path": [],
        "sources": [],

        "evidence_found": False,
        "final_answer": "",
    }

    result = knowledge_graph_rag.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 70)
    print("QUESTION")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["question"])

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("IDENTIFIED ENTITY")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(
        result["project_name"]
        or "No project entity found"
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("GRAPH PATH")
    print("=" * 70)

    if result["relationship_path"]:
        for graph_step in result[
            "relationship_path"
        ]:
            print(graph_step)
    else:
        print("No verified graph path was found.")

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("RETRIEVED POLICY PROPERTIES")
    print("=" * 70)

    print(
        "Department:",
        result["department_name"],
    )

    print(
        "Policy:",
        result["policy_name"],
    )

    print(
        "Annual Limit:",
        result["annual_limit"],
    )

    print(
        "Currency:",
        result["currency"],
    )

    print(
        "Approval:",
        result["approval_rule"],
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL ANSWER")
    print("=" * 70)
    print(result["final_answer"])


# =========================================================
# 10. PROGRAM ENTRY POINT
# =========================================================

if __name__ == "__main__":
    run_knowledge_graph_rag(
        "What certification reimbursement policy applies "
        "to Project Orion?"
    )


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Knowledge Graph RAG?

Knowledge Graph RAG is an advanced RAG architecture that retrieves information from a graph of entities, typed relationships, and properties. It uses graph queries or traversal to obtain structured evidence and provides that evidence to an LLM for answer generation.

It is useful for questions involving relationships, hierarchies, dependencies, ownership, and multi-hop reasoning because these connections are explicitly represented in the graph.

Q2. How does Knowledge Graph RAG work?

The system first identifies entities and intent from the user’s question. It then creates a graph query, such as Cypher, or maps the request to a predefined graph traversal.

The graph database returns matching entities, relationships, properties, and provenance. This subgraph is converted into context, and the LLM generates an answer using only the retrieved graph evidence.

Q3. What is the difference between Graph RAG and Knowledge Graph RAG?

Graph RAG is a broad category covering any RAG architecture that uses graph structures. Its nodes may represent entities, chunks, documents, topics, communities, or semantic similarities.

Knowledge Graph RAG focuses specifically on domain entities, typed relationships, properties, and often an ontology or formal schema. Microsoft GraphRAG, for example, derives a graph and community reports from unstructured documents, whereas Knowledge Graph RAG may query an existing curated graph.

Q4. What is Text-to-Cypher?

Text-to-Cypher is the process of converting a natural-language question into a Cypher graph query. The generated query is executed against Neo4j, and the results are supplied to the LLM as grounding context.

The approach provides flexible natural-language access to graphs but requires schema awareness, query validation, read-only access, execution limits, and authorization controls.

Q5. Why combine vector search with a knowledge graph?

Vector search is effective for semantic matching and can find relevant content even when the user’s wording differs from graph labels. Graph traversal is effective for retrieving explicit relationships and connected evidence.

A hybrid system can first use vector search to identify starting entities or documents and then traverse the graph to retrieve dependencies, owners, policies, and related objects.

Q6. What is Entity Resolution?

Entity Resolution is the process of deciding whether different mentions refer to the same real-world entity. For example, Data Engineering, Data Eng, and DE Department may need to be merged into one canonical department node.

Poor resolution creates duplicate and disconnected nodes. Over-aggressive resolution can incorrectly merge separate entities, so identifiers, aliases, embeddings, rules, and human-reviewed mappings may all be required.

Q7. How do you prevent hallucinated graph relationships?

Every extracted relationship should retain its source passage or structured record. The system can validate edge types against the ontology, assign extraction confidence, require agreement across sources, and send low-confidence relationships for review.

The final answer should also be checked against the graph query result and original source evidence. The LLM should not generate relationships that were not returned by the graph retrieval step.

Q8. When should Knowledge Graph RAG be used?

It should be used when the application frequently receives questions involving relationships, dependencies, hierarchies, entity paths, or multi-source structured reasoning. It is also valuable when an enterprise already maintains a trusted knowledge graph.

It may be unnecessary when most questions involve direct lookup from individual passages. In such cases, vector RAG usually provides a simpler and more economical solution.

Q9. How is Knowledge Graph RAG evaluated?

Evaluation should include entity retrieval accuracy, relationship accuracy, graph-path correctness, property accuracy, supporting-source coverage, final-answer Faithfulness, Answer Relevance, and citation correctness.

The graph itself must also be evaluated for entity duplication, missing relationships, invalid relationships, ontology violations, stale properties, and provenance completeness.

Q10. What are the main production risks?

The main risks include incorrect entity resolution, hallucinated relationships, stale graph data, unauthorized graph access, unsafe generated queries, overly broad traversals, and missing provenance.

Production systems should use controlled schemas, read-only query accounts, query validation, traversal limits, source metadata, incremental updates, monitoring, and regression tests over known graph paths.

Topic 4: Multimodal RAG

a) Definition

Multimodal RAG is a Retrieval-Augmented Generation architecture that retrieves and combines information from more than one data modality. The knowledge base may contain text, images, diagrams, tables, audio, or video, and the final answer may depend on evidence from several of these formats.

In a text-only RAG system, visual content is often ignored or reduced to incomplete extracted text. Multimodal RAG preserves visual evidence and allows a multimodal model to examine an image together with related captions, surrounding text, metadata, and the user’s question.

The query itself can also be multimodal. The user may submit a text question, upload an image, or provide both. Similarly, retrieval can return text nodes, image nodes, or a combination, and response synthesis may use both textual and visual evidence.

LlamaIndex provides multimodal abstractions for models, indexing, retrieval, and query engines. Its multimodal indexing approach can maintain separate text and image vector stores while allowing the retriever to return both types of evidence


b) Purpose

The primary purpose of Multimodal RAG is to recover information that cannot be understood completely through extracted text alone. A chart may contain a trend, a diagram may describe a process, and a scanned form may communicate meaning through layout and spatial relationships.

Many enterprise documents combine paragraphs with tables, screenshots, diagrams, photographs, and charts. Text-only extraction can remove the connection between those elements, causing incomplete retrieval and increasing the risk of incorrect or unsupported answers.

Multimodal RAG improves document understanding by retrieving both text and native visual elements. Modern multimodal pipelines can index image chunks together with text chunks and supply both forms of evidence to a multimodal model during response synthesis.

It is suitable for technical manuals, presentations, financial reports, scanned contracts, engineering diagrams, research papers, product catalogues, and document-intelligence applications. However, it introduces additional parsing, storage, embedding, retrieval, model, evaluation, and compute requirements.

c) Architectural Diagram

i. Multimodal Indexing Architecture

                  ENTERPRISE DATA SOURCES
                  =======================

       PDFs | PowerPoints | Images | Audio | Video | Web
                              |
                              v
                    Multimodal Data Parser
                              |
          +-------------------+-------------------+
          |                   |                   |
          v                   v                   v
        Text               Images             Tables
          |                   |                   |
          v                   v                   v
     Text Chunks       Image Extraction     Structure Parsing
          |                   |                   |
          v                   v                   v
   Text Embeddings     Image Embeddings     Table Representation
          |                   |                   |
          +-------------------+-------------------+
                              |
                              v
                   Cross-Modal Metadata Links
          Document | Page | Caption | Section | Coordinates
                              |
                +-------------+-------------+
                |                           |
                v                           v
          Text Vector Store           Image Vector Store
                |                           |
                +-------------+-------------+
                              |
                              v
                     Multimodal Index

ii. Query-Time Architecture

	               User Query
                 Text | Image | Text + Image
                           |
                           v
                  Multimodal Query Encoder
                           |
              +------------+------------+
              |                         |
              v                         v
        Text Retrieval            Image Retrieval
              |                         |
              v                         v
      Relevant Text Nodes       Relevant Image Nodes
              |                         |
              +------------+------------+
                           |
                           v
                Cross-Modal Re-Ranking
                           |
                           v
                 Multimodal Context Builder
          Text + Images + Captions + Source Metadata
                           |
                           v
